# knots, lashings & sewn joints: Tied, lashed, spliced or sewn with cord or thread

[Back to the index](../report.md). Entries are numbered as in the index (research-priority order). ⚑ marks what needs confirming; corrections are welcome (`research/needs-confirming.md`).

## 326 Basic knots (overhand knot, half hitch, reef knot)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* The few knots most people half-know, the overhand, the half hitch, and the reef (square) knot with its slipped bow form for shoelaces, used as the baseline the other knot entries are compared with

*Also covers:* Baseline entry for the family. Also covers what knots do to rope strength (sources differ: record them side by side), how knots slip, capsize or jam, and why the reef knot must never join two ropes. Safety: knots are not for climbing, lifting or holding people without training and rated gear

### What it is

- **plain_name**: The simple knots most people half-know: a lump tied in the end of a string so it can't pull through a hole, a quick wrap of rope round a post, and the 'right over left, left over right' knot that ties a parcel or, made with loops, your shoelaces
- **term**: Basic knots: the overhand knot (stopper knot), the half hitch (hitch) and the reef or square knot (binding knot), with its slipped form, the shoelace bow
- **aliases**: 
  - overhand knot, thumb knot (ABOK #46; also #4, #514) (Wikipedia 'Overhand knot')
  - half knot: the same tuck tied with two ends round an object, the first half of a reef knot (ABOK #47) (Wikipedia 'Overhand knot'; Animated Knots 'Half Hitch')
  - half hitch, single hitch (ABOK #50) (Wikipedia 'Half hitch'; Animated Knots 'Half Hitch')
  - two half hitches, double half hitch (ABOK #1710); round turn and two half hitches (Wikipedia 'Two half-hitches'; Animated Knots)
  - reef knot, square knot, Hercules knot, double knot (ABOK #1204 among the binding knots; Animated Knots cites ABOK #1402, p. 258) (Wikipedia 'Reef knot'; Animated Knots 'Square Knot')
  - shoelace knot, bow knot, bow: a reef knot with both ends slipped (ABOK #1212) (Wikipedia 'Shoelace knot')
  - granny knot, false knot, lubber's knot (ABOK #1206): the common mistake when tying a reef knot (Wikipedia 'Granny knot')
  - thief knot, bag knot (ABOK #1207): a reef look-alike with the ends on opposite sides (Wikipedia 'Thief knot')
  - surgeon's knot, double throw: a reef knot with an extra twist in the first half knot (Wikipedia 'Reef knot'; Animated Knots 'Square Knot')
  - double overhand knot: a bigger stopper (Animated Knots 'Overhand Knot')
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: quick-release & tool-free
- **one_liner**: Three knots built from the same single tuck: the overhand makes a lump in the end of a cord so it can't slip through a hole, the half hitch ties a cord round a post or ring, and the reef knot ties the two ends of one cord round a bundle, with a slipped version that becomes the shoelace bow; they are the baseline that better knots for each job are compared with.
- **complexity**: Single piece: just the cord, string, rope or tape itself (plus the post, ring or bundle it is tied round).
- **how_it_works**: A knot holds by friction: where the cord bends round itself and crosses itself, a pull on the loaded part (standing part) squeezes the turns together so the free end can't slide out (Wikipedia 'Knot'); a simple analysis says a reef knot holds only if the cord's friction coefficient is above about 0.24 (Wikipedia 'Reef knot'). The overhand is a single tuck in one end that leaves a lump too big to pass through a hole (stopper); the half hitch is the same tuck made round a post and back round its own standing part; the half knot is the same move made with two ends round an object, and two half knots of opposite hand make a reef knot (Animated Knots 'Overhand Knot', 'Half Hitch'; Wikipedia 'Overhand knot'). Making the last tuck with a folded loop of cord (a bight) instead of the end gives a slipped knot that pulls undone with one tug on the free end, as in a shoelace bow (Wikipedia 'Knot', 'Shoelace knot').

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: ties a cord round a bundle, onto a post, rail or ring, or stops it slipping out through a hole
  - lock, and release by hand: all three untie by hand with no tools, and the slipped reef knot (shoelace bow) undoes with one pull
- **motion**: none: a correctly tied knot keeps the cord parts still relative to each other, and slipping counts as failure (Wikipedia 'Knot'); the cord on either side stays flexible.
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 at the knot itself; the cords either side can bend freely.
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - quick-release: slipped knots, such as the shoelace bow (a doubly slipped reef knot), come undone with one pull on the free end; the bow 'has the stability of the reef knot but is significantly easier to untie' (Wikipedia 'Shoelace knot')
  - none for the plain overhand and half hitch: they simply hold by friction (Wikipedia 'Knot')
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): turns of cord squeezed against each other and against the object (Wikipedia 'Knot'; 'Reef knot', Physical analysis)
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): a stopper knot is a 'fixed thicker point' that can't pass through a narrow hole (Wikipedia 'Stopper knot')
- **input_output**: not applicable
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable: all three untie by hand; the slipped forms are tool-free quick release. The overhand is 'very secure, to the point of jamming badly' and may have to be picked apart or cut once loaded (Wikipedia 'Overhand knot').
- **reuse_cycles**: effectively unlimited: the same cord can be tied and untied many times; a hard-jammed knot or a worn, chafed cord is the limit ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **tools_needed**: none. A pointed tool such as a fid can help loosen a jammed knot, and a knife or hot cutter is used to cut and seal synthetic cord (Wikipedia 'Knot', Tools).
- **seal**: none
- **load**: Light. A knot weakens the cord it is tied in, and knotted rope 'almost always fails at the knot or close to it' (Wikipedia 'Knot'). Sources give different figures, side by side: common knots keep 40-80% of the rope's strength (Wikipedia 'Knot'); 'a knot typically reduces the strength by 20-40%' (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'); assume even new rope gives 'no more than 50% of its rated breaking strength' because of knots, kinks and imperfections (Animated Knots 'Safety'); rope tests cited by Petrulis and Petrulyte range from 80-90% for an anchor bend down to 43-47% for a square (reef) knot, and in their own flax-yarn tests the square knot kept 37-49% and the overhand 56-74% of the breaking force (Fibres & Textiles in Eastern Europe, 2012); Wikipedia's overhand-knot infobox lists 50% (no source given). Direction matters: the reef knot is for binding round an object and spills if used to join two ropes; a single half hitch is 'insecure on its own' (Wikipedia 'Reef knot', 'Half hitch'). Never for holding people.
- **adjustable**: no: to change the tension or position, untie and re-tie; knots that can be slid to adjust tension are in Tensioning and adjustable hitches.
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable
- **access_needed**: not applicable: you need only a free end (or a bight) of the cord, and room to pass it round the post, ring or bundle.
- **materials**: Any cord, string, twine, shoelace, rope or flat tape, natural (cotton, hemp, sisal) or synthetic (nylon, polyester, polypropylene, high-modulus polyethylene such as Dyneema (brand)) (Wikipedia 'Rope'). Small cotton string has enough internal friction to stay tied easily, while stiff, thick kernmantle rope is hard to tie and may be slick enough to come apart once tied (Wikipedia 'Knot', Difficulty); synthetic ropes have a disadvantage in 'slipperiness' (Wikipedia 'Rope'). The reef knot doesn't hold if the two ends differ in size, stiffness or smoothness (ABOK quoted in Wikipedia 'Reef knot'). Flat webbing must be tied flat, without twists (Wikipedia 'Knot', Material).
- **maker_difficulty**: easy: no tools or parts; the real risks are tying a granny instead of a reef knot and using these knots for the wrong job.

### Making it

- **processes**: off-the-shelf part: cord, string or rope, tied by hand, 3D print (FDM): printed parts that a cord is knotted to or through (holes, slots, posts), and knot-practice boards (see make_it_yourself)
- **print_in_place**: no: knots are tied in cord; only the parts they are tied to, or practice boards, are printed.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Knots aren't printed, but cords are often knotted onto prints. 1) For a stopper behind a hole, make the hole only a little bigger than the cord so the overhand lump can't pull through; if it does, tie a double overhand, which makes a bigger stopper (Animated Knots 'Overhand Knot') ⚑ [uncertain for the hole sizing]. The overhand is 'one of the few stopper knots that can be tied tightly up against an object' (Animated Knots). 2) Round off the edges of holes, slots and posts where a loaded cord bends, so it doesn't chafe or cut through ⚑ [uncertain]. 3) Tie hitches round a solid post or bar on the part rather than a thin printed loop, which can snap along its layer lines ⚑ [uncertain]. 4) Use a printed knot-practice board to learn these and the better knots that replace them (Printables 619893). 5) Seal or whip the cut end of synthetic cord so it doesn't fray, but note the warning that hard melted ends can cut hands (Geoffrey Budworth, quoted in Wikipedia 'Rope'); see Rope-end finishing. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Cord, paracord, twine, shoelaces and rope, bought by fibre, diameter and breaking strength; cleats, rings, eyes and posts to tie to; cord locks, cord ends and buckles as no-knot alternatives. Choose rope by its breaking strength divided by a design factor of 5:1 to 12:1 (Cordage Institute guideline quoted by Animated Knots 'Safety').
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Reef knot: tie 'right over left; left over right' (or the reverse); both free ends must come out on the same side. Two half knots of the same hand make a weak granny knot, and ends on opposite sides make an even weaker thief knot (Wikipedia 'Reef knot'). In a correct reef knot each loop passes completely over, or completely under, the neck of the other (Wikipedia 'Granny knot').
  - Shoelace check: a reef-based bow sits straight, with the loops lying across the shoe; a bow lying along the shoe from heel to toe is a granny and comes loose more easily; fix it by reversing your starting knot (Ian's Shoelace Site 'Granny Knot'; Wikipedia 'Shoelace knot').
  - Never use a reef knot to join two ropes (as a bend): 'under no circumstances should it ever be tied as a bend' (ABOK, quoted in Wikipedia 'Reef knot'); Animated Knots quotes ABOK p. 258: 'There have probably been more lives lost as a result of using a Square Knot as a bend ... than from the failure of any other half dozen knots combined', and adds 'Never use it for critical loads'. Use a sheet bend or double fisherman's knot instead (Wikipedia 'Reef knot').
  - Half hitches: one is insecure; always add a second (two half hitches), and better still take a round turn round the post first (round turn and two half hitches), which is more secure and lets you hold the load while tying (Wikipedia 'Half hitch'; Animated Knots 'Half Hitch').
  - Knot strength, side by side: 40-80% of rope strength kept (Wikipedia 'Knot'); 20-40% lost (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'); no more than 50% of rated strength assumed (Animated Knots 'Safety'); square knot 43-47% in rope tests and 37-49% in flax yarn, overhand 56-74% in flax yarn (Petrulis and Petrulyte 2012).
  - Design factor: rope breaking strength 5 to 12 times the load (Cordage Institute CI 1401-15, quoted by Animated Knots 'Safety'), with 12:1 for live or critical loads; working load limits use up to 15:1 for critical uses (Wikipedia 'Knot').
  - Leave a long tail, and dress (neaten) and tighten the knot before loading, to stop it slipping when first loaded (Wikipedia 'Knot', Slipping).
  - For a binding that won't slip while you tie the second half, add an extra twist to the first half knot (surgeon's knot); two surgeon's half knots make a secure binding and, tied as a bow, a secure shoelace bow (Animated Knots 'Square Knot', 'Shoelace Bow').
  - Rigging angle: a rope that can lift a weight may fail when the same weight hangs from the middle of a near-horizontal rope, and a falling or jerked load can be many times its weight (Animated Knots 'Safety').

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Shoelaces tied in a bow, which is a reef knot with both ends slipped (Wikipedia 'Shoelace knot'; Animated Knots 'Shoelace Bow')
  - String round a parcel or gift, and the two twisted corners of a bin bag tied together (Wikipedia 'Reef knot'; Animated Knots 'Square Knot')
  - A knot in the end of a cord so it can't slip back out through a hole, or to stop a rope end unravelling (Wikipedia 'Overhand knot')
  - A lead rope or line tied to a ring, bar or pole with two half hitches (Animated Knots 'Two Half Hitches': 'Used to secure an animal or an item to a ring, bar or pole')
- **when_to_use**: Use these for light, non-critical jobs where nothing bad happens if the knot lets go, and where no tools or parts are wanted: the overhand as a quick stopper in a cord end or to stop a rope end unravelling (Wikipedia 'Overhand knot'); two half hitches, or better a round turn and two half hitches, to tie a line to a post, ring or rail (Animated Knots 'Half Hitch'); the reef knot to tie the two ends of one cord round a bundle, a bag neck, a sail cover or a parcel (Wikipedia 'Reef knot'; Animated Knots 'Square Knot'); and the slipped reef (bow) where you want one-pull release. They are handy for prototypes, tying cords onto printed parts and temporary bundling, and they are the starting point for learning better knots.
- **when_not_to_use**: Never use them for climbing, lifting, swings, hammocks, towing or anything that holds a person, without training and rated gear (Animated Knots 'Safety'). Never use the reef knot to join two ropes or cords, especially of different sizes: use a sheet bend or double fisherman's knot (Bends for joining two cords) (Wikipedia 'Reef knot'). Don't trust a single half hitch, or a reef knot that will be jiggled: the reef knot is 'insecure ... unstable when jiggled, and is not suitable for supporting weight' (Wikipedia 'Reef knot'). Instead: for a loop that won't slip, a bowline or figure-eight loop (Fixed loop knots); for a better stopper, a figure-eight or double overhand (Stopper knots and cord-end anchors); for tying to a post, a clove hitch or round turn and two half hitches (Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye); to tension a line, a trucker's or taut-line hitch (Tensioning and adjustable hitches); for a firm binding, the constrictor knot (Binding knots and seizings); for full strength, a splice, which 'is capable of attaining a rope's full strength' (Rope splices; Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'); for frequent quick fastening, a cord lock, cleat or buckle.
- **environment_limits**: Repeated pulling and slackening, flapping or vibration shake knots loose: 'Many knots can loosen with prolonged intermittent strain' (Animated Knots 'Safety'), and slipping is worse when a knot is 'repeatedly strained and let slack' or struck against hard objects (Wikipedia 'Knot'). Water: nylon 'absorbs water and is 10-15% weaker when wet', while polyester is not affected (Wikipedia 'Rope'). Sunlight: polypropylene has 'limited resistance to ultraviolet light, friction, and heat', and some synthetics are damaged by UV (Wikipedia 'Rope'). Rot: natural-fibre cords rot more readily than synthetics (Wikipedia 'Rope'). Heat: synthetic cords melt, which is how their ends are sealed (Wikipedia 'Rope').
- **safety_notes**: Knots are not for climbing, lifting or holding people without training and rated gear, and a home-made knot, splice or seam must never hold a person: 'Wrongly handled, gripped, or tied, rope can kill, maim, or burn' (Animated Knots 'Safety'). The reef knot used to join two ropes has caused many deaths; Ashley wrote that more lives were lost that way than through 'any other half dozen knots combined' (ABOK p. 258 via Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Reef knot'). Never stand in a loop or bight of rope that may suddenly tighten, and don't wrap rope round your hand or fingers for grip (Animated Knots 'Safety'). Don't try to hold a heavily loaded rope in bare hands; take two or more turns round a post, winch or cleat (Animated Knots 'Safety'). Melted synthetic rope ends can be hard and sharp enough to cut a hand (Budworth, quoted in Wikipedia 'Rope').
- **common_failures**: Slipping: the load drags the cord back through the knot until the end pulls out, worse under repeated loading and slackening; prevent it by leaving a long tail, dressing and tightening the knot, adding half hitches or a stopper, or choosing a more secure knot (Wikipedia 'Knot'). Capsizing (spilling): pulling one end of a reef knot outward turns it into a loose hitch that slides off, which is why it fails as a bend (Wikipedia 'Knot', 'Reef knot'; Animated Knots shows even a stack of square knots capsizing). Jamming: the overhand jams 'badly' under load and may need cutting (Wikipedia 'Overhand knot'). Wrong knot: a granny or thief knot instead of a reef; the granny 'can slip when heavily loaded' and also jams, and the thief 'unties itself' when pulled (Wikipedia 'Granny knot', 'Thief knot'). Breaking at the knot well below the rope's rated strength (Wikipedia 'Knot'). Shoelaces coming undone because the bow is really a granny (Ian's Shoelace Site 'Granny Knot'; Wikipedia 'Shoelace knot'). A reef knot slipping because its two ends differ in thickness or stiffness (ABOK via Wikipedia 'Reef knot').
- **related**: 
  - Stopper knots and cord-end anchors: the figure-eight, double overhand and Ashley stopper are better stoppers than the plain overhand
  - Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye: the clove hitch and round turn and two half hitches build on the half hitch
  - Bends for joining two cords: the sheet bend, double fisherman's knot and water knot do the job the reef knot must never do
  - Fixed loop knots: the bowline, figure-eight loop and alpine butterfly make loops that don't tighten or slip
  - Tensioning and adjustable hitches: the trucker's hitch, taut-line hitch and Prusik tighten and hold a line
  - Binding knots and seizings: the constrictor knot binds far more securely than the reef knot
  - Rope splices: interwoven strands that keep nearly all of the rope's strength
  - Rope-end finishing: whipping and heat-sealing stop fraying better than an overhand in the end
  - Pole lashings: square, diagonal and tripod lashings, started and finished with hitches
  - Cord lock (spring toggle), Cam cleat and Clam cleat: hardware that grips a cord without any knot

## 333 Sew-on part (sewing holes, loops and slots on a rigid part)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Small holes, loops, slots or a thin flange designed into a rigid part, often a 3D print, so it can be stitched to fabric, straps or a costume with needle and thread, like a sew-on button or badge

*Also covers:* Also covers sewn-on snaps and hooks, holes through a thickened edge, hot-needle piercing, and the alternatives makers compare it with (hot glue, contact adhesive, hook-and-loop).

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A rigid part, often 3D printed, with small holes, loops, a slot or a thin edge built in so you can stitch it onto cloth, a strap or a costume with a needle and thread, the same way you sew on a button or a badge
- **term**: Sew-on part with sew-through holes (sew tabs, sewing flange)
- **aliases**: 
  - sewing holes / sew holes / sew-through holes (the button term)
  - sew tabs (Adafruit's name for printed tabs with sewing holes)
  - sewable part, sew-on mount, sew-on patch, sew-on badge or label
  - sewing flange (a thin edge, often flexible filament, that you stitch straight through)
  - shank or sewing loop (a loop on the back, as on a shank button, so stitches are hidden)
  - sew-on snaps and sew-on hooks and eyes (hardware sold with holes for sewing)
  - findings: a jump ring or jewellery bail glued to a print, then sewn on (cosplay term)
  - thread shank (a short stalk of wound thread that lifts a button above thick fabric)
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: print-in-place & 3D-printing joints, cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware
- **one_liner**: Holes, loops, slots or a thin flange designed into a rigid part let you sew it to fabric with needle and thread, so the thread loops lock the part on by shape, like a sew-on button, and it can be cut off and re-sewn later.
- **complexity**: Single piece plus thread: one rigid part with sewing features (a ring of holes near the edge, loops or a shank on the back, a slot for a strap, or a thin flange), sewn with thread to the fabric. It becomes a few parts when a ready-made sew-on finding (a snap half, a hook, a jump ring or a jewellery bail) is glued to the part first and then sewn on (Dangerous Ladies). Optional extras: a reinforcing patch or fusible interfacing behind the fabric (Dangerous Ladies), pins to hold it in place while sewing (Adafruit).
- **how_it_works**: Each stitch is a small loop of thread that passes through a hole in the part, through the fabric, and back, so the part is held by shape (form-fit): it can only come off if the thread breaks, the fabric tears or the thin bridge of plastic beside the hole snaps. Several holes spread round the edge share the pull and keep the part flat, and the thread ends are knotted on the inside (Adafruit's hat badges are stitched with a needle on each end of the thread and 'Tie off at each tab'). Variants: holes through the face like a sew-through button; loops or a shank on the back so stitches don't show; small holes through a thickened edge that turn a corner (one hole in the side edge meeting one in the back at 90 degrees) so the thread is hidden from the front (r/3Dprinting thread); a thin flange of flexible filament stitched straight through (Printables 'Sewable Flexible Coin Cell Holder with Flange'; 'Alti 2 N3 ... TPU Sew on Mount'); a slot or slit that a strap or elastic passes through, the strap then being sewn back on itself (Printables 'Ghost ... Mask (with Sewing Holes)'); and sew-on snaps or hooks whose other half is glued to the part (Dangerous Ladies).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: join and hold: fixes a rigid part (badge, label, armour piece, magnet case, tracker holder, mask) to fabric, a strap or a costume
- **motion**: none: once sewn the part stays put on the fabric, although the fabric around it still bends and a part sewn at only one point can swivel
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 when sewn down tightly through several holes. A part held at a single point (one central hole, one loop or one ring) can still turn or swing: Dangerous Ladies notes that one pin through one hole in the fabric 'will allow your piece to spin 360 degrees freely'.
- **special_behaviour**: none: it is a plain fixed attachment; the clever part is only in where the holes go (hidden on the back, or turning a corner through a thick edge)
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): thread loops pass through holes in the part and through the fabric, so the part cannot lift off without breaking thread, plastic or fabric
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): tight stitches also press the part against the fabric so it doesn't rattle or slide (a minor effect)
  - material bond (glue, solvent or melting): only in the variant where a metal finding is glued to the part before sewing, or where glue is added under a sewn patch as a helper (Dangerous Ladies; Instructables '3D Printed Patch')
- **input_output**: not applicable: it is a fixed joint that does not pass motion on
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: semi-permanent (can be undone but may damage parts): snip the threads 'with scissors or a seam ripper' to move or replace the part (Adafruit); the part can be re-sewn but the thread is not reused, and needle holes stay in the fabric. Sewing through printed 'fabric' leaves 'permanent and visible damages such as holes' (Li and Lee 2022). Sew-on snaps and hooks make the part itself removable without cutting, but leave the other half on the costume (Dangerous Ladies).
- **reuse_cycles**: a few times: each re-sew needs new thread and makes new needle holes in the fabric (Adafruit; Li and Lee 2022); with sew-on snaps the part itself can be taken on and off many times (Dangerous Ladies)
- **tools_needed**: A hand-sewing needle (Adafruit uses two, one on each end of the thread), thread, scissors and pins to hold the position. A small drill bit in a rotary tool if printed holes come out too small for the needle (Adafruit). Without designed holes: a drill, or a needle heated in a flame or a soldering iron to melt holes through the plastic (r/3Dprinting thread; Printables 'Alti 2 N3 ... TPU Sew on Mount'; Edinburgh College of Art student project). A heavy-duty sewing machine helps when sewing straight through TPU (Printables 'Alti 2 N3 ... TPU Sew on Mount'). Glue for findings: superglue, or two-part epoxy for heavy pieces (Dangerous Ladies).
- **seal**: none: nothing makes it seal
- **load**: Light. Fine for badges, labels, patches, cosplay armour pieces, magnet cases, small trackers and pouches. No source opened gives a strength figure; it is set by the thread, the fabric and the thin bridge of plastic beside each hole ⚑ [uncertain]. It is strongest when the pull runs along the fabric and is shared by many stitches (shear), and weakest when one edge is peeled up or a heavy piece hangs from a single loop. Back woven fabric with a non-stretch patch or interfacing when 'expecting to support the weight of larger resin or print pieces' (Dangerous Ladies). Never use a sewn-on part or a home-sewn joint to hold a person (harness, climbing, lifting or tow gear). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **adjustable**: no: to move the part you cut the threads and sew it again (Adafruit)
- **play_and_precision**: Not a precision joint. Small play: thread stretches and the fabric gives, so a part sewn at one or two points can rock or turn; holes spread around the edge hold it flatter and steadier. Pin it in place while sewing, because 'for a flush fit, it helps to keep the pins in place until the knot is tied at the inside' (Adafruit). On a curved surface such as a hat, a rigid flat print only touches in places; Adafruit softens PLA in just-off-the-boil water to curve it, but 'don't expect to get more than a general curve'.
- **access_needed**: both sides: the needle has to come out of the back of the fabric and go back again, and knots are tied on the inside (Adafruit). Holes through a thickened edge that turn a corner keep the thread hidden from the front (r/3Dprinting thread), but you still need to reach the back of the fabric.
- **materials**: The part: PLA, PETG and TPU (flexible) prints, resin prints, wood, leather or metal. Flexible TPU can be sewn straight through, so a thin TPU flange needs no holes (Printables 'Sewable Flexible Coin Cell Holder with Flange'; 'Alti 2 N3 ... TPU Sew on Mount'); Adafruit prefers flexible filament for hat badges and uses PLA only with extra steps. For anything that will be machine-washed use PETG 'or other filaments that can withstand 60°C' (Printables 'Cloth face mask accessories (sew on)'), because PLA 'gets soft and deforms at temperatures over 60 °C' (Prusa). The fabric: woven and knit fabrics, felt, leather, vinyl and webbing; stretchy or loosely woven fabric should be stabilised with fusible interfacing or a non-stretch patch (Dangerous Ladies). Thread: ordinary sewing thread matched to the filament colour (Adafruit); one maker suggests nylon thread through hidden edge holes (r/3Dprinting thread). Findings for resin and printed parts: metal or plastic snaps, hooks, jump rings, bails and pin backs (Dangerous Ladies).
- **maker_difficulty**: easy: add a row of holes in CAD (or drill or hot-needle them afterwards) and sew by hand; basic hand-sewing is the only skill needed

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - 3D print (FDM): the usual case; holes, loops, slots and thin flanges print with the part, and TPU flanges can be sewn straight through (Printables models listed below)
  - 3D print (resin): print the part, then glue on a metal jump ring, spade bail, snap half or hook and sew that to the fabric (Dangerous Ladies)
  - woodwork: drill small holes in a wooden badge or toggle ⚑ [uncertain: no woodwork source opened]
  - laser cut: cut sewing holes in acrylic, wood or leather shapes ⚑ [uncertain: no laser-cutting source opened]
  - off-the-shelf part: sew-on snaps, hooks and eyes, sew-through buttons and shank buttons are made for sewing (Wikipedia 'Snap fastener', 'Hook-and-eye closure', 'Button') *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **print_in_place**: no: the part prints in one piece with its holes, but it is joined to the fabric afterwards by sewing (printing the plastic straight onto an open mesh during a pause is the separate entry 'Printing onto fabric')
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Hole size: makers' sewable models nearly all use about 2 mm holes (Printables 'Sewable brand labels' 2 mm holes centred 3 mm from the edge on a 1.2 mm thick label; 'Sew-on Loop' 2 mm holes; 'Sew on Velcro' 2 mm holes; Instructables '3D Printed Patch' 2 mm holes in a 1 mm backing). Ordinary hand-sewing needles are much thinner: John James Sharps run from 1.02 mm (size 1) down to 0.41 mm (size 12) in diameter, so a 2 mm hole leaves room for the needle and a doubled thread. Small holes often print undersized or partly closed, so make them a little bigger than you think or drill them out afterwards ('You may also have to drill out the sew holes with a small bit and rotary tool', Adafruit; 'Don't make the thread holes too small or else they won't print well', Printables 'Parametric Button Generator'). 2) Edge distance: leave a solid bridge of plastic between hole and edge. With a 0.4 mm nozzle one perimeter is about 0.45 mm and two about 0.9 mm, and walls thinner than one perimeter don't print (Prusa), so keep at least about 1 mm of plastic outside each hole; the label example above leaves 2 mm. 3) Orientation: print sew tabs and flanges lying flat with the holes vertical, so the pull of the thread runs along the layers; FFF prints are 'stronger in one direction than another' (Prusa). Holes through a thickened side edge (printed sideways) put the bridge across layer lines and are weaker ⚑ [uncertain: inferred from Prusa's general advice]. 4) Smooth edges: sharp hole edges can fray or cut thread; chamfer or deburr hole mouths, and prefer chamfers to fillets on edges facing the bed, which print better (Prusa) ⚑ [uncertain: no source tests thread wear on printed holes]. 5) Hidden stitches: give the part some thickness and put small holes in the side edge meeting holes in the back at 90 degrees, so the thread turns the corner and is invisible from the front (r/3Dprinting thread); or add loops or a ridge with holes on the back (same thread). 6) Slots for straps and elastic: slits print poorly without support and are fragile; one mask needed organic supports for its 2 cm elastic slits, and the maker advises sizing the elastic so it doesn't put 'a lot of stress' on them (Printables 'Ghost ... Mask (with Sewing Holes)'). Sew the strap back on itself with a strong stitch pattern such as a box-X (Ripstop by the Roll). 7) Flexible filament: TPU parts can be sewn through directly, by machine or by hand; if the needle struggles, melt holes with a heated needle (Printables 'Alti 2 N3 ... TPU Sew on Mount'). Adafruit's hat badges use flexible filament (SemiFlex preferred; its NinjaFlex settings are 230 °C and 30 mm/s); with PLA they dip the part in just-off-the-boil water for a few seconds to curve it to the hat. 8) Washing: for washable parts print in PETG or another filament that withstands 60 °C (Printables 'Cloth face mask accessories (sew on)'); the patch Instructable recommends cold hand washing because 'machine wash may be too rough'. 9) Typical failures: holes too small or bridged over, a thin bridge snapping at a layer line, thread cut by a sharp edge, and a stiff flat part peeling away from curved or stretchy fabric. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search for: 'sew-on snap fastener' or 'sew-on press stud' (a stud and socket with holes round the rim; Wikipedia 'Snap fastener' says snaps can be attached by riveting, sewing or snap pliers); 'hook and eye' fasteners (Wikipedia 'Hook-and-eye closure'); 'sew-through button' and 'shank button' (Wikipedia 'Button'); jewellery 'jump ring', 'spade bail' or 'glue-on bail', and 'pin back' or 'bar pin' (Dangerous Ladies); 'sew-on hook and loop tape' (sewing-machine-friendly Velcro (brand)); 'fusible interfacing' for reinforcing fabric (Dangerous Ladies); hand-sewing needles such as Sharps, and heavier Leather/Glovers needles for leather (John James Needle Guide); strong polyester or nylon sewing thread ⚑ [uncertain: no thread maker's page opened]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Sewing holes about 2 mm in diameter: used by Printables 'Sewable brand labels' (2 mm holes, centres 3 mm from the edge, 1.2 mm thick), 'Sew-on Loop' (2 mm holes), 'Sew on Velcro' (2 mm holes in a 3 mm plate) and the Instructables '3D Printed Patch' (2 mm holes, 1 mm backing).
  - Needles are thinner than you might think: John James Sharps are 1.02 mm (size 1) to 0.41 mm (size 12) in diameter, Tapestry needles up to 2.34 mm (John James Needle Guide), so a 2 mm hole suits ordinary sharps with doubled thread.
  - Keep at least two perimeters (about 0.9 mm with a 0.4 mm nozzle) of plastic between a hole and the edge; walls thinner than one perimeter (about 0.45 mm) won't print (Prusa, 'Modeling with 3D printing in mind').
  - Spread the load: use several holes round the edge rather than one, and back woven fabric with a non-stretch patch or fusible interfacing for heavy pieces (Dangerous Ladies).
  - When stitching through thin or open features, change direction rather than sewing in one straight line, which kept the thread tauter in an Edinburgh College of Art student test; thick wool 'was too strong and broke the delicate strips' of a printed lattice, while thin thread worked best (Edinburgh College of Art, 'Sewing through Glitches', 2019).
  - For a flat button on heavy fabric, wind a thread shank to raise the button above the fabric (Wikipedia 'Button').
  - Washable parts: PETG or other filament that withstands 60 °C (Printables 'Cloth face mask accessories (sew on)'); PLA softens above 60 °C (Prusa).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A sew-through button with two or four holes, or a shank button with a loop on the back (Wikipedia 'Button')
  - Sew-on snaps and hooks and eyes on garments and bras (Wikipedia 'Snap fastener', 'Hook-and-eye closure')
  - Sew-on name labels, brand labels and badges (Printables 'Sewable brand labels', 'Sew-on patches')
  - A small sewable battery holder or LED board on a costume or bag, stitched through holes or a flange (Printables 'Sewable Flexible Coin Cell Holder with Flange')
- **when_to_use**: When a rigid part has to stay on cloth, webbing or a costume through bending, wear and washing, and glue would peel, stiffen the fabric or soak through. Good for printed badges and patches (Adafruit; Instructables), cosplay armour pieces and masks (the mask maker added sewing holes because 'the cloth was a pain to position', Printables 'Ghost ... Mask'), magnet cases sewn into curtains (Printables 'Case for 10x2mm magnet with sewing holes'), tracker and altimeter holders on bags, straps or suits (Printables 'Air Tag Holder - sew-on'; 'Alti 2 N3 ... TPU Sew on Mount'), and loops for keys inside a bag (Printables 'Sew-on Loop'). In one r/3Dprinting thread the options were ranked from 'Most janky' (hot glue) through 'Mid janky' (drill, hot-needle pierce or design holes and hand sew) to 'Least janky' (adhesive-backed hook-and-loop on the print with sew-on hook-and-loop on the cloth). Sewing is also one of the joints that suits 3D-printed clothing panels (Li and Lee 2022).
- **when_not_to_use**: Not for anything that must hold a person or a heavy, safety-critical load: use rated webbing hardware sewn by a qualified maker. Not where the part must come off often for washing or swapping: use sew-on snaps, hooks or pin backs (Dangerous Ladies) or hook-and-loop (r/3Dprinting thread). Not where there is no room for holes or the piece is tiny and light: glue it with a flexible glue (Dangerous Ladies recommends flexible superglue, or a vinyl patch as an intermediate layer). Not on very delicate printed lattices or printed 'fabrics', where sewing leaves permanent visible holes (Li and Lee 2022); printed snap buttons were quicker to assemble and reusable, though less comfortable (Li and Lee 2022). Alternatives makers compare it with: hot glue (quick but 'most janky'), contact adhesive ('works quite well'), spray adhesive, E6000 (brand) glue or diluted PVA brushed on (all r/3Dprinting thread), and printing the part straight onto an open mesh during a pause (see 'Printing onto fabric').
- **environment_limits**: Heat: PLA parts soften and deform above about 60 °C (Prusa), so avoid hot washes, tumble dryers and hot cars; PETG is fine below about 80 °C (Prusa), and makers recommend filament that withstands 60 °C for machine-washed parts (Printables 'Cloth face mask accessories (sew on)'). Washing: the patch Instructable recommends cold hand washing because a machine wash 'may be too rough'. Sunlight: PLA 'degrades under UV light' (Prusa). Flex and vibration: a stiff flat part on stretchy fabric works the stitches loose or tears the fabric around them over time; stabilise the fabric with interfacing (Dangerous Ladies). Thread can rot or fade outdoors depending on fibre ⚑ [uncertain: no thread source opened]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **safety_notes**: Never trust a sewn-on part or home-sewn joint to hold a person (climbing, harness, lifting or tow gear). Needles and pins: prick hazard, and keep small parts and loose needles from children. Melting holes with a heated needle or a soldering iron risks burns, and melting plastic gives off fumes: emissions from melted filament 'can include a large number of ultrafine particles and volatile organic compounds' (Wikipedia, 'Health and safety hazards of 3D printing'), so work with ventilation. When gluing findings, superglue and epoxy can leak through fabric and glue layers together (Dangerous Ladies).
- **common_failures**: Holes print too small or bridged shut: enlarge them in CAD or drill them out (Adafruit). The bridge between a hole and the edge snaps: move holes further in, thicken the part, print it flat (Prusa). Thread wears through on a sharp hole edge: chamfer or deburr the holes ⚑ [uncertain]. Thin slits for elastic break: add supports when printing and don't overtighten the elastic (Printables 'Ghost ... Mask'). The fabric tears or stretches round the stitches under a heavy piece: back it with a non-stretch patch or interfacing (Dangerous Ladies). The part rocks or spins: add more holes spread round the edge (Dangerous Ladies on single-hole spinning). The part warps or softens in a hot wash: print in PETG or a filament that withstands 60 °C (Printables 'Cloth face mask accessories'). Glue alternatives fail by peeling: one of two fabric glues tested by Li and Lee let the joined panels come apart after two days. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **related**: 
  - Printing onto fabric (fabric embedded in a 3D print): the plastic is printed through an open mesh during a pause, so no sewing is needed, but only flat panels the size of the print bed can be made
  - Snap fastener (press stud): sew-on versions are sewn through holes in their rim; makes the part removable without cutting thread (Dangerous Ladies)
  - Hook-and-loop fastener: sew the soft side to the cloth and stick or glue the other to the print, for parts that come off for washing (r/3Dprinting thread)
  - Basic strap and garment closures (button, toggle and loop): the sew-through button is the classic sew-on part; that entry covers closing an opening
  - Load-bearing stitch patterns for straps and webbing (bar tack, box-X): how to sew a strap that passes through a slot in a part
  - Sewn seams and hand stitches: the stitches themselves
  - Eyelets and grommets: reinforced holes in the fabric rather than in the part
  - Hot-melt adhesive (glue gun), contact adhesive and elastomeric flexible craft glue: the glue-on alternatives; faster, but they can peel, stiffen or soak through fabric
  - Lacing (cord through rows of holes or eyelets): cord through holes in both pieces, adjustable and removable
  - Leather and textile rivets: a permanent metal fixing through part and fabric instead of thread

## 334 Stopper knots and cord-end anchors

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Ways to stop the end of a cord, string, elastic or thin cable pulling out through a hole in a part, with a bulky knot behind the hole or in a pocket, a crimped stop, a clamp under a screw head, or turns round a post

*Also covers:* Also covers the overhand, figure-eight and larger stopper knots, counterbored knot pockets, washers behind the hole, and clamping string under a bolt in robots.

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A fat knot, a squashed metal sleeve, a screw that pinches the string, or a few turns round a peg, used to stop the end of a cord, string, elastic or thin wire from pulling back out through a hole in a part
- **term**: Stopper knot and cord-end anchor (cord termination)
- **aliases**: 
  - stopper, end knot or knob knot (a knot that makes a lump in the cord)
  - overhand knot (ABOK #46; Wikipedia also lists #4, #514, #515, #519)
  - figure-eight knot, figure-of-eight or Flemish knot (ABOK #570; Wikipedia also lists #420, #520)
  - double overhand knot or barrel knot (ABOK #516)
  - stevedore knot (ABOK #456, #522)
  - Ashley stopper knot or oysterman's stopper (ABOK #526)
  - EStar stopper (for very slippery rope such as Dyneema)
  - knot pocket, counterbored knot recess or cord end cap (a printed recess or cap that hides the knot)
  - crimp bead, crimp tube, stop sleeve or ferrule (a metal sleeve squashed onto the cord)
  - string clamp under a bolt or washer (gm0: 'clamping a string with a bolt')
  - tie-off post or anchor peg (turns round a post, then tied off)
  - underwriter's knot or electrician's knot (the stopper used inside lamp plugs; ABOK #237, #775)
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: print-in-place & 3D-printing joints, cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware, clamping, magnetic & friction connections
- **one_liner**: A knot, bead or crimped sleeve that is too big to fit through a hole, or a clamp or a few turns round a post that grips the cord, holds the end of a cord, string, elastic or thin cable in a part so it can be pulled without coming out.
- **complexity**: Single piece or a few parts. Always: the cord and a hole, slot or post in the part. Optional: a washer or bead behind the knot to spread the load (gm0), a printed pocket or end cap to hide the knot (Printables 'Cord End Caps'), a metal crimp sleeve squashed with a crimping tool (Beadaholique; Nicopress), or a screw, washer and nut or a printed clamp plate that pinches the cord (gm0; Printables 'Paracord thimble and rope clamp').
- **how_it_works**: A stopper knot makes 'a fixed thicker point on an otherwise-uniform thickness rope' so that it cannot slip 'through a narrow passage, such as a hole' (Wikipedia 'Stopper knot'): pull on the cord and the knot jams against the back of the hole, and the plastic round the hole takes the load (shape, or form-fit). Bigger stoppers cover more of the hole's edge and hold in bigger holes; Ashley's stopper gives 'greater resistance to pulling through an opening than other common stoppers' (Wikipedia). A washer or bead behind the knot spreads the load over more plastic (gm0). A crimped sleeve does the same job for wire, fishing line or beading wire: the sleeve is squashed flat onto the cord so it grips by friction and makes a lump. A screw clamp presses the cord against the part, so it holds by friction and can be loosened to re-tension (gm0). Turns round a post use friction that grows exponentially with the angle of wrap (Wikipedia 'Capstan equation'), so the knot that finishes them off only carries a small part of the pull.

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: anchors the end of a cord, string, elastic or thin cable to a part
  - tighten and hold tension: the clamp-under-a-bolt and turns-round-a-post versions let you pull the cord tight and lock it there; clamping 'makes it much easier to quickly change the tension of a string' (gm0)
- **motion**: none: the cord end is fixed, though the cord can still swing or twist where it leaves the hole
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 along the pull (the knot or clamp stops the cord moving out); a knotted cord can still turn about its own axis and swing at the hole, which is harmless for most uses.
- **special_behaviour**: none: a stopper simply cannot fit through the hole; the clamp version can be loosened to re-tension the cord, which is an adjustment rather than a built-in behaviour
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): a knot, bead, washer or crimped sleeve that is bigger than the hole jams against it
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): a screw or clamp plate pinches the cord, a crimp sleeve grips by being squashed onto it, and turns round a post hold by rope friction (capstan effect)
- **input_output**: not applicable: it anchors a cord end rather than passing motion on
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable for knots and screw clamps, permanent for crimps. The figure-eight 'doesn't bind; it can be undone easily' even after jamming against a block, and the stevedore 'is easier to untie than a Figure 8 Knot even after heavy loading', while the overhand 'can bind so tightly that it can be really difficult to undo' and the EStar 'cannot be untied after heavy loading' (Animated Knots). The overhand is 'very secure, to the point of jamming badly' and suits permanent ends (Wikipedia). Crimped sleeves have to be cut off.
- **reuse_cycles**: many times for knots and screw clamps (the cord wears and kinks at the hole first); once for crimped sleeves
- **tools_needed**: None for knots. Scissors or a knife and, for synthetic cord, a flame to seal the cut end ('consider burning the edges', Printables 'Parametric Multi-Cord Anchor'); a thin wire hook to pull thin cord through holes (same model); a drill to open up small printed holes (Yale OpenHand T42 drills its tendon holes). Crimps: crimping pliers or flat-nose pliers for crimp beads (Beadaholique), or the matching Nicopress tool and its gauge for wire-rope stop sleeves (Nicopress). Clamps: a screwdriver or hex key for the screw (e.g. an M3 x 8 mm screw and nut in Printables 'Paracord thimble and rope clamp').
- **seal**: none: nothing makes it seal
- **load**: Light to medium, limited by the cord, which is weakest at the knot, and by the plastic round the hole. Strength figures differ, so here they are side by side: Animated Knots says 'assume that even brand new rope will perform at no more than 50% of its rated breaking strength' because of knots, kinks and imperfections; Yachting Monthly's tests at Marlow Ropes (2015) found a simple overhand knot cut the break load of 10 mm Dyneema and 12 mm polyester braid to '35 per cent of their ISO specification'. Wikipedia calls the figure-eight 'larger, stronger, and equally secure' compared with the overhand. Strong only in a straight pull along the cord; a cord cannot push, and a sideways pull drags it over the hole edge. A small printed hole can deform or crack before a strong cord breaks, especially with thin, hard cord such as Kevlar, which is why gm0 ties it round a washer ⚑ [uncertain: no source measured the pull-out force of a knot in a printed hole]. Never use a home-made knot or anchor to hold a person. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **adjustable**: Knots: no, retie to change the length. Screw clamp: yes, loosen, pull and retighten; 'clamping a string with a bolt instead of tying it around something makes it much easier to quickly change the tension' (gm0). Turns round a post: yes, unwrap and rewrap. Crimps: no.
- **play_and_precision**: Not a locating joint. Some length change: knots settle and tighten under the first heavy load, and stretchy cord or elastic lengthens under load, so allow slack and re-check tension after first use (Yale OpenHand T42 leaves 'enough tendon to leave slack after tying both ends' and then tensions with the servo pulley) ⚑ [uncertain: no source measures how much a knot settles]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **access_needed**: both sides: usually you pass the cord through the hole and tie or crimp the stopper on the far side. A knot pocket or end cap on the near face, a clamp, or a post on the accessible face lets you work from one side only.
- **materials**: Cords: natural fibre (cotton, jute), synthetic braided cord and paracord (nylon, polyester), very slippery high-modulus cord such as Dyneema (UHMWPE), which needs the EStar stopper or another non-slip knot (Animated Knots), elastic shock cord (NEMO tent-pole guide), fishing line, abrasive Kevlar string (gm0), beading wire (Beadaholique) and thin steel wire rope (Nicopress stop sleeves). Parts: printed PLA, PETG or TPU, wood, metal; PETG for outdoor use, PLA indoors (Printables 'Parametric Multi-Cord Anchor'; Prusa). Crimp sleeves are usually copper or aluminium ⚑ [uncertain: material named only in search-result summaries, not on an opened page]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **maker_difficulty**: easy: a hole and a knot; the only design choices are hole size, edge smoothing and how big a stopper the hole needs

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - 3D print (FDM): holes, knot pockets, end caps, posts and screw-clamp plates print with the part (Printables models below)
  - 3D print (resin): the same features; resin parts are more brittle, so spread the load with a washer ⚑ [uncertain: no resin-specific source opened]
  - CNC or drilling: drill holes and round their edges; Yale OpenHand T42 drills its 1.5 mm tendon holes after printing
  - laser cut: holes and tie-off slots in sheet ⚑ [uncertain: no laser-cutting source opened]
  - off-the-shelf part: crimp beads and tubes, wire-rope stop sleeves, cord end caps, washers, beads *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **print_in_place**: no: the hole, pocket, post or clamp prints with the part, but the cord is threaded, knotted, crimped or clamped afterwards
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Hole size: make pass-through holes a little bigger than the cord so it threads easily but the knot can't follow; one parametric anchor uses 0.5 mm clearance (a 3.5 mm hole for 3 mm cord) and 2 mm walls (Printables 'Parametric Multi-Cord Anchor'). Small holes print undersized, so drill them out if needed: the Yale OpenHand T42 drills its tendon holes to 1.5 +/- 0.5 mm after printing. 2) Smooth edges: a cord pulling round a sharp printed edge frays; T42 says 'minimize contact between tendon and ABS but ensure that tendon runs freely' and has you 'file down and deburr' bearing surfaces; chamfer or round the hole mouth on the side the knot sits. 3) Spread the load: for abrasive string such as Kevlar, 'tie the string around a washer to distribute the loads across the hole' instead of tying it straight into a hole in a plastic part (gm0); a bead behind the knot does the same for small cords ⚑ [uncertain: bead not tested by a source]. 4) Knot pockets and end caps: a recess or cap round the knot hides it and stops it snagging; one printed cord-end cap uses a 6 mm hole for paracord and drawstrings, with 'a small knot on the end' inside the main piece and a decorative cap threaded over it (Printables 'Cord End Caps'). Print pockets with the open end facing up, because printers 'can't print in mid-air' and only short bridges print without support (Prusa) ⚑ [uncertain: orientation is inferred from Prusa's general advice]. 5) Layer direction: the knot pushes on the plastic round the hole, so print with the hole axis vertical (the part lying flat) where you can, since FFF prints are 'stronger in one direction than another' (Prusa) ⚑ [uncertain: no test of knot pull-out versus orientation found]. 6) Screw clamps: a printed clamp plate with an M3 x 8 mm screw and a nut in a pocket pinches 4 mm paracord against a printed eyelet; tighten 1-2 turns, adjust the cord, then tighten fully, taking care 'not to squash the outer wrap of the cord between the closing edges' (Printables 'Paracord thimble and rope clamp'); a printed cable stop clamps a 3 mm cable with two M3 screws and nuts (Printables 'Cable Stop Clamp'). 7) Material: PETG for outdoor use, PLA indoors (Printables 'Parametric Multi-Cord Anchor'); PLA softens above about 60 °C and PETG is good below about 80 °C (Prusa), so a hot PLA part can let a knot pull through a softened hole. 8) Typical failures: hole too big (knot pulls through), hole too small or rough (cord won't thread or frays), a thin bridge behind the hole splitting along a layer line, and the knot shaking loose under vibration (pick a more secure stopper, below). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search for 'crimp beads' and 'crimp tubes' with 'crimping pliers' (jewellery and beading wire; Beadaholique), 'Nicopress stop sleeve' or 'wire rope stop sleeve' with its matching hand tool and gauge (Nicopress), 'fishing crimp sleeves' for monofilament ⚑ [uncertain: no fishing tackle page opened], 'cord end' or 'cord end cap' and 'cord lock' (drawstrings), 'fender washer' or 'penny washer' (large-diameter washers to spread the load) ⚑ [uncertain: washer types not named on an opened page], 'shock cord' and 'tent pole tips' (NEMO), and for Bowden-type cables 'barrel nipple' or 'cable end stop' (see the Bowden cable entry). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Use the bulkiest stopper the hole needs: the overhand (ABOK #46) is the smallest; the figure-eight (ABOK #570) is 'much better' than the overhand, but the double overhand (ABOK #516), stevedore (ABOK #456) and Ashley stopper (ABOK #526) 'all make better Stopper knots because they are larger and more stable' (Animated Knots, 'Figure 8 Knot').
  - Four recommended stoppers and when to choose each (tying steps: Animated Knots and Wikipedia pages listed in sources): figure-eight (ABOK #570) when you want to undo it again easily, though it can 'fall undone'; stevedore (ABOK #456) when it must stay easy to untie after heavy loads; double overhand (ABOK #516) for a reliable lump you won't undo ('the preferred Stopper knot' for climbing); Ashley stopper (ABOK #526) for the biggest lump, when the hole is 'too large for the figure-eight' (Ashley, quoted by Wikipedia) or vibration might shake a figure-eight loose. Special cases: the plain overhand (ABOK #46) is the smallest and 'one of the few stopper knots that can be tied tightly up against an object', and the EStar is for very slippery rope, being the only stopper Evans Starzinger found 'did not slip in Dyneema' (Animated Knots).
  - Strength, side by side: assume new rope gives 'no more than 50% of its rated breaking strength' because of knots and kinks (Animated Knots, 'Safety'); an overhand knot cut 10 mm Dyneema and 12 mm polyester braid to 35% of rated break load in Yachting Monthly's 2015 tests at Marlow Ropes.
  - Leave a tail: when tightening a double overhand, 'be sure to leave some tail sticking out from the end of the knot' (Wikipedia 'Double overhand knot'); for the Ashley stopper the half knot 'MUST be pulled snug first' (Animated Knots).
  - Tie abrasive string round a washer rather than straight into a hole in a plastic part (gm0 'Rigging').
  - Clamp instead of tying when you will need to re-tension: 'Clamping a string with a bolt instead of tying it ... makes it much easier to quickly change the tension of a string' (gm0 'Rigging').
  - Holes about 0.5 mm bigger than the cord, with 2 mm walls (Printables 'Parametric Multi-Cord Anchor').
  - Wire-rope stop sleeves: 'Thread cable through sleeve so that it extends 1/8"', swage with 'the correct number of presses' and check each finished sleeve in the gauge supplied with the tool (Nicopress instructions).
  - Turns round a post: the grip grows exponentially with the total angle of wrap and the friction coefficient, independent of the post's radius (Wikipedia 'Capstan equation'), so a few turns before the knot take most of the load off it.

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A drawstring or hoodie cord with a knot or a cord-end cap so it can't disappear into its channel (Printables 'Cord End Caps')
  - Tent-pole shock cord tied off at the pole tip (NEMO: 'tie the cord back onto itself ... Pull the knot super tight to minimize slipping')
  - A beaded necklace finished with a crimp bead squashed onto the wire (Beadaholique)
  - A rope through a pulley block or a sailing line with a figure-eight in the end so it can't run out (Animated Knots; Wikipedia)
- **when_to_use**: Whenever a cord, string, elastic or thin cable has to end at a part: drawstrings and toggles, guy lines finished behind a runner, elastic springs and shock cord in printed toys and tools, pull cords, robot tendons and string-driven slides, and bead or jewellery strings. A knot behind a hole is the simplest and cheapest option, needs no tools and can be retied; robot builders anchor tendons this way at the fingertip (Yale OpenHand T42: tendons run through routing ports, 'anchoring at the back of the fingertip'). Use a screw clamp where the tension needs setting and resetting (gm0), a crimp where the cord is wire or too slippery or thin to knot well (Nicopress; Beadaholique), and turns round a post where the load is high for the cord and you want the knot to carry little of it (Wikipedia 'Capstan equation').
- **when_not_to_use**: Never for anything that holds a person (climbing, rescue, swings, lifting): use rated hardware and trained methods. Not for steel wire rope carrying real load: use proper wire-rope terminations (clips, thimbles and swaged sleeves; see that entry). Not where the cord must be released quickly under load: use a cam cleat, clam cleat or cord lock. Not for slippery Dyneema-type cord with ordinary stoppers, which can slip (Animated Knots); use the EStar, a crimp or a clamp. Not for electrical cable strain relief in mains equipment unless the method is part of an approved design; that is the job of a cable gland or the product's own strain relief, and mains work must follow local law (see 'Cable gland and strain relief').
- **environment_limits**: Water: natural fibre swells when wet and jams knots (Ashley's 'oysterman's stopper' turned out to be 'a badly water-swollen figure eight stopper knot', Wikipedia), and a loaded double overhand 'can be difficult to untie, especially when wet' (Wikipedia). Slippery modern fibres: Dyneema-type cord lets most stoppers slip; the EStar was the one Starzinger found held (Animated Knots). Repeated loading and vibration: 'Many knots can loosen with prolonged intermittent strain' (Animated Knots 'Safety'); the figure-eight 'can fall undone', while the Ashley stopper 'is far less prone to shake loose' (Animated Knots). Heat: a PLA part softens above about 60 °C (Prusa), so the hole can stretch and let the knot through in a hot car or in sunlight; PETG is better below about 80 °C (Prusa). Sunlight: PLA 'degrades under UV light' (Prusa); cord also weakens in the sun ⚑ [uncertain: no cord-maker page opened]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **safety_notes**: Never trust a home-made knot, crimp or printed anchor to hold a person. 'Handle rope with care, inspect and test any knot you tie, and respect any rope subject to a heavy load' (Animated Knots 'Safety'). A tensioned elastic cord or string that slips its anchor whips back and can hit eyes or hands, so keep faces clear when testing ⚑ [uncertain: no source opened states this]. Cut ends of wire and crimped wire are sharp. When sealing synthetic cord ends with a flame, the melted plastic is hot and sticky and can burn ⚑ [uncertain]. The underwriter's knot is a stopper used inside mains plugs and lamp holders; this entry gives no mains wiring steps, and mains work must follow local law. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **common_failures**: Knot pulls through: the hole is too big or the plastic has softened or cracked; use a bulkier stopper (Ashley or double overhand), a washer or bead behind it (gm0), or a smaller hole. Cord breaks at the knot, its weakest point (Yachting Monthly; Animated Knots): use stronger cord or turns round a post to take load off the knot. Cord frays or is cut at the hole edge: chamfer, deburr and keep contact gentle (Yale OpenHand T42). Knot shakes loose: the figure-eight 'can fall undone' (Animated Knots); use a stevedore, Ashley or double overhand stopper. Knot jams and can't be undone: the overhand binds badly (Animated Knots; Wikipedia); use a figure-eight or stevedore if you'll want it off again. Slippery cord slides out: use the EStar stopper, a crimp or a clamp. Crimp slips: not pressed fully or wrong size; use the right tool and the gauge (Nicopress) and pull the loop to length before crimping, because afterwards 'the loop will be permanently fixed' (Beadaholique). Clamp crushes or cuts the cord, or the cord slides out from under it: don't let the cord get squashed between the closing edges (Printables 'Paracord thimble and rope clamp').
- **related**: 
  - Rope-end finishing (whipping, heat-sealing, back splice, aglet): stops the end fraying; a stopper knot stops it pulling through a hole
  - Basic knots (overhand knot, half hitch, reef knot): the overhand is the simplest stopper
  - Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye: tie the cord round something instead of stopping it behind a hole
  - Wire-rope terminations (wire-rope clip, thimble, crimped sleeve): the rated way to end steel wire rope
  - Cord lock (spring toggle) and guy-line slider: grip a cord anywhere along its length instead of at the end; a guy-line slider still needs a stopper knot in the tail
  - Bowden cable (push-pull cable): the inner wire ends in a metal nipple that sits in a slot in the lever (or in a clamp), the factory version of a stopper in a pocket
  - Capstan cable drive and tendon-driven joint: cable anchors on drums and fingertips are stopper knots, crimps or clamps
  - Cable gland and strain relief: the electrical version of anchoring a cable where it enters a part
  - Elastic band and elastic cord springs, and shock-corded sectional poles: elastic cord anchored by knots behind holes
  - Eyelets and grommets: reinforce a hole in fabric or sheet so a knot or cord doesn't tear through
