## 377 Load-bearing stitch patterns for straps and webbing (bar tack, box-X stitch)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Dense stitch patterns used where a strap is sewn back on itself or onto a buckle, ring or another strap, such as a bar tack, a box with an X through it or a zigzag pattern, so the load spreads over many stitches

*Also covers:* Also covers thread and needle choice and published test results (sources differ: record them side by side). Safety: never home-sew climbing slings, harnesses, tow or lifting straps.

### What it is

- **plain_name**: The dense blocks of stitching you see where a strap is folded back and sewn to itself or round a buckle or ring: a tight zigzag bar, a box with an X through it, or rows of W shapes, which spread the pull over hundreds of stitches so the joint doesn't tear
- **term**: Load-bearing stitch pattern for webbing (bar tack, box-X stitch, W-W stitch; sewn webbing joint)
- **aliases**: 
  - bar tack, bar-tack, bartack: a short, dense block of zigzag stitches (Wikipedia 'Bar tack'); on parachutes typically a '1 inch x 42 stitch bar tack' (FAA Parachute Rigger Handbook)
  - box-X stitch, box X, X-box, boxed cross (Fire Engineering 1984; Ripstop by the Roll; Sailrite)
  - W-W pattern, WW, double W, three-point or four-point double W (FAA; Fire Engineering 1984)
  - Z stitch, zigzag pattern (Ripstop by the Roll)
  - diamond stitch pattern (Sailrite)
  - straight-stitch rows, parallel rows, reinforcement stitches (Practical Sailor; Sailrite)
  - sewn loop, sewn eye, webbing loop, stitched webbing join, webbing junction (Sailrite; Practical Sailor; FAA)
  - sandwich (inserted) reinforcement: an extra strip of webbing sewn inside the overlap (Practical Sailor)
  - lockstitch (stitch type 301) and zigzag lockstitch (stitch types 304 and 308), the machine stitches used (FAA)
  - arrowhead tack and crow's foot tack (garment relatives of the bar tack) (Wikipedia 'Bar tack')
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware
- **one_liner**: A strap is overlapped on itself or round a buckle, ring or slot and sewn with a dense, planned stitch pattern (bar tacks, a box with an X, W shapes or close rows) so that the pull is shared by many stitches and the joint can carry a large share of the webbing's own strength.
- **complexity**: A few parts: the webbing (doubled over or overlapped), the thread, and often the hardware it wraps round (buckle, D-ring, slide or a slot in a rigid part); sometimes an extra strip of webbing sandwiched inside the overlap (Practical Sailor). Made with a lockstitch sewing machine (with zigzag for bar tacks) or by hand with a sailmaker's needle and palm (Sailrite; Practical Sailor).
- **how_it_works**: Each stitch is a small loop of thread through both layers; when the strap is pulled, the layers try to slide apart and every stitch is loaded across its thread, so the joint's strength is roughly the number of stitches times the strength of each (Sailrite's formula: stitches per inch x thread strength x 1.5 = pounds per inch of stitching). The patterns put many stitches into a short overlap and run lines across and along the strap so the load is spread (Ripstop by the Roll; Sailrite). Stretchy nylon webbing loads the stitches nearest the free tail end first, so joints fail from the tail row and then unzip in a moment; dense stitching close to the ends, and a stiffening strip sewn inside the overlap, make the stitches share the load more evenly (Practical Sailor; Fire Engineering 1984).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: join and hold: joins a strap to itself (a loop or eye), to a buckle, ring, slide or slot, or to another strap (Sailrite; Ripstop by the Roll)
- **motion**: none: the sewn joint doesn't move; the strap can still swivel round the bar of the hardware it is sewn round.
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 between the sewn layers; a loop sewn round a ring or bar can still turn about that bar (1).
- **special_behaviour**: none: it simply holds. (Contrasting thread makes broken stitches easy to see, which helps inspection; Practical Sailor; Sailrite.)
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the thread passes through both layers, so they can't slide apart without breaking or tearing out the stitches
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): tensioned stitches also press the layers together, which helps share the load (FAA: thread tension affects seam strength)
- **input_output**: not applicable
- **power**: passive (no power needed).
- **removability**: semi-permanent (can be undone but may damage parts): stitches can be cut out with a seam ripper or scalpel, as riggers do for repairs, but every needle hole stays, and the FAA lists 'the number of times the webbing is re-sewn' among the factors that lower the strength of the junction (FAA).
- **reuse_cycles**: once: re-sewing the same webbing is possible but weakens it each time (FAA); for anything important use new webbing.
- **tools_needed**: A lockstitch sewing machine that can feed two or more layers of webbing (Sailrite says lockstitch is 'the only' type recommended for load-bearing equipment; Fire Engineering 1984 says the same), with zigzag for bar tacks (Sailrite; Ripstop by the Roll); a strong needle in good condition (FAA: blunt or damaged needles weaken webbing); or, by hand, a sailmaker's needle and sailmaker's palm (Practical Sailor). Also: scissors or a hot knife to cut and seal synthetic webbing, a marking pencil and ruler to draw the pattern, basting tape to stop the layers sliding, and a seam ripper (Sailrite; FAA).
- **seal**: none.
- **load**: Medium to heavy along the strap; the joint is strongest when the pull is straight along the overlap (stitches in shear) and weakest when the loop is spread open, which peels the stitches at the throat one row at a time: Practical Sailor found any webbing eye 'will peel apart at 500 to 800 pounds' if spread wide. Published efficiencies (joint strength as a share of webbing strength) differ, side by side: hand-stitched nylon levels off at about 69% with plain stitching, 83% with more rows and 100% with a sandwiched webbing insert, and 'commercial bar tacking is known to be about 90 percent efficient' (Practical Sailor); Sailrite's machine-sewn loops always broke below unstitched webbing, e.g. two W samples at 4,300 lb (Sailrite); Fire Engineering's 1984 test on 9,000 lb webbing saw stitching start to break at 3,000 lb (W) and 3,500 lb (box), with the box pattern failing at 4,500 lb, and much lower figures with thinner bobbin thread (Fire Engineering 1984). A badly made harness of 6,000 lb webbing may hold only 4,000 lb (Fire Engineering 1984).
- **adjustable**: no: the overlap length and position are fixed once sewn; add a buckle or slide for adjustment.
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable
- **access_needed**: both sides: the needle passes through all the layers and the machine's bobbin (or a hand needle) works from underneath, so the joint must be sewn before closed hardware is trapped in a finished assembly.
- **materials**: Webbing: nylon (stretchy, soft, strongest thread grip), polyester (better in sun and for marine use), polypropylene (cheap, for bags and crafts), Dyneema; flat or tubular (Sailrite; Practical Sailor). Thread: bonded nylon or bonded polyester, polyester only for outdoor use, nothing thinner than Tex 90 (Sailrite); parachute harnesses use 5-cord nylon (about 40 lb) and canopies E thread (about 8.5 lb) to V-T-295 (FAA); by hand, doubled 40-50 lb polyester whipping twine (Practical Sailor). Avoid cotton (a harness re-sewn with cotton thread broke at about 10 lb per thread versus 45 lb for 5-cord nylon; FAA) and Dyneema or Kevlar twine on nylon webbing (Dyneema twine 'cuts through, resulting in a 30 percent loss in strength'; Practical Sailor). Hardware sewn in: metal or plastic buckles, D-rings, slides, or a rigid 3D-printed part with slots.
- **maker_difficulty**: moderate: a home or portable walking-foot machine and the right thread can sew bar tacks and box-X joints for bags, straps and gear (Ripstop by the Roll; Sailrite), but thick layers and heavy thread may need a heavy-duty or semi-industrial machine (Sailrite), and life-safety straps must not be home-made (UIAA; FAA).

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - sewing by machine (lockstitch, with zigzag for bar tacks) or by hand: not one of the standard making methods, but the core process here
  - off-the-shelf part: webbing, bonded thread, buckles, D-rings and slides
  - 3D print (FDM): buckles, slides, D-rings and slotted rigid parts that the strap is sewn round (Printables 305674, 228168)
- **print_in_place**: no: it is sewn; only the hardware the strap is sewn to may be printed.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Size the slot of a printed buckle, slide or D-ring from the measured webbing, not its nominal size: one printed tri-glide made for '1 inch' webbing uses 27 mm slots because that is the real width (Printables 305674); leave room for the doubled strap's thickness. 2) Print strap hardware flat so the strap's pull runs along the layer lines, with four or more walls (Printables 228168; Printables 415716 suggests 4-5 perimeters and 100% infill for a strong buckle). 3) Prefer PETG for printed strap hardware; PLA slowly 'flows' (creeps) under constant load (maker notes, Printables 415716 and 602174). 4) A closed printed bar must be threaded onto the strap before the loop is sewn; if the strap must be added later, use a slotted 'no-thread' design (Printables 305674) or a screw-clamp repair buckle (Printables 1333399). 5) Keep the stitch pattern far enough from the printed part for the presser foot and needle to clear it; Fire Engineering's 1984 guidance asks for a stitched area at least 3 in long where hardware is attached, and Sailrite suggests an overlap about three times the webbing width for 1 in webbing. 6) Don't stitch over melted (heat-sealed) webbing ends: the hard burr abrades the thread (Practical Sailor); fold the end under or keep it clear of the stitches. 7) To sew webbing onto a printed part instead of round it, see Sew-on part (sewing holes, loops and slots on a rigid part).
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Catalogue names: 'bonded nylon thread' and 'bonded polyester thread' in Tex 90 (V-92) and Tex 135 (V-138) (Sailrite: 'V-92 ... is the same as Tex 90'; 'Tex 135 (V-138)'), or lighter for bags only; parachute 'nylon thread V-T-295' sizes E, FF, 3-cord and 5-cord (FAA); 'polyester whipping twine' (Practical Sailor); heavy needles, size 18 and up for E (V-69) thread (FAA), size 20 used by Sailrite for Tex 90; 'sailmaker's needle' and 'sailmaker's palm' (Practical Sailor); nylon, polyester and polypropylene 'flat webbing' and 'tubular webbing' (Sailrite); 'basting tape' (Sailrite); 'hot knife'; 'seam ripper' (FAA); machines: portable walking-foot, compound-feed, heavy-duty harness and bar-tack machines (FAA lists examples; Sailrite tested on an Ultrafeed LSZ, brand). Brand names are given only as catalogue search terms.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Stitching needed (Sailrite): seam strength in lb per inch of stitching = stitches per inch x thread strength (lb) x 1.5; e.g. 8 SPI x 15 lb (Tex 90) x 1.5 = 180 lb/in, so a 4,050 lb loop needs 22.5 in of total stitching. Add extra stitching as a safety factor, because some samples broke below the calculated figure.
  - Stitches per inch, side by side: 8-12 SPI is 'generally recommended for sewing webbing' (Sailrite, 2019, Tex 90 thread); 4-7 SPI for safety equipment, as more than seven reduces elasticity and cuts fibres and fewer than four snags (Fire Engineering 1984, size 6 thread); 7-11 SPI for parachute canopy lockstitch and zigzag work (FAA). The FAA warns that 'too many stitches per inch dramatically affects the strength of the seam by perforating the material'.
  - Pattern strength, side by side: the FAA says 'A W–W pattern is stronger than a box X pattern'; Fire Engineering (1984) found the three-point double W 'slightly stronger' than the boxed cross but the boxed cross 'more than adequate' and better where sideways strength matters; Sailrite (2019) found 'no clear "winning" stitch pattern' and that the amount of stitching matters most; Practical Sailor found that for nylon webbing only the stitching within about 1 inch of the ends made a real difference, and that X, box or Z arguments are 'likely a carryover' from canvas and polyester; Ripstop by the Roll calls the box-X 'one of the strongest attachment methods you can sew on a conventional machine'.
  - Add plain reinforcement rows: Sailrite's W, diamond and box-X samples needed separate three-pass straight-stitch rows above and below the pattern to reach a reasonable strength; Practical Sailor's best hand-sewn pattern for 1 in, 4,000 lb webbing was eight rows of seven 1/8 in stitches with rows 1/8 in apart (average break 3,650 lb), and twice as many stitches for a closed loop whose ends face opposite ways.
  - Overlap length: about three times the webbing width for 1 in webbing, and nearly twice the width for 2-3 in webbing, even if the formula needs less (Sailrite); a stitched pattern at least 3 in long where hardware is attached, with 120-150 stitches in a 3-1/2 x 1-3/4 in seam (Fire Engineering 1984); a webbing eye at least 2 in long for a 5/16 in carabiner or shackle, longer for bigger hardware (Practical Sailor).
  - Edges and ends: keep stitching at least 1/4 in from the edge and backstitch at least 1/2 in at the end of every line (Fire Engineering 1984).
  - Home-machine bar tack (Sailrite): Sailrite's samples used two straight passes to lock the layers, then three zigzag passes at 4 mm width and 4 mm stitch length, ten bar tacks 1/2 in apart, which proved more than needed (eight would do). Ripstop by the Roll: limit plain straight stitching to '3-4 passes at a max' because more holes weaken the webbing. Garment bar tacks are much smaller, about 1/16-1/8 in wide and 1/4-3/8 in long (Wikipedia 'Bar tack').
  - A sandwiched insert of webbing inside the joint (Practical Sailor used a 3/4 in overlap) lets the far stitches share the load and reached 100% of the webbing's rated breaking strength in hand-sewn tests (Practical Sailor).
  - Thread: bonded nylon or polyester, at least Tex 90, polyester for outdoor use, contrasting colour so popped stitches show (Sailrite); E (V-69) thread needs at least a size 18 needle (FAA); larger needles make larger holes and blunt needles damage webbing (FAA).
  - Webbing: the loop can never be stronger than the webbing; choose webbing with a higher minimum breaking strength than needed, and about twice the needed strength for outdoor use because sunlight weakens it (Sailrite). Retire webbing when about 30% of the fibres are worn at any point (Sailrite).
  - Test your joint: sew samples with the same materials and layers before sewing the project (Ripstop by the Roll).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - The box-X or bar-tacked joints where backpack and bag straps are sewn round a buckle or D-ring (Ripstop by the Roll; Sailrite)
  - The bar tacks on a climbing sling (Practical Sailor: climbing slings 'aren't sewn in an X; they are bar-tacked')
  - The W-W stitch blocks on a parachute harness where the chest strap joins the main lift web (FAA: 'Most chest straps are installed with a 3-point WW')
  - The small bar tacks at the bottom of a jeans fly and on belt loops and pocket corners (Wikipedia 'Bar tack')
- **when_to_use**: Use a sewn pattern to make a permanent loop, eye or attachment in webbing for bags, backpack straps, camera and instrument straps, pet leads, tool rolls, cargo and storage straps, sail ties and reefing straps, and to fix a strap to a buckle, D-ring, slide or a slot in a 3D-printed or wooden part (Sailrite; Practical Sailor; Ripstop by the Roll). Sewing is the standard way to join webbing, because knots in webbing are bulky and weaken it ('reduce strength 40 percent or more'; Practical Sailor). Choose a bar tack when you have a zigzag machine and a short overlap, a box-X for a neat, sideways-strong joint on a home machine, and W or diamond patterns plus reinforcement rows when you want to calculate the strength (Sailrite; Ripstop by the Roll; Fire Engineering 1984).
- **when_not_to_use**: Never home-sew anything that holds a person or a heavy moving load: climbing slings, harnesses, tethers, tow straps or lifting straps. Climbing slings must meet EN 566 (at least 22 kN since 1994; UIAA/BMC), parachute harness work may only be done by certificated master riggers (FAA), and even Sailrite's guide, which shows DIY loops for towing and lifting dinghies, carries the disclaimer that it 'is not corroborated or verified by a safety authority'. Buy certified, factory-made gear instead. Don't use a sewn joint where the strap must be adjusted (use a ladder-lock buckle, cam buckle or tri-glide), or opened and closed often (use a side-release buckle or hook-and-loop). For wire rope use crimped sleeves or clips; for rope use a splice or a knot. Avoid random heavy stitching in place of a planned pattern: Fire Engineering (1984) says equipment sewn that way should be destroyed.
- **environment_limits**: Sunlight (UV) weakens nylon webbing and thread: use polyester thread outdoors and webbing with twice the needed strength (Sailrite); Practical Sailor notes nylon 'has never been very good in the sun' and found sewn failures only where webbing was sun-rotted or badly abraded, and suggests a coat of paint or a cover to protect stitching. Store webbing clean, dry and out of the sun (Sailrite; UIAA/BMC). Heat: nylon and Dyneema have low melting points; a loaded rope pulled through a nylon sling can melt it 'in as little as 3 metres of lowering' (UIAA/BMC). Wear: retire webbing with about 30% of its fibres worn, or if it crackles when flexed (Sailrite). Printed hardware: PLA creeps under steady load (maker note, Printables 415716). Exact temperature limits for webbing and thread were not checked ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **safety_notes**: Never home-sew climbing slings, harnesses, safety tethers, tow straps or lifting straps; a failed sewn joint fails suddenly ('the rest of the stitching failed in a millisecond'; Practical Sailor). Life-safety slings must meet EN 566 (UIAA/BMC), and the FAA restricts harness repairs to certificated master riggers and gives a real case where a harness re-sewn with cotton thread broke at the junction (FAA). Spread loops peel apart at much lower loads than straight pulls (Practical Sailor). Use contrasting thread so broken stitches show, inspect stitching often, and retire worn webbing (Sailrite; UIAA/BMC). Hot knives and flame-sealing webbing ends are burn and fume hazards ⚑ [uncertain: no opened source states this]. Keep fingers clear of the needle when feeding thick webbing through a machine ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **common_failures**: 1) Joint unzips from the tail end: the end row takes most of the load in stretchy nylon, then the rest fails at once (Practical Sailor). Fix: dense stitching near the ends, extra rows, or a sandwiched insert. 2) Too little stitching for the load (Sailrite's formula) or random stitching (Fire Engineering 1984). 3) Too many stitches per inch or too many rows, which perforate and weaken the webbing (FAA; Ripstop by the Roll). 4) Wrong thread: cotton (FAA), thread thinner than Tex 90 (Sailrite), Dyneema or Kevlar twine on nylon webbing, which cuts it (Practical Sailor), or a thin bobbin thread that breaks first (Fire Engineering 1984). 5) Damaged or oversized needles weakening the webbing (FAA). 6) Loose or uneven thread tension giving raised, skipped or weak stitches (Fire Engineering 1984; FAA). 7) Stitching over melted ends, whose burr abrades the thread (Practical Sailor). 8) Eye spread wide by a big shackle or several attachments, peeling the throat stitches (Practical Sailor). 9) No reinforcement rows above and below a W, diamond or box-X pattern (Sailrite). 10) Sun-rotted or abraded webbing tearing beside sound stitching (Practical Sailor).
- **related**: 
  - Sewn seams and hand stitches (lockstitch, saddle stitch, flat-felled seam): joins fabric along a line; this entry is the dense, load-bearing version for straps
  - Sew-on part (sewing holes, loops and slots on a rigid part): how to sew straps and fabric onto a 3D-printed or rigid part
  - Ladder-lock buckle (tension lock, tri-glide), Side-release buckle, Cam buckle (webbing cam buckle): the hardware these joints usually attach; they make the strap adjustable or openable
  - Basic strap and garment closures (prong buckle, button, toggle and loop): mentions bar tacks at buttonhole ends
  - Hook-and-loop fastener: often box-stitched onto straps
  - Basic knots (overhand knot, half hitch and reef knot) and the water knot: the no-sew way to join webbing, but weaker and bulkier (Practical Sailor; Wikipedia 'Webbing')
  - Rope splices (eye splice, short splice, braided-rope splices): the equivalent permanent eye in rope
  - Leather and textile rivets: Fire Engineering (1984) notes rivets can reinforce sewn areas if washers are used
  - Heat-sealed seam (heat-sealed plastic film and coated fabric): joins coated fabrics by melting instead of sewing
  - Wire-rope terminations (wire-rope clip, thimble, crimped sleeve): the equivalent loop for steel cable
  - Conductive thread (sewn circuit, e-textile stitching): stitching that carries current rather than load

  - [Life Belts and Harnesses: Their Strength Lies in Stitching (Fire Engineering, October 1984)](https://www.fireengineering.com/firefighting-equipment/life-belts-and-harnesses-their-strength-lies-in-stitching/): Figures of the lockstitch, the four-point double W and boxed cross patterns, an overlap sliding under load, and photos of test samples failing *(licence: link only (copyright Fire Engineering))*

Its notes say not to use it for safety-of-life applications *(licence: Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-ShareAlike)*

## 378 Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye (clove hitch, round turn and two half hitches)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Knots that tie a line to something solid, such as a post, rail, ring, bolt or a hole in a part, and can be tied and undone without tools

*Also covers:* Also covers the cow hitch (lark's head), timber hitch, horn-cleat hitch and the Palomar knot for slippery line.

### What it is

- **plain_name**: Ways of tying a rope, cord or string straight onto something solid, such as a post, rail, ring, bar, hook, eye or a hole in a part, with no hardware, so it holds while pulled and can be untied again by hand
- **term**: Hitch (hitch knot): clove hitch, round turn and two half hitches, cow hitch (lark's head), timber hitch, cleat hitch and Palomar knot
- **aliases**: 
  - hitch, hitch knot: a knot 'used to secure the end of a rope to a fixed object' (Wikipedia 'Round turn and two half-hitches')
  - clove hitch (ABOK #1245, p. 224, per Animated Knots; Wikipedia lists many related entries, e.g. #11, #1176-#1180, #1773-#1779)
  - round turn and two half hitches (ABOK #1720, p. 296, per Animated Knots; Wikipedia also lists #1721, #1784, #1834-#1836, #1883, #1884, #1910); round turn and a half hitch, two round turns and two half hitches; with more turns, a pipe hitch (Wikipedia)
  - two half hitches and buntline hitch: what you get by tying a clove hitch round the rope's own standing part after a turn round the object (Wikipedia 'Clove hitch')
  - cow hitch, lark's head, lark's foot, girth hitch, ring hitch, lanyard hitch, strap hitch, baggage tag loop, tag knot, strop bend (ABOK #1673 and #1802; lark's head #5) (Wikipedia 'Cow hitch'; Animated Knots 'Cow Hitch')
  - timber hitch, bowyer's knot, lumberman's knot, countryman's knot (ABOK #1665, p. 290; Wikipedia also lists #1668, #195, #479, #2161); with a half hitch added further along: timber hitch and half hitch, or killick hitch (Wikipedia 'Timber hitch'; Animated Knots 'Timber Hitch')
  - cleat hitch, cleat knot, cleat tie, on a horn cleat (ABOK #1615 per Wikipedia 'Cleat hitch'; Animated Knots cites #1594, p. 284, for the belaying-pin version)
  - Palomar knot: a fishing knot for tying line to a hook, lure, swivel or small ring (Wikipedia 'Palomar knot', which gives no ABOK number; Animated Knots 'Palomar Knot')
  - anchor hitch, anchor bend, fisherman's bend (ABOK #1841, p. 309): a close alternative to the round turn and two half hitches on a ring (Animated Knots 'Anchor Hitch')
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware
- **one_liner**: A knot that ties a line directly to a post, rail, ring, bar, eye, log or a hole in a part, holding by the turns and tucks gripping the object and the rope itself, so it needs no hardware or tools and can be untied by hand; which hitch to use depends on what you tie to and whether you will adjust or release it.
- **complexity**: Single piece: just the cord, plus the thing it is tied to (a post, rail, ring, eye bolt, hook, horn cleat, log or a hole in a part). Optional: a horn cleat to make tying off quick (Animated Knots 'Cleat Hitch'), or a washer that the cord is tied round so a printed hole doesn't take all the load (gm0 'Rigging').
- **how_it_works**: A hitch takes the line round the object and then tucks it under itself, so that pulling on the loaded (standing) part pinches the tucked end against the object and holds it by friction. Extra turns round the object add friction, so the turns take most of the pull and the final hitches only have to hold what is left: the round turn 'should take the initial strain while you complete the knot' (Animated Knots), and on a small spar it 'makes a stronger knot and dissipates the wear' (Ashley, quoted by Wikipedia 'Round turn and two half-hitches'). The clove hitch is two single hitches made the same way round, and the cow hitch two made in opposite directions, which is why the cow hitch needs both legs pulled (Wikipedia 'Cow hitch'). The timber hitch grips only while it is pulled and 'practically falls apart when pull ceases' (Ashley, via Animated Knots). The Palomar doubles the line through the eye before tying a simple knot, which keeps 'almost all of the original line strength' (Wikipedia 'Palomar knot').

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: ties a line to a post, rail, ring, bar, hook eye, eye bolt, log or a hole in a part
  - lock, and release by hand: all are tied and untied by hand with no tools; the timber hitch and a cleat hitch without a locking turn come off quickly, and a clove hitch can be loosened and slid to adjust the length (Wikipedia 'Clove hitch', 'Timber hitch'; Animated Knots 'Cleat Hitch')
- **motion**: none once tied and loaded; a clove hitch can be rolled along a bar to move it, as when adjusting stage curtains (Animated Knots), and a cow hitch or round turn on a ring can slide round the ring.
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 along the pull once tied and loaded; the line can still swing and twist about the object, and a ring can turn inside the hitch ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - quick-release: the timber hitch 'cannot jam and may be instantly loosened' (Ashley, quoted by Wikipedia 'Timber hitch'); a cleat hitch is often left without a locking half hitch where the line must be let go quickly under load (Animated Knots 'Cleat Hitch'); half hitches can be tied with a bight (a loop) instead of the end (Animated Knots 'Round Turn & Two Half Hitches')
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): turns round the object and tucks under the standing part grip by friction; more turns give more grip (Wikipedia 'Round turn and two half-hitches' relates it to the capstan equation)
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): a hitch closed round a ring, or through an eye or hole, cannot come off over the object; the Palomar's loop passes round the hook or ring (Wikipedia 'Palomar knot')
- **input_output**: not applicable
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: tool-free quick release: all are untied by hand. Some bind: a clove hitch in small cord 'may become difficult to untie' after heavy loading (Wikipedia 'Clove hitch'), while the cow hitch is 'less likely to bind and be hard to untie' (Animated Knots) and the timber hitch is 'easily untied even after heavy loading' (Wikipedia 'Timber hitch'). A Palomar in fine fishing line is usually cut off rather than untied ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **reuse_cycles**: effectively unlimited: the knot can be retied again and again; the cord wears where it bends round the object, so inspect it ('inspect and test any knot you tie', Animated Knots 'Safety').
- **tools_needed**: None. Optional: a knife or scissors to trim the tail (the Palomar's to about 3 mm: Wikipedia 'Palomar knot'), a horn cleat, and a washer to tie abrasive string round in a printed part (gm0 'Rigging').
- **seal**: none
- **load**: Light to medium, limited by the cord, which is weakest at the knot, and by how well the hitch grips the object. Strength figures differ, so here they are side by side: Animated Knots says to 'Assume that even brand new rope will perform at no more than 50% of its rated breaking strength'; a chart taken from Day's 'The Art of Knotting and Splicing' gives about 60% for the clove hitch, 65% for two half hitches and 70% for the timber hitch, but its compiler says it should 'only be used as a very rough guide' (IGKT Solent 'Knot Strength Chart'); SAIL Magazine's 2008 pull tests on 1/2 in rope at New England Ropes found a clove hitch with two half hitches lost 35% (nylon double braid), 36.6% (polyester double braid) and 61.44% (Spectra-cored braid) of the rope's strength, while a round turn and two half hitches 'rolled open in all three tests before the rope snapped', at 36-68% loss; for fishing line the Palomar 'retains almost all of the original line strength' (Wikipedia) and is 'one of the strongest and most reliable fishing knots' (Animated Knots). Strong only in a pull along the line: a cow hitch 'Can fail unless equal tension is applied to both of the standing parts', and a clove hitch 'Can spill if the standing part is pulled forcibly in the wrong direction' (Wikipedia). Never use a home-made knot to hold a person.
- **adjustable**: Partly: with a clove hitch, 'feeding in rope from either direction will loosen the knot to be tightened at a new position' (Wikipedia 'Clove hitch'), and it can be rolled along a bar (Animated Knots); the other hitches are untied and retied to change length. To pull a line tight and adjust it, use the hitches in Tensioning and adjustable hitches.
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable: hitches don't locate parts; they settle and may slip a little when first loaded (a clove hitch in climbing rope 'will slip to a point, and then stop slipping', Wikipedia), so dress the knot, leave a tail and re-check it.
- **access_needed**: not applicable as a fastener, but you must be able to reach round the post or rail, or pass the end through the ring, eye or hole. A cow hitch made with a closed loop 'can be tied without access to the ends of the fastening loop' (Wikipedia 'Cow hitch'); for a Palomar the hook or lure 'has to pass through the loop' (Animated Knots); a clove hitch made as stacked loops can be dropped over the end of a post (Animated Knots lists a loops method) ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **materials**: Any cord, rope, string or line: natural fibre, nylon, polyester and paracord; slippery high-modulus cords (Spectra, Dyneema) slip more and lost the most strength in SAIL Magazine's tests, and modern synthetic rope needs more timber-hitch turns (five or more instead of Ashley's three) (Wikipedia 'Timber hitch'); 'With certain types of cord, the clove hitch can slip when loaded' (Wikipedia); braided and monofilament fishing line (Palomar, recommended for braid by Animated Knots); abrasive Kevlar string (gm0). Tied to: wood, metal or printed posts, rails and pegs (round, not square: Wikipedia), rings and D-rings, eye bolts, horn cleats (PETG, ASA, ABS or nylon outdoors, not PLA: Printables 'Sun Sail Rope Cleat / Small Horn Cleat').
- **maker_difficulty**: easy: no parts and a few minutes' practice per knot with the Animated Knots animations; the design job is giving the part a round, smooth post, rail, eye or cleat to tie to.

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part: cord, rope and horn cleats; the hitch itself needs no making
  - 3D print (FDM): round posts, rails, eyes, slots and horn cleats to tie onto (Printables models in make_it_yourself)
  - woodwork: pegs, rails and cleats
  - laser cut or CNC: tie-off holes, slots and posts in sheet ⚑ [uncertain] *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **print_in_place**: no: the post, eye or cleat is printed and the cord is tied on afterwards.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Make posts, pegs and rails round: the clove hitch 'is also unreliable when used on a square or rectangular post, rather than round' (Wikipedia 'Clove hitch'). 2) Give them a generous diameter and smooth edges: on a small spar a round turn 'makes a stronger knot and dissipates the wear' (Ashley via Wikipedia); round or chamfer the edges of holes and eyes, and for abrasive string such as Kevlar, 'tie the string around a washer to distribute the loads across the hole' instead of straight through a hole in a plastic part (gm0 'Rigging'). 3) If you need to re-tension the line, clamp it instead of tying it: 'Clamping a string with a bolt instead of tying it around something makes it much easier to quickly change the tension' (gm0). 4) Strength direction: a printed peg sticking out of a plate tends to snap along a layer line when a rope pulls it sideways, since 'FFF prints are stronger in one direction than another' (Prusa); print pegs lying down, or use a screw, bolt or metal pin as the post ⚑ [uncertain]. 5) Printed horn cleats: use PETG, ASA, ABS or nylon outdoors, because PLA 'is not ideal for sun, heat, or long-term outdoor tension'; add extra walls and infill, mount with proper screws, and treat it as 'not certified marine, climbing, towing, or life-safety hardware' (Printables 'Sun Sail Rope Cleat / Small Horn Cleat'). 6) Make rings and eyes big enough: a round turn passes the cord through twice, and a Palomar's loop must pass over the whole object (Animated Knots) ⚑ [uncertain: no source gives a hole size]. 7) Typical failures: sharp hole edges cutting the cord, a square peg letting a clove hitch roll off, a thin printed eye splitting along its layers, and PLA cleats creeping in the sun. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Horn cleats (nylon, aluminium or stainless), rope rings and D-rings, eye bolts and screw eyes, bollards and belaying pins for bigger lines; cords such as paracord, braided polyester or nylon cord, braided fishing line and monofilament; whipping twine or a hot knife to finish the tails (see Rope-end finishing). Search supplier catalogues by these names; the hitch itself needs no parts.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Choose by job (tying steps: Animated Knots pages in sources): clove hitch (ABOK #1245) to tie the middle of a line along posts or a rail, or for a quick hold you will adjust, but it 'should be deeply distrusted when used by itself'; round turn and two half hitches (ABOK #1720) to tie the end of a line to a ring, bar, pole or post, even while it is pulling; cow hitch or lark's head (ABOK #1673) to fix a loop, lanyard, tag or strap to a ring, bar or zip pull; timber hitch (ABOK #1665) to drag, lift or start a lashing round a log, pole or bundle; cleat hitch (ABOK #1615) on a horn cleat; Palomar knot for slippery fishing line or braid to a hook, swivel or small ring (Animated Knots; Wikipedia).
  - Round turn first: the two passes of the round turn hold the strain while you tie, then add the half hitches, always 'in the same direction'; add one or two more turns for a heavy load (Animated Knots 'Round Turn & Two Half Hitches'). The anchor hitch (ABOK #1841) is so similar that it is 'doubtful if there is much benefit in using one knot over the other'; for anchors people add extra half hitches or seize the tail (Animated Knots 'Anchor Hitch').
  - Back up a clove hitch: once adjusted, secure it with two half hitches round the standing part, as when tying a boat fender to a rail (Animated Knots 'Clove Hitch').
  - Cow hitch: pull on both legs equally or it 'Can fail' (Wikipedia 'Cow hitch'); it is 'just as likely to slip as the Clove Hitch but less likely to bind' (Animated Knots).
  - Timber hitch, sources differ: Ashley says 'three tucks or turns are ample', but later sources suggest five or more turns in modern synthetic rope; add a half hitch further along (killick hitch) when towing or lifting long items (Wikipedia 'Timber hitch').
  - Cleat hitch: lead the line round the far horn first, then make figure-eight crossings; 'Two crossovers is the bare minimum'; don't take the first turn round under the first horn a second time or it may jam; leave off the locking half hitch on lines that may need releasing under load (Animated Knots 'Cleat Hitch').
  - Palomar: moisten it before tightening and trim the tail to about 3 mm (Wikipedia 'Palomar knot'); make the loop big enough to pass over the hook or lure (Animated Knots).
  - Rope strength with knots, side by side: no more than 50% of rated strength assumed (Animated Knots 'Safety'); about 60-70% for these hitches in a rough chart from Day (IGKT Solent); 35-68% lost in SAIL Magazine's 2008 tests. Design factor: 5:1 to 12:1 between breaking strength and working load, 12:1 for critical loads (Cordage Institute, quoted by Animated Knots 'Safety').
  - Spread the load in printed parts: tie abrasive string round a washer rather than straight into a hole in plastic (gm0 'Rigging').

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A boat fender tied to a rail with a clove hitch, and a mooring line made fast on a horn cleat (Animated Knots)
  - A luggage tag or a phone or camera lanyard attached with a lark's head (cow hitch) loop, or a cord loop on a zip pull (Wikipedia 'Cow hitch')
  - An animal's lead tied to a post or ring (Wikipedia 'Cow hitch'), or a line tied to a ring or dock post with a round turn and two half hitches (Animated Knots)
  - Strings on a classical guitar or ukulele bridge, held by timber hitches (Wikipedia 'Timber hitch')
- **when_to_use**: Use a hitch whenever a cord, string or rope has to be fixed to something solid and you have no hardware, or want to be able to untie it later: tying a line to a rail, post, ring or eye bolt; hanging things from a bar; fixing a lanyard loop to a ring or a printed eye; starting and finishing lashings round poles (the clove hitch 'is also commonly used in pioneering to start and finish a lashing', Wikipedia); lifting or dragging a log or a bundle of poles (timber hitch); tying off on a cleat; and tying fishing line, braid or thin string to a hook, ring or small eye (Palomar). In robots and printed mechanisms a hitch round a smooth post is a quick way to anchor a string, though clamping it under a bolt is easier to re-tension (gm0 'Rigging').
- **when_not_to_use**: Never for anything that holds a person (climbing, swings, rescue, lifting people): use rated equipment and trained methods. Not where the line must be pulled tight and adjusted: use Tensioning and adjustable hitches (trucker's hitch, taut-line hitch). Not where you need a fixed loop to drop over a hook or post: use Fixed loop knots (bowline, figure-eight loop). Not a clove hitch on its own for anything important, on a square post, or where the pull changes direction (Animated Knots; Wikipedia); back it up or use a round turn and two half hitches. Not a cow hitch with only one leg loaded (Wikipedia). Not to join two cords: use Bends for joining two cords. Not for steel wire rope: use Wire-rope terminations. Where the line must be released quickly under load or tied off often, a Cam cleat or Clam cleat is faster.
- **environment_limits**: Slippery modern fibres (Spectra, Dyneema, polypropylene) grip less: the Spectra-cored rope lost the most strength in SAIL Magazine's tests, 'With certain types of cord, the clove hitch can slip when loaded' (Wikipedia), and timber hitches in synthetic rope need five or more turns (Wikipedia). Repeated pulling and slackening (wind, waves, vibration) loosens knots: 'Many knots can loosen with prolonged intermittent strain' (Animated Knots 'Safety'), so back up hitches that will be left in place. Heavily loaded small cord can bind and become hard to untie (Wikipedia 'Clove hitch'). Printed posts and cleats: PLA 'is not ideal for sun, heat, or long-term outdoor tension'; use PETG, ASA, ABS or nylon outdoors (Printables 'Sun Sail Rope Cleat / Small Horn Cleat'). Wet natural-fibre rope swells and makes knots harder to untie ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **safety_notes**: Never trust a home-made knot to hold a person: climbing, rescue, swings and lifting people need rated equipment and training. 'Handle rope with care, inspect and test any knot you tie, and respect any rope subject to a heavy load'; 'Never stand in a loop or a bight of a rope which may suddenly tighten, e.g. an anchor line or tow line'; and remember that angles, shock loads and moving loads can cut a rope's real strength far below its rating (Animated Knots 'Safety'). A loaded line that slips or breaks whips back, so keep faces and hands clear when testing ⚑ [uncertain]. Use a round turn so you can hold a pulling load while tying, and keep fingers out from between the rope and the post or cleat when the line is under strain ⚑ [uncertain]. A printed cleat or post is light-duty hardware, not certified for marine, towing or life-safety use (Printables 'Sun Sail Rope Cleat / Small Horn Cleat'). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **common_failures**: Clove hitch slips or rolls off: it 'slips and, paradoxically, can also bind' (Animated Knots); it spills if pulled hard in the wrong direction and is unreliable on square posts (Wikipedia); back it up with two half hitches or use a round turn and two half hitches. Round turn and two half hitches rolls open under very high load before the rope breaks (SAIL Magazine 2008): add extra half hitches or seize the tail (Animated Knots 'Anchor Hitch'). Half hitches tied in opposite directions: always tie them the same way round (Animated Knots). Cow hitch slides when only one leg is loaded (Wikipedia). Timber hitch lets go when the pull stops or with too few turns in synthetic rope: add turns and a half hitch (Wikipedia). Cleat hitch jams: the first turn went round the same horn twice (Animated Knots). Palomar fails: tail cut too short or knot tightened dry (Wikipedia). Cord frays or is cut at a sharp printed edge: round the edges or tie round a washer (gm0). Knot works loose over time under intermittent load (Animated Knots 'Safety').
- **related**: 
  - Basic knots (overhand knot, half hitch, reef knot): the half hitch is the building block of these hitches
  - Tensioning and adjustable hitches (trucker's hitch, taut-line hitch, Prusik): when the line must be pulled tight, adjusted or grip another rope
  - Fixed loop knots (bowline, figure-eight loop, alpine butterfly): make a loop to drop over a post or clip to a ring instead of tying round it
  - Bends for joining two cords (sheet bend, double fisherman's knot, water knot): join two lines instead of tying one to an object
  - Pole lashings (square, diagonal, shear and tripod lashings): started and finished with clove hitches and timber hitches
  - Stopper knots and cord-end anchors: stop a cord pulling back through a hole instead of tying round something
  - Cam cleat and Clam cleat: hardware that holds a hauled line with no knot and releases with a flick
  - Eye bolts, screw eyes and hooks: the rings and eyes these hitches are often tied to
  - Spring-gate hook (carabiner, snap hook): clip on and off instead of tying
  - Rope splices (eye splice, short splice, braided-rope splice): a permanent eye that keeps more strength than a knot

## 379 Tensioning and adjustable hitches (trucker's hitch, taut-line hitch, Prusik)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Knots that let you pull a line tight and hold it, or slide a loop to adjust it, with no hardware, as in the trucker's hitch for lashing a load, the taut-line hitch for tent lines and the Prusik that grips another rope or a pole

*Also covers:* Also covers the rolling hitch and adjustable grip hitch, and why slippery modern cords defeat friction hitches. Safety: friction hitches can let go suddenly; climbing uses need training and rated gear.

### What it is

- **plain_name**: Knots that pull a rope or cord tight and keep it tight with no buckle or ratchet: a rope 'pulley' tied into the line itself for lashing down a load, a sliding loop on a tent line that you push along to tighten it, and a loop of cord wrapped round a rope or pole that grips when it is pulled and slides when you push the knot
- **term**: Tensioning and adjustable hitches: the trucker's hitch (a rope tackle tied in the line), the taut-line or midshipman's hitch (an adjustable loop made with a rolling hitch), the rolling hitch, the adjustable grip hitch, and the Prusik and its relatives (slide-and-grip friction hitches)
- **aliases**: 
  - trucker's hitch (ABOK #2124, p. 344, per Animated Knots; Wikipedia also lists #1514, #2125, #2126): also harvester's hitch, haymaker's hitch, lorry knot or lorry driver's hitch, power cinch, wagoner's hitch, dolly knot, hay knot, rope tackle (Animated Knots 'Trucker's Hitch'; Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch')
  - McCarthy hitch: Andrew Skurka's name for a simplified trucker's hitch on tarp guy lines, using the fixed loop at the tarp as the pulley (Skurka)
  - taut-line hitch, tautline hitch, tent-line hitch, tent hitch, rigger's hitch, adjustable hitch (ABOK #1856, the version taught by the Boy Scouts of America, and #1800; #1857 is the same with the last half hitch reversed) (Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch')
  - midshipman's hitch (ABOK #1855, p. 310): the more secure taut-line version, built on rolling hitch #1735; Animated Knots also lists it as 'Taut Line Hitch' (Animated Knots 'Midshipman's Hitch'; Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch')
  - awning hitch: the first two tucked turns of a rolling hitch, which already hold temporarily while tent lines are rigged (ABOK #1798, p. 304 in Animated Knots 'Rolling Hitch'; ABOK #1854, p. 310 in Animated Knots 'Midshipman's Hitch')
  - rolling hitch, Magnus hitch, Magner's hitch (ABOK #1734 to a pole or rod, #1735 to another rope, #1736 the Magnus hitch with the last hitch reversed) (Wikipedia 'Rolling hitch'; Animated Knots 'Rolling Hitch')
  - adjustable grip hitch, adjustable loop, Cawley adjustable hitch (no ABOK number; devised by Canadian climber Robert Chisnall in 1982) (Wikipedia 'Adjustable grip hitch')
  - Prusik, Prusik knot, Prusik hitch, triple sliding hitch, often misspelled Prussik (ABOK #1763, p. 300, shows the same structure for hoisting a spar) (Wikipedia 'Prusik'; Animated Knots 'Prusik Knot')
  - slide-and-grip knots, friction hitches, 'hold and release to slide' hitches: the family name (Animated Knots 'Slide & Grip Knots'; Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch', 'List of friction hitch knots')
  - other friction hitches that do the same jobs: Klemheist or French Machard (ABOK #1762), Blake's hitch, Distel hitch, autoblock (French Prusik), Bachmann hitch, icicle hitch, Farrimond friction hitch (Wikipedia 'List of friction hitch knots')
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: tensioning, reels & take-up, self-locking & anti-back-drive
- **one_liner**: Knots that tension a line and hold it with no hardware: the trucker's hitch ties a loop into the rope that works like a pulley so you can haul a lashing tight, while friction hitches such as the taut-line, rolling, adjustable grip and Prusik hitches wrap round a rope or pole, grip when the line is loaded and slide by hand when it is slack, so the line can be tightened or moved again later.
- **complexity**: Single piece for most of them: just the rope or cord (trucker's, taut-line, rolling and adjustable grip hitches, and Blake's hitch). A Prusik or Klemheist uses two pieces: a closed loop of thinner cord (the Prusik loop) wrapped round the main rope or a pole. Plus whatever the line goes round: a hook, rail, ring, peg, tree or pole.
- **how_it_works**: The trucker's hitch ties a loop into the standing part of the rope; the free end goes round a hook or rail and back through that loop, so the loop acts as a pulley and, in theory, every unit of pull on the end puts three units of tension into the rope over the load; rope rubbing on rope cuts this to something like 1.6:1, but the same friction helps hold the gain while you tie the end off with half hitches (Animated Knots 'Trucker's Hitch'; Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch'). Friction hitches work like a rope wrapped round a capstan: each wrap multiplies the grip, and the force needed to make a line slip grows exponentially with the total wrap angle and the friction between the surfaces (Wikipedia 'Capstan equation'). Pulling on the loaded end tightens the wraps and puts a slight kink (dog-leg) into the rope they grip, which locks them; pushing on the knot itself loosens the wraps so it can be slid (Animated Knots 'Rolling Hitch'; Wikipedia 'Prusik'). The taut-line hitch is a rolling hitch tied round the line's own standing part after it has gone round an anchor, so sliding the knot changes the size of the loop and therefore the line's length without retying (Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch').

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - tighten and hold tension: the trucker's hitch hauls a lashing tight with a built-in pulley effect, and the sliding hitches take up slack in tent lines, ridge lines and clotheslines and hold it
  - join and hold: ties a line to a hook, rail, peg or tree, or attaches a cord to another rope or a pole
  - stop it moving back (one-way): the rolling hitch is 'designed to resist lengthwise movement for only a single direction of pull' (Wikipedia 'Rolling hitch'), as are the Klemheist, Blake's and icicle hitches; a Prusik riding against a pulley acts as a rope ratchet (progress capture device) (Wikipedia 'Prusik')
  - lock, and release by hand: slide the knot by hand to adjust it; slipped finishes pull undone with one tug
- **motion**: slide: a friction hitch is meant to be slid along the rope or its own standing part by hand when it is slack, to change the loop size or its position; while a trucker's hitch is being tensioned its end slides through the loop. Once loaded and tied off, nothing should move, and slipping counts as failure.
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 while loaded and holding; 1 (sliding along the line) when slack and pushed by hand.
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds): the trucker's hitch multiplies your pull (ideally 3:1) and the taut-line hitch lets a line be re-tightened whenever it sags, 'without retying the knot' (Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch')
  - hand-release only (locks under load, releases when a person operates it): friction hitches hold when the line is loaded and slide only when the knot itself is pushed; 'When weight is removed, the loop can be moved along the rope by placing a hand directly on the barrel' (Wikipedia 'Prusik')
  - one-way (moves freely one way, locks the other): the rolling, Klemheist, Blake's and icicle hitches hold a pull in one direction only; the Prusik is symmetrical and grips both ways (Animated Knots 'Prusik Knot'; Wikipedia 'Prusik')
  - quick-release: the trucker's hitch is usually finished with a slipped half hitch, and the slipped adjustable grip hitch 'can be easily untied by pulling the end ... even under quite heavy load' (Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch', 'Adjustable grip hitch')
  - torque-limiting, loosely: an overloaded Prusik 'will initially slip', which rescuers treat as a warning or 'pressure relief device', but the slip point varies widely with cord, rope and age, so it is not a reliable set limit (Wikipedia 'Prusik'; Rhodes and others quoted by McCullar, USFA)
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): wraps of cord squeezed onto a rope, a pole or the line's own standing part, gripping harder the harder the line is pulled (Wikipedia 'Capstan equation', 'Prusik')
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the fixed loop that forms the trucker's hitch 'pulley' (e.g. an alpine butterfly or directional figure-eight loop) and the half hitches that tie it off (Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch')
- **input_output**: Trucker's hitch only: a hand pull on the free end -> tension in the rope over the load, in line. Ideal 3:1 when the lower anchor is fixed and you pull away from it (Animated Knots), but only 2:1 if the hitch is used to hoist a weight hanging from it (IGKT forum, quoting an earlier Wikipedia text); in practice 'may be more like 1.6:1' because rope runs over rope (Animated Knots), i.e. roughly 50% efficient [derived from Animated Knots' figures]. Once tied off it cannot run backwards (not back-drivable), and the friction 'helps hold the gain while the end is secured' (Animated Knots). Not applicable to the sliding friction hitches, which hold rather than pass motion on.
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable, and tool-free quick release when the finish is slipped: all of these untie by hand. A heavily loaded Prusik 'can be quite constricted and difficult to untie' (Wikipedia 'Prusik'), and an unslipped finishing half hitch on a trucker's hitch can jam under large loads (Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch').
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: the same rope or cord is tied and untied again and again, but a trucker's hitch tied repeatedly in the same spot wears the rope where the end runs through the loop (Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch', citing Riley 1912 and Pawson), and a friction hitch that has slid under load can be glazed or melted by friction heat (Frank, quoted by McCullar, USFA).
- **tools_needed**: none. A pointed spike (marlinspike or fid) helps to free a jammed hitch: Practical Sailor needed 'a marlinspike and 10 minutes of hard work' to free a sailor's hitch from Spectra line (Practical Sailor, Aug 2009).
- **seal**: none
- **load**: Light to medium, and only as a pull along the line. Knots weaken rope: assume even new rope gives 'no more than 50% of its rated breaking strength' (Animated Knots 'Safety'). Trucker's hitch: the rope over the load ideally gets 3 times your pull while the hook or rail it goes round is pulled with 2 times your pull (Wikimedia Commons diagram 'Truckers hitch pulley model'), but friction cuts the real gain to about 1.6 times (Animated Knots). Friction hitches hold until they slip, and the slip load depends on the cord: in Practical Sailor's 2009 test at jib-sheet loads (the hitch must hold 'about 2,000 pounds' on a 40-foot boat), the rolling hitch slipped on every material except 9/16 in Dacron double braid, while the icicle hitch held on all of them. Prusik figures for 8 mm nylon cord on 11-12.5 mm rescue rope, side by side: about 9 kN (Olson and Thorne), slipping at 7.0-9.5 kN (Smith), at 1,200 lbf, about 5.3 kN (Ray), or at 800-1,200 lb (Rhodes), all quoted by McCullar for USFA; McCullar's own tests averaged 14.1 kN on new 11 mm rope and 14.8 kN on new 12.5 mm rope, but only 10.9-11.2 kN with older ropes and cords, failing by stripping the rope's sheath or by the cord snapping, sometimes 'with little warning' (1 kN is about 100 kg-force). The adjustable grip hitch 'will hold fast when loaded, but slip when shock loaded' (Wikipedia). Weak against jerks, flapping and pulls at an angle to the gripped rope: 'If the tension is away from the standing rope or pole, this knot is likely to fail' (Animated Knots 'Rolling Hitch'). Never for holding people without training and rated gear.
- **adjustable**: yes: this is the point of them. A taut-line or midshipman's hitch is slid along the standing part to shorten or lengthen the loop: hold the standing part inside the loop, pull toward the anchor and slide the hitch away to take up slack, or slide it toward the anchor to loosen (Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch'). A Prusik or Klemheist is pushed along the rope when slack. A trucker's hitch can be re-tensioned by untying the half hitches and hauling again, or permanently adjustable if it is finished with a taut-line or Farrimond hitch (Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch'; Animated Knots: 'Finishing with a Rolling Hitch has the advantage that it facilitates adjustment').
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable (they don't locate parts). Note that friction hitches can creep: in three-strand nylon 'a Midshipman's Hitch may slide over time' (Animated Knots), and stretchy rope relaxes, which is why a taut-line hitch is used where a line 'will need to be periodically adjusted in order to maintain tension' (Wikipedia).
- **access_needed**: not applicable: you need a free end of the line (or a bight for slipped finishes) and room to pass it round the anchor. A Prusik loop can be put on the middle of a rope or round a pole without reaching either end of it.
- **materials**: Any rope or cord, but the friction hitches depend heavily on the rope: 'When rope was mostly tarred hemp, they all gripped. Those days are over, and many of these knots (all?) will fail with modern slippery ropes such as Spectra, and Dyneema (HMPE)' (Animated Knots 'Slide & Grip Knots'). HMPE fibres are 'slippery (very low coefficient of friction)' and melt at only 144-152 °C, and in HMPE rope 'usually trusted knots slip undone easily - at loads as low as 15% of the breaking strength' (Animated Knots 'Rope Materials'); Wikipedia says Dyneema/Spectra should not be used for Prusik loops unless made for it. With polypropylene too, assume the hitch 'will just slide' (Animated Knots 'Midshipman's Hitch'). Three-strand nylon is slippery enough to creep (Animated Knots). The Farrimond friction hitch 'holds fairly well in paracord' (Animated Knots). Grip is better when the gripping cord is thinner than the rope it grips and when the line is older and rougher: 'This is one of the few applications where old line is preferable to new' (Practical Sailor 2009; Wikipedia 'Prusik'). Prusik loops are made of 5-6 mm nylon accessory cord joined with a double fisherman's bend (Animated Knots; Wikipedia). Rolling hitch #1734 and the icicle hitch also grip poles, rails and tubes; the icicle hitch held even on greased stainless-steel tube (Practical Sailor).
- **maker_difficulty**: easy to moderate: no tools or parts are needed, but each hitch takes practice to tie and dress neatly, and every friction hitch has to be tested in the actual cord before it is trusted (Animated Knots 'Klemheist Knot': 'test it to see that it both grips and releases well').

### Making it

- **processes**: off-the-shelf part: rope, cord, paracord or accessory cord, tied by hand, 3D print (FDM): the hooks, rails, pegs, cleats and knot-practice boards that these hitches are tied to (see make_it_yourself)
- **print_in_place**: no: the knots are tied in cord; only the parts they pull on, or practice boards, are printed.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: The knots aren't printed, but they are often tied to printed parts. 1) A printed hook, ring or rail that a trucker's hitch goes round carries two legs of rope, so it is pulled with about twice the tension in the line (Wikimedia Commons diagram 'Truckers hitch pulley model': rope 3F, anchor 2F); size it for that, not for the line tension alone ⚑ [uncertain for the sizing margin]. 2) Print hooks and posts so the pull runs along the layers rather than prying them apart: 'Like wood, FFF prints are stronger in one direction than another' (Prusa Knowledge Base, 'Modeling with 3D printing in mind'). 3) Round and smooth every edge a loaded cord bends over; rope running under tension over a sharp printed edge chafes, and the trucker's hitch already wears the rope where it runs through its own loop (Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch') ⚑ [uncertain for printed edges specifically]. 4) A friction hitch tied round a smooth printed rod behaves like one round a smooth tube: use a hitch made for lengthwise pull on a pole (rolling hitch #1734 or the icicle hitch, which held on greased stainless tube in Practical Sailor's test) and test it before relying on it. 5) Makers use the Prusik as a grip: RepRap developers tied a Prusik round filament to measure an extruder's pulling force and the filament's tensile strength (Wikipedia 'Prusik'). 6) If a knot keeps slipping in thin, slick cord, a printed guy-line runner or cam cleat may be the better answer (see Guy-line slider). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Rope and cord sold by fibre, diameter and breaking strength (nylon, polyester, polypropylene, paracord, HMPE such as Dyneema (brand)); nylon accessory cord (5-6 mm) and sewn Prusik loops; tent pegs, guy-line cord, hooks, rails and cleats to tie to; a Prusik-minding pulley for hauling systems (Wikipedia 'Prusik'). Hardware that does the same jobs: guy-line sliders (e.g. Line-Lok (brand)), cam and clam cleats, cam buckles, ratchet tie-down straps and mechanical rope grabs. Choose rope with a breaking strength 5 to 12 times the load (Cordage Institute guideline quoted by Animated Knots 'Safety').
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Trucker's hitch gain, side by side: theoretical 3:1, 'In practice the mechanical advantage is much less, may be more like 1.6:1' (Animated Knots); 3:1 only when the lower anchor is fixed and you pull away from it, and 2:1 if you use it to lift a weight hanging from it (IGKT forum 'Trucker's hitch myths', quoting an earlier Wikipedia text); 'reduced substantially by friction when using knots' (Wikipedia).
  - Make the trucker's hitch loop so it can't close: use a fixed loop (alpine butterfly, directional figure-eight, bowline on a bight) and form it so the working end, not the standing end, passes through it: 'If the standing end goes through the loop, it will close under load' (Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch'). A sheepshank-style loop is quicker but 'can fall apart under too little load or too much load' (Wikipedia); a plain figure-eight loop 'tends to be hard to untie after heavy loads' (Animated Knots).
  - Hold the gain while tying off: pinch the rope where it passes through the loop, then tie two half hitches below the loop (Animated Knots); for one-pull release use a slipped half hitch (Skurka; Wikipedia), or finish with a taut-line or Farrimond hitch so the line can be re-tensioned without jamming (Wikipedia). Pulling the line sideways and taking in the slack ('sweating' the line) adds tension (Wikipedia).
  - Taut-line hitch choice: the midshipman's hitch (#1855) is the more secure; #1856 and #1857 adjust more easily, and #1857 twists less (Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch'; Ashley via Animated Knots). Snug the hitch firmly round the standing part, then 'Load slowly and adjust as necessary' (Wikipedia).
  - Pull along the line, never sideways: 'The "pull" MUST be in line with the main rope (or the pole)' (Animated Knots 'Rolling Hitch'). Never tie the adjustable grip hitch close to a tree or large object, as 'the knot may release' (Animated Knots).
  - In slippery rope add turns: an extra initial wrap and/or an extra finishing half hitch (Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch'; Animated Knots 'Midshipman's Hitch'); three wraps instead of two for the adjustable grip hitch (Animated Knots); Blake's hitch goes from 4/2 to 5/2 turns in slippery rope (Prohaska via Wikipedia 'Blake's hitch'). With Spectra, Dyneema or polypropylene assume a rolling hitch 'will not hold at all' (Animated Knots).
  - Gripping line should be thinner than the line it grips (Practical Sailor 2009); 'the greater the difference between the diameter of the cord used for the hitch and the main line, the greater the ability for the hitch to hold', but thinner cord has a lower safe load and jams more (Wikipedia 'Prusik').
  - Prusik: two to four wraps 'depending on the materials' (Wikipedia), three as standard (Animated Knots); webbing Prusiks are not recommended for heavier loads because they have been shown to slip (Wikipedia, citing Freedom of the Hills).
  - Test first: 'before using any Slide and Grip knot, test it to see that it both grips and releases well', changing the number of turns to suit the ropes (Animated Knots 'Klemheist Knot').
  - Aircraft tie-down (Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch'): one taut-line hitch 15-30 cm from the aircraft, adjusted for tension, then a second one 5-20 cm further away finished with a half hitch.

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Tent guy lines tightened with a taut-line hitch, 'a common use' (Wikipedia 'Taut-line hitch'; Practical Sailor: 'Every Boy Scout uses this hitch to secure tent guy lines to tent pegs')
  - A rope lashing a load or tarpaulin onto a trailer, truck or roof rack with a trucker's hitch (Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch'; Animated Knots)
  - A tarp or hammock ridge line with a Prusik or Farrimond hitch to position and tension the tarp (Wikipedia 'Prusik'; Animated Knots 'Farrimond Friction Hitch')
  - An adjustable clothesline loop made with a midshipman's (rolling) hitch (Animated Knots 'Rolling Hitch')
- **when_to_use**: Use these when a rope or cord has to be pulled tight and kept tight with no hardware, or re-tightened later: lashing a load or tarp onto a trailer or roof rack (trucker's hitch), tensioning tent guy lines, tarp ridge lines and clotheslines that sag as the fabric and rope stretch (taut-line, midshipman's, adjustable grip and Farrimond hitches), and attaching a cord to a rope, pole or tube so it can be slid along and then hold a lengthwise pull (rolling, icicle and Prusik hitches) (Wikipedia; Animated Knots). They are useful for prototypes, camping kit and garden or workshop jobs where a buckle or ratchet would be lost, broken or overkill, and the trucker's hitch gives a pulley-like pull with nothing but the rope (Skurka; Animated Knots).
- **when_not_to_use**: Not for climbing, rescue, tree work or anything that holds a person without proper training and rated gear: the Prusik and its relatives are life-safety tools only in trained hands (Wikipedia 'Prusik'; Animated Knots 'Safety'). Not in slippery cord (Dyneema, Spectra, polypropylene, some thin guy-line cords): use a splice, a stitched knot or a cleat or line-lock made for that cord (Animated Knots 'Rope Materials'). Not where the load jerks, flaps or shock-loads the line, or pulls at an angle to the rope the hitch grips (Wikipedia 'Adjustable grip hitch'; Animated Knots 'Rolling Hitch'). For road loads, cargo or anything heavy, use rated ratchet or cam-buckle straps; for fine, lasting tension in wire or rod, a turnbuckle; for one-handed quick adjustment on thin cord, a guy-line slider or cord lock; for repeated hauling, a block and tackle with a mechanical rope grab.
- **environment_limits**: Wet, frozen, icy or muddy rope reduces friction: 'Prusiks are ineffective upon frozen wet ropes' (Wikipedia 'Prusik'), and 'may not hold in icy or muddy conditions' (Hudson and Vines, quoted by McCullar, USFA). Nylon rope is weakened 10-20% by soaking (reversibly), stretches about 10% at 30% of breaking load and 'exhibits some creep under high loads', so lines slacken and need re-tensioning; polyester (Dacron) stretches less and creeps negligibly (Animated Knots 'Rope Materials'). Sunlight: polypropylene 'is degraded by ultra-violet light' (Animated Knots 'Rope Materials'). Heat: friction from a hitch sliding under load heats the cord; nylon can melt and bond (Frank, quoted by McCullar, USFA), and HMPE melts at only 144-152 °C (Animated Knots). Repeated loading, wind and vibration shake knots loose: 'Many knots can loosen with prolonged intermittent strain' (Animated Knots 'Safety').
- **safety_notes**: Friction hitches can let go suddenly: in slippery cord, under a jerk, or if the knot itself is grabbed while loaded. Animated Knots warns that gripping the turns of a loaded Klemheist 'may be dangerous and will generate heat', and that holding and pulling directly on a Blake's hitch 'loosens the knot and allows descent - unexpected and uncontrolled'. In USFA rescue tests some Prusik failures 'occurred with little warning and would result in the load falling to the ground' (McCullar). Climbing, tree-climbing and rescue uses need training and rated gear; never trust a home-made knot, hitch or splice to hold a person (Animated Knots 'Safety'). A rope hauled tight with a trucker's hitch stores energy and can whip back if it or its anchor breaks; nylon in particular recoils with enough energy 'to maim or kill' (Animated Knots 'Rope Materials'). Never stand in a loop or bight that may suddenly tighten, and never wrap the line round your hand for grip (Animated Knots 'Safety').
- **common_failures**: Slipping or creeping: the hitch slides along the rope because the cord is too slick, too thick or too new, has too few turns, or wasn't snugged before loading; 'If the hitch is not made very snug before applying any strain, it will not tighten further under load' (Wikipedia 'Rolling hitch'); fix with more wraps, thinner or rougher gripping cord, or a better-gripping hitch such as the icicle hitch (Practical Sailor 2009). Pulling at an angle: a rolling hitch loaded away from the line it grips 'is likely to fail' (Animated Knots). Shock-loading: the adjustable grip hitch slips when jerked (Wikipedia). Trucker's hitch problems: the loop closes up because it was made the wrong way round, a sheepshank-style loop falls apart or capsizes, the finishing half hitch jams under load, or the rope wears where the end runs through the loop (Wikipedia 'Trucker's hitch'). Jamming: a heavily loaded Prusik becomes 'quite constricted and difficult to untie' (Wikipedia 'Prusik'), and the sailor's hitch jammed badly on Spectra (Practical Sailor). Heat damage: a hitch that slides under load glazes or melts the cord (McCullar, USFA). Twisting: the taut-line hitch can twist the line under load; version #1857 has 'less tendency to twist' (Wikipedia).
- **related**: 
  - Guy-line slider (friction line tensioner): a small plate or toggle that does the taut-line hitch's job on thin cord, quicker to adjust but one more part to lose
  - Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye (clove hitch, round turn and two half hitches): tie a line to something solid but don't tension or slide
  - Fixed loop knots (bowline, figure-eight loop, alpine butterfly): make the fixed loop that forms the trucker's hitch's pulley
  - Basic knots (overhand, half hitch, reef knot): the half hitches that finish these hitches; the reef knot must never be used to join ropes
  - Bends for joining two cords: the double fisherman's bend closes a cord into a Prusik loop
  - Cam cleat and Clam cleat: hold a hauled rope with teeth or a grooved V, released with one flick
  - Rope grab and ascender (cam rope clamp): a mechanical, rated version of the Prusik's grip; faster but heavier, and toothed cams can strip a rope's sheath
  - Pulley system (block and tackle): real pulleys give close to the ideal gain that the trucker's hitch loses to friction
  - Ratchet tie-down strap and Cam buckle: rated strap hardware for road loads and anything heavy
  - Turnbuckle: screw-thread tensioning for wire, rod or rope that holds for years
  - Cord lock (spring toggle): pinches a cord at any point; for drawstrings rather than tensioned lines
  - Capstan and windlass: the same wrap-friction effect used to hold or haul a heavily loaded rope
