## 382 Fixed loop knots (bowline, figure-eight loop, alpine butterfly)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Knots that make a loop that won't tighten up, at the end of a line or in its middle, to drop over a hook or post or to clip into

*Also covers:* Also covers why a bowline can shake loose when unloaded, and the alpine butterfly as the safe way to isolate a damaged section. Safety: for anything that holds a person, use a rated system

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A loop tied in a rope or cord that stays the same size when you pull on it, either at the end of the line or anywhere along its middle, so you can drop it over a hook, post or peg, or clip a carabiner or snap hook into it
- **term**: Fixed loop knots (eye knots): the bowline, the figure-eight loop and the alpine butterfly loop, plus the more secure bowline variants
- **aliases**: 
  - bowline, boling knot (archaic), 'king of knots' (ABOK #1010; Wikipedia also lists #1716) (Animated Knots 'Bowline'; Wikipedia 'Bowline')
  - figure-eight loop, figure-eight on a bight, Flemish loop, Flemish eight (ABOK #1047; Wikipedia also lists #531) (Wikipedia 'Figure-eight loop')
  - figure-eight follow-through, figure-eight retrace, rethreaded figure eight: the same loop tied round a closed ring or harness (Wikipedia 'Figure-eight loop'; Animated Knots)
  - alpine butterfly loop, butterfly loop, butterfly knot, lineman's loop, lineman's rider (ABOK #1053; Ashley also shows it as the harness loop, #532) (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Butterfly loop')
  - double bowline, round turn bowline (ABOK #1013) (Wikipedia 'Double bowline'; Animated Knots 'Water Bowline')
  - water bowline: a bowline with an extra half hitch forming a clove hitch (ABOK #1012) (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Water bowline')
  - Yosemite bowline, bowline with a Yosemite finish or tie-off (Wikipedia 'Yosemite bowline'; Animated Knots 'Bowline')
  - left-hand bowline, cowboy bowline, Dutch bowline (ABOK #1034½) (Animated Knots 'Bowline'; Wikipedia 'Bowline'; Šimon et al. 2020)
  - Eskimo bowline, Cossack knot; its slipped form is the Kalmyk loop (Wikipedia 'Bowline'; Animated Knots 'Bowline')
  - bowline on a bight: a two-loop bowline tied in the middle of a rope (ABOK #1080) (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Bowline on a bight')
  - directional figure eight, inline figure-eight loop (ABOK #1058) (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Directional figure eight')
  - double figure-eight loop, bunny ears, dog-eared loop (ABOK #1085) (Animated Knots 'Figure 8 Double Loop')
  - farmer's loop (ABOK #1054) and double dragon loop (not in ABOK): other mid-line and end loops (Animated Knots)
  - eye: the proper name for the fixed loop a knot or splice makes; if it slipped it would be a noose (Wikipedia 'List of knot terminology')
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware, quick-release & tool-free
- **one_liner**: Knots that make a loop that won't shrink or slip, at the end of a rope (bowline, figure-eight loop) or in its middle (alpine butterfly), to hook, clip or drop over something, and that untie by hand afterwards.
- **complexity**: Single piece: just the rope or cord itself, plus the hook, post, ring or clip the loop goes onto. A backup knot (stopper) is often tied in the loose end (tail).
- **how_it_works**: Each knot pinches the rope against itself so the loop (the eye) can't slide smaller; a loop that slips is a noose, not an eye (Wikipedia 'List of knot terminology'). The bowline is a small loop (nipping turn) in the long part of the rope (standing part) with the tail passed up through it, round the standing part and back down (Animated Knots 'Bowline'); it 'tightens when loaded at (pulled by) the standing part' (Wikipedia 'Bowline'), so with no load nothing squeezes the tail, which is why 'With no load it can be untied easily' and also why, unloaded, it 'can very readily work its way untied' (Animated Knots). The figure-eight loop is a figure-eight knot tied in doubled rope, either on a bight or retraced round a ring, giving two parallel strands that are easy to check (Animated Knots 'Figure 8 Follow Through Loop'). The alpine butterfly is twisted into a bight in the middle of the rope, so it can be pulled 'from the loop to either end of the rope; between the two ends with the loop hanging free; or to the loop with the load spread between the two ends' (Animated Knots 'Alpine Butterfly Loop').

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: makes a fixed attachment point (an eye) in a rope to hook, clip or drop over a post, or to link to another loop
  - lock, and release by hand: unties by hand with no tools; the bowline and alpine butterfly stay easy to undo even after a heavy load
- **motion**: none: the loop keeps its size and the knot should not move; the rope on either side stays flexible and the loop can swing on its hook.
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 within the knot. A loop on a hook, ring or carabiner can still pivot and swing about it, like a simple pin joint.
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - none: the useful property is simply that the loop is fixed and does not tighten (an eye rather than a noose) (Wikipedia 'List of knot terminology'). Note that the directional figure eight is made to be pulled one way only: 'a strain from the wrong end actually capsizes the knot into one that slides, i.e., it functions as a noose' (Animated Knots 'Figure 8 Directional Loop')
  - quick-release: only in slipped forms, such as the Kalmyk loop (a slipped Eskimo bowline); a slipped knot is less secure but releases more easily (Animated Knots 'Bowline', 'Terminology')
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): turns of rope squeeze the tail and standing part together when loaded (Wikipedia 'Knot')
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the closed loop surrounds the hook, post, ring or carabiner it hangs on
- **input_output**: not applicable
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable: unties by hand. The bowline 'With no load ... can be untied easily' but 'cannot be tied, or untied, when there is a load on the standing end' (Animated Knots 'Bowline'); the alpine butterfly 'remains reasonably easy to undo' after a heavy load (Animated Knots); the figure-eight loop 'can become difficult to untie after heavy loading, and can jam badly' (Wikipedia 'Figure-eight loop'), and the directional figure eight 'can be difficult to undo after a shock load' (Animated Knots).
- **reuse_cycles**: effectively unlimited: the same rope can be tied and untied many times; worn, chafed or shock-loaded rope is the limit, not the knot ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **tools_needed**: none. A fid (a tapered spike) can help loosen a jammed figure-eight loop, and a knife or hot cutter cuts and seals synthetic cord (Wikipedia 'Knot', Tools).
- **seal**: none
- **load**: Medium for maker uses, limited by the rope: a loop knot keeps only part of the rope's strength, and sources give different figures, side by side. Bowline: 'approximately 2/3 of its strength' (Wikipedia 'Bowline'); double bowline 70-75% (Wikipedia 'Double bowline'). CMC Rescue tests on 12.5 mm rescue rope (McKently, ITRS 2014): bowline 67% (1987, nylon), 75% (2008, nylon), 58% (2014, polyester); figure-eight loop 80%, 77%, 70%; alpine butterfly 77% and 61% loaded loop-to-end, 66% and 60% end-to-end; directional (inline) figure eight 73% and 63% loop-to-end but only 51% and 49% end-to-end; Hudson and Vines, quoted in the same paper: bowline 73-74%, figure eight 78-80%. Šimon, Dekýš and Palček (2020) on 10.5 mm static rope: figure-eight loop about 64%, bowline on a bight 61%, left-hand bowline 60%, bowline 58.5%; pulled sideways (cross-loaded), alpine butterfly 54.6% against 44.1% for the figure-eight loop. CMC's 2023 tests on thinner ropes led them to recommend assuming 50% 'until further testing proves otherwise' (CMC Pro), and Animated Knots says to assume even new rope gives 'no more than 50% of its rated breaking strength'. Direction matters: strongest pulled along the standing part with the loop loaded; weakest when the loop is pulled apart from inside (ring-loading), where bowlines untied rather than broke, the standard bowline at about 1.95 kN and the left-hand bowline at about 10.8 kN (Šimon et al. 2020). Never for holding people without training and a rated system.
- **adjustable**: no: the loop size is fixed once tied; to change it, untie and re-tie. Double-loop knots (double figure-eight loop, bowline on a bight) let you set the two loop sizes while tying (Šimon et al. 2020). For a loop that slides to adjust tension, see Tensioning and adjustable hitches.
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable: it doesn't locate parts precisely. The knot settles and the rope stretches when first loaded, so dress (neaten) and tighten it before use to limit slipping (Wikipedia 'Knot', Slipping).
- **access_needed**: not applicable: no panel access is involved. The bowline and figure-eight follow-through need a free end (the follow-through can be tied round a closed ring or post); the alpine butterfly, figure-eight on a bight, directional figure eight and bowline on a bight can be tied in the middle of a rope without using either end (Wikipedia 'Butterfly loop', 'Figure-eight loop'; Animated Knots 'Mid Loop Knots').
- **materials**: Any rope, cord, paracord or string; natural fibres, nylon and polyester grip best. The bowline 'when tied in certain materials or loading conditions ... may not hold' (Wikipedia 'Bowline'); for slippery high-modulus polyethylene (HMPE, e.g. Dyneema (brand)) the double dragon loop 'works with high modulus and slippery ropes such as Dyneema' (Animated Knots 'Double Dragon Loop'). The alpine butterfly 'works best with softer ropes' (Wikipedia 'Butterfly loop'). Manuals recommend the figure-eight loop in rope of at least 9 mm because it is hard to untie after heavy loading (Šimon et al. 2020). Flat webbing uses an overhand loop instead (65% in 1-inch tubular nylon webbing, CMC 1987 test quoted by McKently 2014). Fishing line has its own loops (perfection loop, surgeon's loop, Bimini twist) (Animated Knots 'End Loop Knots'), and hollow-braid rope can be spliced into an eye instead.
- **maker_difficulty**: easy: no tools or parts. The hard part is tying them correctly every time (a bowline 'is too easily tied incorrectly', Animated Knots) and choosing the right knot for the direction of pull.

### Making it

- **processes**: off-the-shelf part: rope or cord, tied by hand, 3D print (FDM): hooks, posts, rings and knot-practice boards that the loop goes onto (see make_it_yourself)
- **print_in_place**: no: knots are tied in cord; only the parts the loop goes onto, or practice boards, are printed.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Loops aren't printed, but they are often dropped over printed hooks and posts or clipped to printed rings. 1) Give a printed hook or post a smooth, generously rounded seat: rope is weakest where it bends tightly, and tests show thicker ropes, which bend more gently in a knot, keep more of their strength (CMC 2023: average 54%, 60% and 65% in 9.5, 11 and 12.5 mm rope) ⚑ [uncertain as a hook-sizing rule]. 2) Don't stretch a loop wide over a broad post or two hooks: a bowline pulled apart from inside (ring-loaded) can spill at low force (Šimon et al. 2020); if the loop must sit wide, the Eskimo bowline 'is preferred when the loop will be stretched wide' (Animated Knots 'Bowline'). 3) Printed hooks, rings and thimbles are for light loads only; one printed rope-ring designer notes such rings 'are for light rigging and organization, not life-safety loads' (Printables 1772939). 4) Orient a printed hook so the pull runs along its layers rather than trying to peel them apart ⚑ [uncertain]. 5) Practise on a printed ring-and-cleat trainer or knot board before relying on a knot (Printables 588348, 979503). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Rope and cord sold by fibre, diameter and breaking strength (braided nylon or polyester cord, paracord, accessory cord, static kernmantle rope); hooks, eye bolts, rings, cleats and posts to drop a loop over; spring-gate hooks (carabiners, snap hooks) to clip into it; rope thimbles to protect the inside of a loop from wear. Where it really matters, use rated parts instead: factory-sewn slings or spliced eyes. Choose rope by its breaking strength divided by a design factor of 5:1 to 12:1 (Cordage Institute CI 1401-15, quoted by Animated Knots 'Safety').
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Choose by where the loop goes and which way it is pulled: end of the line and easy to untie, a bowline (ABOK #1010) with a backup; end of the line, most secure and easiest to check, a figure-eight loop or follow-through (ABOK #1047); middle of the line, or pulled in several directions, an alpine butterfly (ABOK #1053); directional figure eight (ABOK #1058) only when the pull always comes from one known end, otherwise 'the Alpine Butterfly Loop is preferred' (Animated Knots 'Bowline', 'Alpine Butterfly Loop', 'Figure 8 Directional Loop').
  - Back up a bowline: when climbing 'A Safety Knot is essential', e.g. a double overhand (strangle knot) round the loop or the standing end, or a Yosemite tie-off; tighten the bowline first and then the tie-off, or it can become a slip knot (Animated Knots 'Bowline'). Because a badly dressed Yosemite bowline can collapse into a noose, some advise a standard or double bowline with a double overhand backup instead (Wikipedia 'Yosemite bowline').
  - Tail length, side by side: bowline tail 12 times the rope's circumference by a rule some texts quote, though this 'is rarely seen in practice' (Animated Knots 'Bowline'); figure-eight loop without a backup knot, 4 to 8 inches (about 10-20 cm) (Wikipedia 'Figure-eight loop'); Yosemite bowline, at least 10 cm, more in thick rope (Wikipedia 'Yosemite bowline').
  - Dress it before loading: the figure-eight loop should show two strands side by side all the way round, with the outer turns snugged in; 'Dressed this way the knot withstands a load better' (Animated Knots 'Figure 8 Follow Through Loop'). Crossed strands in a figure-eight loop lose strength (Wikipedia 'Knot').
  - Strength for planning: assume about 50% of the rope's rated strength (CMC 2023; Animated Knots 'Safety'); measured values range from about 44% (figure-eight loop pulled sideways) to 82% depending on knot, rope and direction (see load) (McKently 2014; Šimon et al. 2020).
  - Pulled sideways (cross-loaded), the figure-eight loop loses about 20% of its efficiency; for a mid-line loop pulled across the rope the alpine butterfly did about 10.5 percentage points better (Šimon et al. 2020).
  - To bypass a damaged section of rope, tie an alpine butterfly with the damaged part inside the loop, which then carries no load; it is 'far safer than The Sheep Shank' (Animated Knots 'Alpine Butterfly Loop'; Wikipedia 'Butterfly loop'). Animated Knots says the sheepshank 'should be avoided'.
  - On a bowline on a bight, clip through both loops: 2011 testing showed it 'might slip when only one loop is loaded' (Wikipedia 'Bowline on a bight').
  - Design factor: rope breaking strength 5 to 12 times the load, 12:1 for live or critical loads (Cordage Institute CI 1401-15, quoted by Animated Knots 'Safety').

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A mooring line made fast to a ring or post with a bowline (Animated Knots 'Bowline')
  - Climbers at an indoor wall tying the rope to their harness with a figure-eight follow-through, the standard method (Wikipedia 'Figure-eight loop')
  - Light aircraft tied down with bowlines, as the US Federal Aviation Administration recommends (Wikipedia 'Bowline')
  - A line of hikers clipping along one shared rope, or the first loop of a trucker's hitch for tying down a load, both made with an alpine butterfly (Animated Knots 'Alpine Butterfly Loop')
- **when_to_use**: When you need a loop that won't close up: to drop over a hook, post, peg or cleat, to clip a carabiner or snap hook into, to tie a line to a ring or eye, or to link two ropes loop-to-loop (two bowlines can be linked to join two ropes, Animated Knots 'Bowline'). Pick the bowline for an end loop you will want to untie easily after a heavy load, such as a mooring or tie-down line; the figure-eight loop where security and easy checking matter more than easy untying; and the alpine butterfly when you need a loop in the middle of a line (hand loops along a rope, the first loop of a trucker's hitch, a tie-in point between the ends) or a way to put a damaged section out of action (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Butterfly loop'). Good for prototypes, hanging things from printed hooks and temporary rigging where no hardware is wanted.
- **when_not_to_use**: Never for climbing, rescue, swings, hammocks, lifting or anything that holds a person without training and a rated system; for anything that holds a person, use rated gear. Don't use a bowline where the line may have to be released under load, because it 'cannot be tied, or untied, when there is a load on the standing end' (Animated Knots 'Bowline'): use a round turn and two half hitches, which 'can be tied and untied under load' (Animated Knots 'Safety'), see Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye. Don't use a plain bowline on a line that will flap or go slack repeatedly, or in slippery Dyneema: use a water bowline, double bowline with a backup, figure-eight loop or double dragon loop. Don't load a directional figure eight from the wrong end, and don't use a figure-eight loop where it must be untied easily after big loads. For a loop that should slide to tension a line, use Tensioning and adjustable hitches; for a permanent loop at nearly full strength, an eye splice (Rope splices); for steel cable, a thimble and clips or sleeves (Wire-rope terminations). Never use a sheepshank to shorten a rope or bypass damage (Animated Knots 'Sheepshank').
- **environment_limits**: Repeated loading and slackening, flapping and shaking: the bowline 'Shakes Undone If Not Loaded' (Animated Knots) and 'tends to spontaneously loosen under cyclic loading' (Šimon et al. 2020), whereas the figure-eight loop 'does not tend to untie under cyclic loading' and the alpine butterfly 'does not tend to disintegrate under cyclic loading' (Šimon et al. 2020). Water: nylon 'absorbs water and is 10-15% weaker when wet', while polyester 'is not affected by water' (Wikipedia 'Rope'); after an hour in tap water CMC measured bowline 67% (nylon) and 58% (polyester), figure-eight loop 91% and 68% (McKently 2014); the water bowline was devised for wet use where other knots may slip or jam (Wikipedia 'Water bowline'). Sunlight: some synthetic ropes 'can be damaged more easily by UV light' (Wikipedia 'Rope'). Slippery modern fibres (HMPE) make many knots slip; choose a knot that suits them (Animated Knots 'Double Dragon Loop').
- **safety_notes**: A home-made knot must never hold a person: for anything that holds a person, use a rated system and get training. Researchers no longer recommend the bowline 'in life-critical applications' (Šimon et al. 2020), and Animated Knots warns that it 'should be used with great care when climbing ... A Safety Knot is essential'. Inspect and stress-test every knot, 'but not in the direction of fall!' (Animated Knots 'Bowline'). Never stand in, or put a hand or limb in, a loop or bight that may suddenly tighten, and don't wrap rope round your hand (Animated Knots 'Safety'). Use a design factor of 12:1 for live or critical loads, and remember a rope pulled sideways (as when rigging between two points) or jerked by a falling load carries far more than the load's weight (Animated Knots 'Safety').
- **common_failures**: Shaking undone: an unloaded bowline works loose, as when Grog's bowline 'floated undone' and lost a scrubbing brush (Animated Knots 'Safety'); prevent it with a backup knot, a Yosemite tie-off, a water bowline or a more secure knot. Wrong tying: the bowline is easily mis-tied; a mis-tied alpine butterfly gives a 'false butterfly' that 'is prone to slipping' (Wikipedia 'Butterfly loop'); leaving the tail outside the first loop of a directional figure eight ties a different knot (ABOK #1057) (Animated Knots). Spilling under ring-load or sideways pull: bowlines untied at low force when the loop was pulled apart (Šimon et al. 2020) and can 'slip when pulled sideways' (Wikipedia 'Bowline'). Capsizing into a noose: the directional figure eight pulled from the wrong end, or a badly dressed Yosemite bowline (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Yosemite bowline'). Jamming: the figure-eight loop after heavy loading (Wikipedia 'Figure-eight loop'). Slipping of a bowline on a bight with only one loop loaded (Wikipedia 'Bowline on a bight'). Tails too short to stay in the knot. Breaking at the knot well below the rope's rated strength (Wikipedia 'Knot'). The sheepshank failing under modest load in nylon (Animated Knots 'Sheepshank').
- **related**: 
  - Stopper knots and cord-end anchors: the double overhand and figure-eight knots used as a backup in a loop's tail
  - Basic knots (overhand knot, half hitch, reef knot): the overhand loop is the simplest fixed loop, but it jams
  - Bends for joining two cords: every bend has matching loop knots; the sheet bend has the same structure as the bowline, and the alpine butterfly bend is tied like the alpine butterfly loop
  - Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye: tie straight onto the object instead of making a loop to drop over it; the round turn and two half hitches can be tied and untied under load
  - Tensioning and adjustable hitches (trucker's hitch, taut-line hitch, Prusik): loops that slide to tension a line; an alpine butterfly can start a trucker's hitch
  - Rope splices (eye splice, short splice, braided-rope splice): a permanent eye that keeps nearly all of the rope's strength
  - Spring-gate hook (carabiner, snap hook, lobster clasp): the usual hardware clipped into a fixed loop
  - Eye bolts, screw eyes and hooks: what a loop is dropped over or tied to
  - Wire-rope terminations (wire-rope clip, thimble, crimped sleeve): the rated way to make an eye in steel cable
  - Binding knots and seizings: a seizing can lash a loop's tail down semi-permanently for extra security

  - [Alpine Butterfly Loop (Animated Knots by Grog)](https://www.animatedknots.com/alpine-butterfly-loop-knot): Hand-wrapping animation and video for tying a loop in the middle of a rope, with notes on loading directions and isolating a damaged section *(licence: link only)*
  - [Figure 8 Follow Through Loop (Animated Knots by Grog)](https://www.animatedknots.com/figure-8-follow-through-loop-knot): Animation of the figure-eight loop retraced round a ring, with photos of the stopper backup, final dressing, the figure eight on a bight and the directional figure eight *(licence: link only)*
  - [Palstek innen.jpg (Wikimedia Commons, by Markus Bärlocher)](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Palstek_innen.jpg): Clear photo of a bowline tied in rope, with the tail inside the loop *(licence: Public domain)*

## 383 Bends for joining two cords (sheet bend, double fisherman's knot, water knot)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Knots that join the ends of two ropes, cords or flat tapes, including ones of different thickness

*Also covers:* Also covers the double sheet bend, the Zeppelin bend, closing a cord into a loop, netting knots, and leaving long tails. Never the reef knot (see Basic knots)

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A knot that ties the ends of two ropes, cords, strings or flat straps together so they work as one longer line, even a thick cord to a thin one, or that ties the two ends of one cord together to close it into a ring
- **term**: Bends (end-to-end joining knots): the sheet bend and double sheet bend, the double fisherman's knot (grapevine bend), the water knot (tape knot) for webbing, and the Zeppelin bend
- **aliases**: 
  - bend: the general name for any knot that joins two lines (Animated Knots 'Terminology'; Wikipedia 'Bend (knot)')
  - sheet bend, weaver's knot, weaver's hitch (ABOK #1431; weaver's knot #485 per Animated Knots; Wikipedia also lists #1, #66 and #2) (Animated Knots 'Sheet Bend'; Wikipedia 'Sheet bend')
  - double sheet bend: the smaller cord taken twice round the bight (ABOK #1434, also #488) (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend')
  - becket hitch: the same knot tied to a rope handle or eye (a becket) instead of another rope (Animated Knots 'Sheet Bend')
  - netting knot or mesh knot: the sheet bend as tied with a netting needle in net making (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend', 'Net (device)')
  - double fisherman's knot, double fisherman's bend, grapevine bend (ABOK #294; Wikipedia also lists #1415, #498); triple fisherman's knot for slippery fibres (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot')
  - fisherman's knot (single), angler's knot, waterman's knot, Englishman's knot, true lover's knot (ABOK #293, #1414) (Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot')
  - water knot, tape knot, ring bend, grass knot, overhand follow-through (ABOK #296; Wikipedia also lists #1412, #343) (Animated Knots 'Water Knot'; Wikipedia 'Water knot')
  - Zeppelin bend, Rosendahl bend (not in the 1944 ABOK) (Wikipedia 'Zeppelin bend')
  - alpine butterfly bend, butterfly bend: the bend version of the alpine butterfly loop (ABOK #1053) (Animated Knots)
  - figure-eight bend, Flemish bend, rewoven figure 8 bend (ABOK #1411) (Animated Knots 'Figure 8 Bend')
  - carrick bend (ABOK #1439), used for hawsers, heavy rope and nets (Animated Knots 'Carrick Bend')
  - Hunter's bend or rigger's bend (#1425A) and Ashley bend (ABOK #1452): similar interlocked-overhand bends that tend to jam (Animated Knots)
  - offset overhand bend, flat overhand bend, 'European death knot' (EDK), offset water knot, thumb knot (ABOK #1410) (Animated Knots 'Flat Overhand Bend'; Wikipedia 'Offset overhand bend')
  - blood knot (ABOK #295) and Albright knot: fishing-line bends (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Bend (knot)')
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **one_liner**: Knots that join two cords end to end, or close one cord into a ring: the sheet bend for cords of different thickness, the double fisherman's for a compact, very secure join, the water knot for flat tape, and the Zeppelin bend when it must still untie after a heavy pull.
- **complexity**: Single piece: the two cord ends (or both ends of one cord) and nothing else. A backup knot is often tied in each loose end (tail) for extra security.
- **how_it_works**: Pulling on the two long parts (standing parts) squeezes each cord end in the grip of the other, so the ends can't slide out. In the sheet bend the thicker cord is folded into a U (bight) and the thinner one passes through it, round behind both legs and tucks under itself (Animated Knots 'Sheet Bend'); it has the same structure as a bowline (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend'). The double fisherman's is 'two strangle knots (like double overhand knots) each tied round the other standing end', which slide together and lock (Animated Knots 'Double Fisherman's Bend'). The water knot is an overhand knot tied in one strap and retraced in the reverse direction by the other (Animated Knots 'Water Knot'). The Zeppelin bend is 'two symmetrically interlinked overhand knots', which makes it 'stable, secure, and highly resistant to jamming' (Wikipedia 'Zeppelin bend').

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: joins two cords, ropes, threads or straps end to end, or closes one cord into a loop (for example a Prusik loop or a sling)
  - lock, and release by hand: the sheet bend, Zeppelin bend, alpine butterfly bend and carrick bend untie by hand after loading; the double fisherman's and water knot jam and are best treated as semi-permanent
- **motion**: none: once set, the knot should not move; the cords either side stay flexible.
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 at the knot itself.
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - none: bends simply hold by friction; the useful differences between them are whether they jam, how they cope with cords of different size, and how they resist shaking loose (Wikipedia 'Knot', Jamming; Animated Knots 'Bend Knots')
- **holding_principle**: friction or clamping (force-fit): turns of each cord grip the other and tighten as the standing parts are pulled (Wikipedia 'Knot'; Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot', Properties)
- **input_output**: not applicable
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable to semi-permanent, depending on the bend. Removable: the Zeppelin bend 'can be untied even after being heavily loaded but not, however, while still under load' (Animated Knots); the sheet bend is valued for its 'non-jamming properties' (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend'); the alpine butterfly bend and carrick bend untie easily (Animated Knots). Semi-permanent: the double fisherman's 'can lock up so tightly that it is effectively welded' (Animated Knots) and all fisherman's variants 'jam when tightened' (Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot'); the water knot is 'Difficult to untie' (Wikipedia 'Water knot'); Hunter's and Ashley bends jammed so tight in tests 'would have had to be cut' (Animated Knots).
- **reuse_cycles**: many times for non-jamming bends (sheet bend, Zeppelin); once or a few times for jamming bends such as the double fisherman's, which may end up being cut out ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **tools_needed**: none for rope and cord. For net making, a netting needle (shuttle) that carries the twine and a gauge that sets the mesh size (Wikipedia 'Net (device)'). A fid can help loosen a jammed knot (Wikipedia 'Knot', Tools); fishing-line bends are lubricated before pulling tight (Animated Knots 'Albright Knot').
- **seal**: none
- **load**: Medium, limited by the weaker cord: a bend keeps only part of the cord's strength, and sources differ, side by side. Sheet bend 48-58% (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend' infobox); in one study of 8 bends in equal climbing rope it 'pulled apart with less than half the tension that other knots withstood', yet Ashley judged it 'quite secure enough for ordinary purposes' (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend', Security). Double fisherman's 79% (1987, nylon), 79% (2008, nylon), 80% (2014, polyester); figure-eight bend 81%, 57%, 66% (CMC Rescue tests in 12.5 mm rope, McKently, ITRS 2014). Water knot 64% in 1-inch tubular nylon webbing (CMC 1987 test, McKently 2014; CMC 'Water Knot' page). Alpine butterfly bend: 'some quoted breaking strains are as low as 53% to 58%' (Animated Knots). For planning, assume 50% (CMC 2023 recommendation; Animated Knots 'Safety'). Direction: bends are made for an end-to-end pull; a knot that snags or is pulled sideways can roll (capsize): the offset overhand bend becomes increasingly likely to capsize above about 4 kN (Wikipedia 'Offset overhand bend'), and a snag can 'catch the outer strap and flip it loose' in a water knot (Animated Knots). Never for holding people without training and a rated system.
- **adjustable**: no: a bend fixes the join; to change the length, untie and re-tie. One exception: a necklace cord closed with a fisherman's knot whose two strangle knots are left apart can be slid to adjust its length (Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot').
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable: it doesn't locate parts. Expect the knot to settle and the tails to creep a little when first loaded, so set it hard and leave long tails (Wikipedia 'Water knot').
- **access_needed**: not applicable: no panel access is involved, but you need both free ends with no load on them; a bend can't really be tied while the ropes are under tension (Animated Knots 'Sheet Bend'; Wikipedia 'Zeppelin bend').
- **materials**: Rope, cord, string, yarn, netting twine, fishing line and flat webbing or tape. Different thickness or stiffness: the sheet bend 'is practical for joining lines of different diameter or rigidity' (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend'), with the thicker cord forming the bight, and the double sheet bend 'When the ropes are markedly different in size' (Animated Knots); the figure-eight bend 'should not be used with ropes that differ much in size' for critical loads (Animated Knots); for fishing lines of different type or size, the Albright knot (Animated Knots). Flat webbing and leather: the water knot (Wikipedia 'Water knot'). Slippery fibres (HMPE such as Dyneema (brand) or Spectra (brand), aramid such as Kevlar (brand)): the fisherman's knot 'can slip or come undone' in HMPE or nylon monofilament, so use a triple fisherman's (Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot'; Animated Knots); the Zeppelin bend was found 'exceptionally secure and shake-resistant in all materials' (Animated Knots, citing Roo). Yarn for knitting: the Ashley and Hunter's bends, whose jamming is an advantage there (Animated Knots).
- **maker_difficulty**: easy: no tools; the risks are tying a look-alike wrongly (the interlinked-overhand bends are easily confused) and choosing a bend that doesn't suit the cords.

### Making it

- **processes**: off-the-shelf part: cord, rope, webbing or twine, tied by hand, 3D print (FDM): netting needles (shuttles), mesh gauges and knot practice boards (see make_it_yourself)
- **print_in_place**: no: bends are tied in cord; only tools such as netting needles are printed.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Bends themselves aren't printed, but net making uses two simple printable tools: a netting needle (shuttle) that holds the twine so it can be passed through each mesh, and a gauge, often 'a smooth stick', that keeps the meshes the same size (Wikipedia 'Net (device)'). 1) Print a netting needle flat on its side; it may need sanding smooth (one designer suggests sanding under water) so it doesn't snag twine (Printables 1144418, designer's note). 2) Size the needle to the job: one 280 mm PLA shuttle holds about 20 m of 3 mm paracord and can be scaled in the slicer, or thickened by scaling its height for stiffness (Printables 176836, designer's note). 3) Make the gauge a smooth flat strip or rod whose width sets the mesh size; nets often start and finish with a half-size gauge so the edges come out smooth (Wikipedia 'Net (device)') ⚑ [uncertain for printed gauges]. 4) When cords are joined to make a lanyard or loop for a printed part, put the bend in free cord, away from holes and edges, so it can tighten fully and be inspected ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Cord, paracord, accessory cord (5 or 6 mm for Prusik loops, Animated Knots 'Prusik Knot'), rope, tubular webbing, netting twine and fishing line; netting needles and mesh gauges for net making and mending. Alternatives that join without a knot: a short splice (Rope splices) keeps nearly the full strength; factory-sewn webbing slings and rated cord loops; for steel cable, crimped sleeves (Wire-rope terminations).
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Choose by the cords: different sizes, sheet bend (ABOK #1431) with the thicker cord as the bight, or the double sheet bend when they are markedly different; similar ropes and a compact, very secure join, double fisherman's (ABOK #294); flat webbing, water knot (ABOK #296); must untie after a heavy load, Zeppelin bend or alpine butterfly bend (Animated Knots 'Sheet Bend', 'Double Fisherman's Bend', 'Water Knot', 'Zeppelin Bend').
  - Sheet bend check: both tails should come out on the same side of the knot; 'The alternative version - with the tails on opposite sides - is less reliable' (Animated Knots 'Sheet Bend'). Wikipedia notes that tests on the matching left-hand bowline found little strength difference, so the gap may be small (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend').
  - Leave long tails, side by side: every Animated Knots bend page warns 'For critical loads the ends must be left much longer' than in its animation; water knot ends at least 7.5 cm (3 in), and the knot set with full body weight (Wikipedia 'Water knot'); flat overhand bend 'about half a meter (18 - 24 in)' (Animated Knots) against 'never less than 200mm in offset knots' (Wikipedia 'Offset overhand bend'). In one test a 3 in tail of a water knot in 9/16 in (14.3 mm) tubular webbing crept back into the knot in just over 800 cycles of 250 lb (113 kg) (Wikipedia 'Water knot').
  - Back up critical bends: for the figure-eight bend and the water knot, tie each tail in a double overhand stopper round the other standing part (Animated Knots 'Figure 8 Bend'; Wikipedia 'Water knot').
  - Slippery fibres: for Spectra, Dyneema or Kevlar/Technora use a triple fisherman's (Animated Knots); with HMPE or aramid cores, fisherman's knots can fail at high loads (generally above 15 kN) by the sheath separating and the core slipping through (Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot').
  - Closing a cord into a loop: the double (or triple) fisherman's is 'the way to join two ends of a line to form a Prusik Loop'; rough starting lengths for a pair of Prusik loops are 1.5 m and 1.83 m of 5-6 mm cord (Animated Knots 'Double Fisherman's Bend', 'Prusik Knot'). Webbing slings are closed with a water knot (Wikipedia 'Water knot').
  - Fishing line: blood knot with at least five and up to seven turns each side for lines of similar size; Albright knot with about 10 wraps for lines of different size or type; lubricate before pulling tight (Animated Knots 'Blood Knot', 'Albright Knot').
  - Net making: tie the first row to a headrope, then work each later row with sheet bends round a gauge using a netting needle; first and last rows are often made on a half-size gauge (Wikipedia 'Net (device)'). Animated Knots shows the sheet bend and the carrick bend used at net junctions.
  - Never use the reef (square) knot as a bend: Ashley wrote that used that way it 'is probably responsible for more deaths and injuries than have been caused by the failure of all other knots combined' (quoted in Wikipedia 'Bend (knot)'). See Basic knots.
  - Strength for planning: assume about 50% of the weaker cord's rated strength (CMC 2023; Animated Knots 'Safety'); measured values run from about 48% (sheet bend) to about 80% (double fisherman's) (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend'; McKently 2014).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Retying a broken shoelace or string (the Hunter's bend was first made public by a man who had used it for years on broken shoelaces) (Animated Knots 'Hunter's Bend')
  - A fishing net or a garden or sports net, whose meshes are tied with sheet bends (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend', 'Net (device)')
  - A loop of cord closed with a double fisherman's knot, as climbers use for a Prusik loop (Animated Knots 'Prusik Knot')
  - A necklace cord tied with a fisherman's knot whose two halves slide to adjust the length (Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot')
- **when_to_use**: When two pieces of cord, rope or tape must become one longer line, or one cord must be closed into a ring, and no splicing or sewing is wanted. Use the sheet bend for quick, everyday joins and above all for cords of different thickness or stiffness, such as a thin line to a thick rope, and for net making; the double sheet bend when the sizes are very different. Use the double fisherman's for a compact, very secure join that you don't need to untie, such as closing a cord loop or joining two lengths of similar cord. Use the water knot to join or close flat webbing and tape. Use the Zeppelin bend (or alpine butterfly bend) when you want a secure join that unties easily after a hard pull (Animated Knots; Wikipedia).
- **when_not_to_use**: Never for climbing, abseiling, rescue, swings or anything that holds a person without training and a rated system. Never use the reef knot, granny knot or thief knot to join two cords (see Basic knots). Don't use a double fisherman's where you'll want to untie it, or where the join must pull over rocks or edges without snagging, e.g. canyoning (Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot'): use a Zeppelin bend. Don't use the figure-eight bend with cords of very different size (Animated Knots). Don't trust a single sheet bend in slippery or stiff rope or on a line that flaps slack (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend'): double it or choose another bend. Avoid the Hunter's and Ashley bends in rope because they jam (Animated Knots). For full strength or a permanent join, splice instead (Rope splices) or sew webbing (Load-bearing stitch patterns for straps and webbing); for steel cable use a crimped sleeve (Wire-rope terminations).
- **environment_limits**: Shaking and slack: the sheet bend, like the bowline, 'has a tendency to work loose when not under load' (Wikipedia 'Sheet bend'), while the Zeppelin bend 'is also resistant to the effects of slack shaking and cyclic loading' (Wikipedia 'Zeppelin bend'). Repeated loading: the water knot's outer tail can creep back into the knot at low forces (Wikipedia 'Water knot'), and 'Cyclical loading of a Water Knot can cause the outer strap to work loose' (Animated Knots). Wet and ice: nylon 'absorbs water and is 10-15% weaker when wet' (Wikipedia 'Rope'); some authors are cautious about the flat overhand bend in wet or icy rope (Animated Knots). Slippery fibres: HMPE and nylon monofilament make fisherman's knots slip (Wikipedia 'Fisherman's knot'). Sunlight: some synthetic cords are damaged more easily by UV (Wikipedia 'Rope').
- **safety_notes**: A home-made bend must never hold a person: for anything that holds a person, use a rated system and get training. The reef knot used as a bend has killed many people (Ashley, quoted in Wikipedia 'Bend (knot)'). Several deaths have been linked to water knots in webbing pulling loose when snagged (Walter Siebert and Pit Schubert, cited in Wikipedia 'Water knot'), and the flat figure-eight bend 'can roll at quite a low tension and several deaths have been reported' (Animated Knots 'Flat Overhand Bend'). Of the double fisherman's, Animated Knots says: 'If you tie it and your life depends on it, inspect it carefully. If someone else ties it, inspect it extremely carefully.' Use a design factor of 12:1 for live or critical loads (Animated Knots 'Safety').
- **common_failures**: Using the wrong knot: a reef, granny or thief knot as a bend capsizes and spills (Wikipedia 'Bend (knot)'). Sheet bend tied with the tails on opposite sides, or a single sheet bend in cords of very different size, slipping (Animated Knots). Shaking loose when slack (sheet bend) (Wikipedia). Tail creep in water knots under repeated loading, and the outer strap flipped loose by a snag; prevent it with long tails, backup overhand knots, and by placing the knot so the outer strap isn't caught (Wikipedia 'Water knot'; Animated Knots). Mis-tied look-alikes: the Zeppelin, Hunter's, Ashley and alpine butterfly bends all use interlocked overhand knots, so 'Similarity ... invites confusion - and mistakes', and a wrongly threaded butterfly bend becomes an 'Evil Impostor' (Animated Knots). Rolling (capsizing) of offset overhand and especially flat figure-eight bends at high load (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Offset overhand bend'). Jamming so hard the knot must be cut (double fisherman's, Hunter's, Ashley) (Animated Knots). Tails cut too short. Breaking at the knot well below the cord's rated strength (Wikipedia 'Knot').
- **related**: 
  - Basic knots (overhand knot, half hitch, reef knot): the reef knot is a binding knot and must never be used as a bend
  - Fixed loop knots (bowline, figure-eight loop, alpine butterfly): every bend has matching loop knots; two linked bowlines can also join two ropes, and the alpine butterfly bend is tied like the loop
  - Rope splices (eye splice, short splice, braided-rope splice): the short splice joins two ropes permanently at nearly full strength
  - Tensioning and adjustable hitches (trucker's hitch, taut-line hitch, Prusik): the Prusik needs a cord loop closed with a double fisherman's
  - Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye: the becket hitch is the sheet bend tied to an eye instead of a rope
  - Stopper knots and cord-end anchors: the double overhand used as a backup in a bend's tails
  - Load-bearing stitch patterns for straps and webbing (bar tack, box-X stitch): sewing is the stronger, permanent way to join webbing
  - Wire-rope terminations (wire-rope clip, thimble, crimped sleeve): how steel cable is joined instead of knotting
  - Binding knots and seizings: a seizing can lash a bend's tails down

## 384 Pole lashings (square, diagonal, shear and tripod lashings)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Rope wrapped round two or three poles where they cross or touch, then pulled tight with turns between the poles (frapping turns), to build frames, tripods and A-frames without nails or bolts

*Also covers:* Also covers round lashing to lengthen a pole and garden lashing of bamboo canes. Safety: check any lashed structure under load before people climb or stand on it.

### What it is

- **plain_name**: Rope or string wound tightly round two or three poles where they cross or lie side by side, then cinched with extra turns between the poles, so sticks, canes or logs can be built into frames, tripods, A-frames and trellises without nails, screws or bolts
- **term**: Pole lashings (spar lashings): square, diagonal, shear, round and tripod lashings, each made of wrapping turns (wraps) pulled tight by frapping turns (fraps)
- **aliases**: 
  - lashing (ropework): 'an arrangement of rope, wire, or webbing ... used to secure and fasten two or more items together in a somewhat rigid manner' (Wikipedia 'Lashing (ropework)')
  - square lashing (ABOK #2114), including the traditional, modified and Japanese (Mark II) square lashings (Animated Knots; Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION')
  - diagonal lashing (ABOK #2115), including the Filipino diagonal lashing (Animated Knots; Scout Pioneering)
  - shear lashing, sheer lashing, two-spar shear lashing, for shear legs and A-frames (ABOK #2108-2110) (Animated Knots; Wikipedia)
  - round lashing, for joining two parallel poles to make a longer one (similar to ABOK #2103); West Country round lashing, strop lashing, half-hitch round lashing; gammon is the nautical round lashing of a mast (Animated Knots; Scout Pioneering 'Four Different Lashings to Extend the Length of a Pole'; Wikipedia)
  - tripod lashing, gyn lashing, figure-of-eight lashing, three-spar shear lashing (ABOK #2111); with four poles, a quadpod (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Lashing (ropework)', 'Pioneering (scouting)')
  - floor lashing: lashes a row of poles side by side into a deck or table top (Wikipedia 'Pioneering (scouting)')
  - wraps, fraps or frapping turns, and racking turns: the turns a lashing is built from (Wikipedia 'List of knot terminology'; Animated Knots 'Terminology')
  - pioneering (scouting): building structures from spars and lashings (Wikipedia 'Pioneering (scouting)')
  - bamboo scaffolding ties: nylon or bamboo strips used to lash scaffold poles (Hong Kong Labour Department code)
  - cane wigwam, tepee or bean teepee: garden canes tied together at the top (RHS; Fine Gardening)
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: interlocking joints & joinery, clamping, magnetic & friction connections
- **one_liner**: Rope wrapped round poles where they cross or touch and then tightened with turns between them, to make rigid frames, braced crossings, scissor legs, tripods and lengthened poles with no hardware or cut joints.
- **complexity**: A few parts: one length of rope, twine or string and the two or three poles (spars, canes, dowels or logs) it joins. It starts and finishes with simple hitches (usually a clove hitch, or a timber hitch for a diagonal lashing), and a round lashing may add two wooden wedges.
- **how_it_works**: The rope is first tied to a pole, then wound round both poles several times (wraps). Next, frapping turns go round the wraps in the gap between the poles, at right angles to them, which pulls every wrap tighter, much like pinching in the middle of a loose belt (Wikipedia 'Lashing (ropework)'; Animated Knots 'Square Lashing'). The tight rope presses the poles hard together, so friction between rope and poles, and between the poles themselves, stops them sliding or turning; the end is tied off with a hitch or half hitches (Animated Knots). A diagonal lashing starts with a timber hitch round both poles, which pulls a gap between them closed (Animated Knots 'Diagonal Lashing'); a tripod lashing weaves 'racking turns' in and out between three poles like extended figure eights (Animated Knots 'Tripod Lashing'); a round lashing for lengthening a pole has no frapping turns, because the poles already touch, and is tightened with wedges instead (Scout Pioneering; Animated Knots 'Round Lashing').

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: joins poles where they cross (square and diagonal), at their tips so the legs can spread (shear and tripod), or side by side to make one longer pole (round)
  - tighten and hold tension: frapping turns pull the wraps tight and keep them tight
  - lock, and release by hand: unties by hand so the rope and poles can be used again
- **motion**: none once frapped: the joint is meant to be rigid. A shear lashing is deliberately left as a scissor pivot while the legs are spread, then frapped (Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION'); a loose strop lashing 'allows movement or acts as a hinge' (Scout Pioneering).
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 when finished and frapped; 1 (the legs pivot about the lashing) while shear legs or a tripod are being spread, or in a deliberately loose strop lashing.
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds): frapping turns, wound round the wraps between the poles, take up the slack in the wraps and keep them tight (Wikipedia 'List of knot terminology', Fraps; Animated Knots 'Square Lashing')
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): the tightened wraps clamp the poles together, and friction between rope and poles stops them moving (Wikipedia 'Lashing (ropework)'; Animated Knots)
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the wraps encircle both poles, and in a tripod the crossed legs 'bind against each other for greater stability' (Animated Knots 'Tripod Lashing')
- **input_output**: not applicable
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable: unties by hand and the ropes are re-used ('When the day's work is complete, untie all knots, coil all ropes, check all hardware', Scout Pioneering '22 Pioneering Safety Points'). A West Country round lashing 'can be a little difficult to untie' (Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION').
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: pioneering kits keep lashing ropes cut to standard lengths with whipped ends for repeated use, and ropes and poles are checked before and after each use (Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION', '22 Pioneering Safety Points').
- **tools_needed**: none to tie. Helpful extras: a stick to lever frapping turns tight using your legs (Animated Knots 'Square Lashing'); two wooden wedges and a mallet to tighten a round lashing (Animated Knots 'Round Lashing'); loppers or a saw to cut canes (NPS 'Building with Bamboo'); a mallet for pioneering stakes (Scout Pioneering).
- **seal**: none
- **load**: Light (garden canes) to medium (scout-size spar structures); no laboratory strength figures for rope lashings were found ⚑ [uncertain]. The square lashing 'is designed to be load bearing and can be used to create scaffolding' (Animated Knots), and Hong Kong bamboo scaffolds are fastened with nylon strips (Wikipedia 'Bamboo scaffolding'), but those follow an official code with trained workers. Strongest where the poles press against each other (crossways shear and compression); weakest against the frame twisting out of square (racking), which is why frames are cross-braced with diagonal lashings 'to prevent racking' (Wikipedia 'Lashing (ropework)'), and starting with a clove hitch 'helps keep the structure from racking' (Scout Pioneering). Grip falls if the rope gets wet or the poles shrink (see environment_limits). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **adjustable**: partly: tension can be topped up by adding frapping turns or re-tying, and the spread of shear legs and tripods is set before the fraps are pulled tight (Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION'; Animated Knots 'Tripod Lashing'). Moving a pole means untying the lashing.
- **play_and_precision**: noticeable: a friction joint on rough, irregular poles, so it can settle and creep and is not used to locate parts precisely ⚑ [uncertain]. 'Good lashings are not made in a hurry. Each wrap must be made with a strain on the rope', and frapping turns are 'pulled up as tightly as possible' to keep it rigid (Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION'). It copes well with poles of uneven size and with crossings from 45 to 90 degrees (Animated Knots 'Square Lashing'). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **access_needed**: both sides: the rope must pass right round the poles, so you need to reach all the way round the crossing.
- **materials**: Rope: pure manila is the traditional choice for scout pioneering, 1/4 in for most projects and 3/8 in when the two spars together exceed 6 in diameter; beware sisal sold as manila, which is 'not anywhere near as good' (Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION', 'Lashing Ropes and Spars'); binder twine or cheaper rope for small temporary gadgets (Scout Pioneering). Garden work: 'cotton or nylon string works fine' for finger-thick bamboo on a stake, thin clothesline for three stout saplings in a bean tepee, and baling twine worked poorly for one gardener (Fine Gardening); polypropylene twine for a bamboo trellis (NPS); twine for cane A-frames (RHS). Poles: straight wooden spars with little taper (pine is widely used, hardwoods allow thinner spars), typically 2-4 in at the butt and 6-12 ft long for scout projects (Scout Pioneering); bamboo canes; dowels and staves. Wikipedia notes the clove hitch, the usual starting knot, 'will not work on rectangular shapes' (Wikipedia 'Knot'), so lashings suit round poles best.
- **maker_difficulty**: easy for garden canes and light frames; moderate for tight, load-bearing lashings on thick spars, which need technique and strength to frap tight; anything people climb or stand on needs experienced supervision.

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part: rope, twine or string, tied by hand
  - woodwork: cutting and trimming poles, spars, canes and dowels, and cutting wedges for round lashings
  - 3D print (FDM): printed alternatives to lashing (cane wigwam holders, trellis joints, tripod connectors) and miniature practice models
- **print_in_place**: no: lashings are tied with rope round poles; only alternative connectors or practice parts are printed.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Lashings aren't printed, but printed parts can replace or help them. 1) For a cane wigwam, a printed disc with holes round the edge can replace tying the tops; its designer recommends PETG or ASA because 'PLA will go brittle after a season outdoors' (Printables 1732400, designer's note). 2) Printed bamboo trellis joints (an OpenSCAD customiser) work best in TPU or another flexible filament after two seasons' use, because flexible joints forgive mismatched cane diameters; secure canes with UV-resistant cable ties of at least 40 lb strength pulled tight with a tie gun, since bamboo 'shrinks a bit over time' and slips; printed ridges keep the ties in place (Printables 104360, designer's note). 3) A printed teepee connector for three 25-30 mm wooden rods, with an M6 clamping screw, makes a tripod without lashing (Printables 1275716). 4) If you lash over printed tubes or brackets, add a groove or ridges so the wraps can't slide off, and expect a printed part under constant lashing tension to creep, especially in PLA ⚑ [uncertain]. 5) To practise, make miniature pioneering models with small sticks and white thread, as scouts do (Wikipedia 'Pioneering (scouting)'). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Manila rope in 1/4 in and 3/8 in, binder (baler) twine, polypropylene twine, jute or cotton garden twine, nylon string and thin clothesline; bamboo canes (sold in bundles at garden centres, RHS), wooden spars, dowels and staves; wooden wedges. Hardware alternatives: cable ties (Linear ratchet), hose clamps, U-bolt clamps, clamp-on tube fittings, and printed or bought cane connectors and wigwam toppers.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Rope length, side by side: add the two pole diameters in inches and allow 3 ft of rope per inch, e.g. 2.5 in + 3.5 in = 6 in, so 18 ft (Peschke's Pioneering merit badge pamphlet, quoted by Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION'); the scouting Fieldbook says '1 yard of rope for each inch of the combined diameters' (quoted by Fine Gardening), the same rule; for a 1/4 in bamboo spar on a 1 x 2 in stake one gardener cuts about 5 ft of string and still often runs short (Fine Gardening).
  - Rope size: 1/4 in manila when the two spars together are 6 in diameter or less, 3/8 in above that (Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION').
  - Turns, side by side: square lashing with three or four wrapping turns and three or four frapping turns (Animated Knots 'Square Lashing'); three wraps and two fraps (Scout Pioneering); 'two or three or even four really tight turns, known as frapping turns' (Fine Gardening). Shear lashing: eight or ten wraps, then fraps (Scout Pioneering). Tripod: about six racking turns, then two or three fraps in each gap (Animated Knots).
  - Pick the lashing by how the poles meet: square lashing for poles crossing at about 45-90 degrees (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Pioneering (scouting)'); diagonal lashing, started with a timber hitch, where crossing poles don't touch, as at the middle of an X-brace (Scout Pioneering; Animated Knots); shear lashing for two poles whose far ends spread into an A-frame; tripod lashing for three legs; round lashing to make a longer pole (Wikipedia 'Lashing (ropework)').
  - Shear legs and A-frames: tie the wraps somewhat loose, spread the legs to the required distance, then frap and finish (Scout Pioneering 'Lashing INFORMATION').
  - Tripod: don't over-tighten; unlike other lashings, 'it is possible to make it too tight!', which can stop the legs spreading or overload the rope (Animated Knots 'Tripod Lashing').
  - Lengthening a pole (round lashing): overlap the poles well and use two lashings near the ends of the overlap, each of 6-8 turns (Animated Knots) or 8-10 turns (Scout Pioneering) with no frapping turns; drive wedges between the poles to tighten (Animated Knots); on smooth poles add half hitches to the clove hitches (Scout Pioneering). For comparison, Hong Kong's code for professional bamboo scaffolds asks for overlaps of 1.5-2 m (standards) or at least 2 m (ledgers), ties no more than 300 mm apart, and each tie made with at least 5 rounds of nylon strip (Labour Department, Code of Practice for Bamboo Scaffolding Safety, 5th ed. 2024, printed pp. 30-31).
  - Tighten frapping turns with your legs: wrap the rope round a stick, stand on the poles with knees bent, hold the stick across your thighs and straighten up (Animated Knots, crediting Dana Holgate).
  - Garden canes: push uprights in about 20 cm apart, tie the tops of two rows together into A-frames and lay a horizontal cane along the crossing for strength; wigwams use three or four canes (RHS). A freestanding trellis needs its support stakes buried about 2 ft, or about 6 in if it leans on something (NPS 'Building with Bamboo').

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A wigwam or row of A-frames of bamboo canes tied at the top for runner beans or sweet peas (RHS)
  - Bamboo scaffolding lashed together on buildings in Hong Kong (Wikipedia 'Bamboo scaffolding')
  - Scout camp gateways, tables, towers and bridges built from spars and lashings (Wikipedia 'Pioneering (scouting)')
  - A garden trellis of bamboo canes lashed with twine (NPS 'Building with Bamboo'; Fine Gardening)
- **when_to_use**: When you want to build a frame from round poles, canes or sticks without drilling, cutting joints or buying hardware: garden trellises, bean wigwams and cane cages, camp furniture and gadgets, temporary stands, display frames, rafts and play structures (Animated Knots 'Square Lashing'; Wikipedia 'Pioneering (scouting)'; Fine Gardening). It suits poles of uneven or varying diameter, like bamboo or saplings, that would be hard to fit with standard fittings, and structures that must come apart again with the poles and rope re-used. Use a square lashing for crossings, a diagonal lashing to pull together and brace an X, a shear lashing for A-frames, a tripod lashing for three legs, and a round lashing to lengthen a pole.
- **when_not_to_use**: Not for anything people climb, sit or stand on unless it is built and checked under experienced supervision and tested under load first; for anything that holds a person, use a rated, designed system. Not for permanent outdoor structures, where rope rots or loosens: use bolted or cut joints (Cross-halving slot joint, Japanese interlocking joints) or Clamp-on tube fittings. Not for square-section timber, where the starting clove hitch 'will not work on rectangular shapes' (Wikipedia 'Knot'): screw or bolt it instead. Not for parts that must line up precisely. For quick light ties on thin canes, cable ties (Linear ratchet), hose clamps or a printed connector are faster; for a self-supporting set of crossing sticks, see Reciprocal frame. Don't build in rain or wet conditions (Scout Pioneering).
- **environment_limits**: Wet: 'Rope and spars become slippery, as does your footing. Knots can slip when wet and become unsafe' (Scout Pioneering '22 Pioneering Safety Points'); nylon is '10-15% weaker when wet' (Wikipedia 'Rope'). Rot and weather: natural-fibre rope rots more readily than synthetic, and some synthetics are damaged more easily by UV light (Wikipedia 'Rope'); manila 'withstands repeated wetting and drying cycles' (Peschke, quoted by Scout Pioneering). Shrinkage: green bamboo or wood dries and shrinks, loosening lashings and ties (one trellis designer notes bamboo 'shrinks a bit over time') (Printables 104360; a maker's observation, not a measured rate) ⚑ [uncertain]; Hong Kong's code requires scaffold bamboo to be air-dried at least 90 days before use (Wikipedia 'Bamboo scaffolding'). Bamboo 'deteriorates over time' and is combustible (Wikipedia 'Bamboo scaffolding'). Printed helper parts in PLA go brittle outdoors (Printables 1732400, designer's note). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **safety_notes**: Check any lashed structure under load before people climb or stand on it, and never rely on a home-made lashing to hold a person without experienced supervision. Scouting practice: 'Before allowing general use, run a complete test to see everything is working correctly', keep checking anchors as strain is applied, limit how many people are on a platform, and climb on only 'after all lashings are tight, and the structure has been completely inspected'; under US camp accreditation rules, a project lifting participants more than 6 ft must be formally reviewed, or the climber top-rope belayed (Scout Pioneering '22 Pioneering Safety Points'). Professional bamboo scaffolds may only be built by trained workers under a competent person, and those over 15 m must be designed by a professional engineer (Hong Kong Labour Department code); Hong Kong began phasing bamboo scaffolding out in 2025, citing 23 deaths since 2018 (Wikipedia 'Bamboo scaffolding'). Keep fingers clear when lifting a spar to frap a tripod or shear lashing, don't stand on spars lying on the ground because they roll, and take care with mallets (Scout Pioneering). Over-tightening a tripod lashing can break the rope (Animated Knots).
- **common_failures**: Joint slides or rotates because the wraps were loose or the frapping turns too few or too slack; make every wrap under strain and frap as tightly as possible (Scout Pioneering; Animated Knots). Frame racks (leans out of square) because it has no diagonal bracing; add an X-brace with diagonal lashings (Wikipedia 'Lashing (ropework)'). Starting clove hitch slips on smooth poles; add half hitches, or start with a timber hitch (Scout Pioneering; Animated Knots). Gap left between crossing poles that don't touch; use a diagonal lashing whose timber hitch springs them together, because 'A clove hitch around one pole could not be used to pull the poles together and might come untied' (Animated Knots 'Diagonal Lashing'). Round lashing slips because it is hard to get tight; use two lashings far apart plus wedges (Animated Knots). Tripod lashed too tight to spread, or the rope overloaded when spread (Animated Knots). Rope too short to finish the lashing; join on more rope rather than leaving it short (Scout Pioneering). Lashings loosen as poles dry or rope gets wet, or rope rots outdoors; re-tighten or replace (Scout Pioneering; Wikipedia 'Rope').
- **related**: 
  - Reciprocal frame (self-supporting stick bridge): crossing sticks that hold each other up; its crossings can be lashed
  - Clamp-on tube fittings (slip-on structural fittings): hardware that joins tubes at the same kinds of crossings and corners
  - Cross-halving slot joint (egg-crate joint): a cut joint for two members crossing, for flat stock rather than round poles
  - Japanese interlocking joints (splices and three-way corners): cut joints that lengthen beams or join three members without rope
  - Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye (clove hitch, round turn and two half hitches): the clove and timber hitches that start and finish lashings
  - Binding knots and seizings (constrictor knot, seizing, cable lacing): tight wraps round a bundle; a quick tie for light canes
  - Basic knots (overhand knot, half hitch, reef knot): the reef knot finishes West Country and strop lashings
  - Linear ratchet (cable-tie ratchet): cable ties are the quick hardware alternative for light canes
  - Hose clamp (worm-drive clamp, ear clamp, spring hose clamp): a metal band alternative for clamping poles together
  - Clamp-on accessory mount for bars, poles and edges (handlebar clamp, desk clamp, U-bolt): fixes a part to a pole with hardware
  - Rope-end finishing (whipping, heat-sealing, back splice, aglet): whip lashing-rope ends so they last
