## 385 Rope-end finishing (whipping, heat-sealing, back splice, aglet)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Ways to stop a cut rope, cord or webbing end fraying, by binding it with twine (whipping), melting synthetic fibres, tucking the strands back (back splice), tape, a dip coating or a cap (aglet)

*Also covers:* Also covers which fibres can be melted, and printed melting forms and aglets. Safety: burns from flames and hot knives; hard sealed ends can cut hands

### What it is

- **plain_name**: Ways to stop the cut end of a rope, cord, shoelace or strap from unravelling into a fuzzy tassel: wrap it tightly with thin string, melt the plastic fibres into a solid tip, weave the strands back into the rope, wrap it in tape, dip it in a rubbery liquid, or push a small tube or cap over it
- **term**: Rope-end finishing (whipping, heat sealing or fusing, back splice, liquid whipping, aglet)
- **aliases**: 
  - whipping or whipping knot: a binding of twine round a rope's end 'to prevent its natural tendency to fray' (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot')
  - common whipping (ABOK #3443 according to Animated Knots; Wikipedia gives ABOK #3442), also called sailor's, plain, ordinary or 'wolf' whipping
  - sailmaker's whipping or palm-and-needle whipping (ABOK #3446; the needle-free version is ABOK #3448, Wikipedia)
  - West Country whipping, also printed as 'West County whipping' (ABOK #3458)
  - French whipping (a row of half hitches) and Portuguese whipping (ends reef-knotted together) (Wikipedia, quoting ABOK pp. 547-548)
  - heat sealing, fusing, melting, singeing or burning the end; makers often call melting a paracord end 'whipping' it (Printables 'Paracord Melting (Whipping) Form')
  - hot cutting with a hot knife or rope cutter (an electrically heated blade that cuts and seals in one go)
  - tape and cut (wrap tape, cut through the middle of it)
  - back splice or end splice (ABOK #2813), started with a crown knot (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Rope splicing')
  - liquid whipping, whip-end dip or rope-end dip; e.g. Star brite Dip-It Whip-It (brand)
  - glued end (thin superglue soaked into the cut end) (Edelrid)
  - aglet or aiglet, lace tip, shoelace tip, cord end, rope end cap; heat-shrink aglet
  - constrictor knot or a few turns of tape as a temporary stop until a proper whipping is put on (Wikipedia; Animated Knots)
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: welding, brazing & soldering, adhesives & bonding, cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware
- **one_liner**: A finish put on the cut end of a rope, cord or strap (tight turns of twine, melted fibres, strands tucked back, tape, a rubbery dip or a small cap) that holds the fibres together so the end does not fray, and often makes it easier to thread through holes.
- **complexity**: Single piece or a few parts. Melting and the back splice use only the rope itself. Whipping adds whipping twine (and for the sailmaker's whipping on braided rope a needle and a sailmaker's palm; premiumropes). Tape, a liquid coating or superglue add one material. An aglet adds a small plastic or metal tube or cap, sometimes with glue, or a piece of heat-shrink tubing (Wikipedia 'Aglet'; iFixit 'How to Repair Frayed Shoelaces').
- **how_it_works**: A rope is many fibres twisted or braided together, and the twist or braid is what keeps them together; at a cut end nothing holds them, so they unlay and fray (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'). Whipping wraps tight turns of twine round the end so friction squeezes the fibres together; in the sailmaker's whipping extra 'frapping turns' run along the grooves and lock the wraps so they cannot unwind if one is damaged (Animated Knots). Heat sealing melts plastic fibres (nylon, polyester, polypropylene, polyethylene) so they flow together and harden into one solid end (a material bond); aramid such as Kevlar only chars, and natural fibres such as cotton and hemp don't melt (Animated Knots; Ravenox). A back splice opens the strands, turns them back with a crown knot and weaves each strand over and under the rope's own strands, so the rope's grip holds them (Animated Knots). A liquid whipping, superglue or a glued or crimped aglet binds or clamps the fibres from outside; an aglet's 'firmness and narrow profile make it easier to hold and easier to feed through eyelets' (Wikipedia 'Aglet').

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: binds the loose fibres of a cut rope, cord or strap end together so it can't fray or unravel
  - glue or bond: heat sealing melts the fibres together, and liquid whipping or superglue glues them
- **motion**: none: the finish is fixed to the rope's end
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0: nothing moves; the finish simply stays on the end of the rope (a common whipping can slide off if it is pushed, which is a failure, not a motion; Wikipedia 'Common whipping').
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - none: it only stops fraying. A stiff aglet or a melted, tapered tip also makes the end easier to thread through eyelets and holes, which is a side benefit rather than a mechanism (Wikipedia 'Aglet'; Printables 'Super Deluxe Paracord Melting (Whipping) Form')
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): whipping turns, tape, heat-shrink tubing and crimped metal aglets squeeze the fibres together; the back splice's tucked strands are gripped by the rope's own strands
  - material bond (glue, solvent or melting): heat sealing fuses the fibres; liquid whipping, superglue and glued-on aglets bond them
- **input_output**: not applicable: it finishes a rope end and passes no motion on
- **power**: passive (no power needed) once made. A hot knife or soldering iron needs electricity only while you cut and seal.
- **removability**: Whipping and tape: removable (cut or unwind them off). Back splice: semi-permanent; it can be unpicked, but removing a splice 'leaves a distortion of the rope' (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'). Melted, glued or dipped ends and glued or crimped aglets: permanent; cut the end off to remove them.
- **reuse_cycles**: once: a finish is made for one end; to change it you cut it off and make a new one (a loosened West Country whipping can be re-tied a few times; Animated Knots)
- **tools_needed**: A sharp knife or scissors. Whipping: whipping twine, and for the sailmaker's whipping a large needle (essential on braided or kernmantle rope) and a sailmaker's palm (Animated Knots; premiumropes). Heat sealing: a lighter or other flame, a hot knife (rope cutter, 'essentially just soldering irons with a cutting blade'), or a soldering iron; a heat-resistant surface and thin leather gloves (Edelrid). Back splice: none ('No additional tools or equipment are required', Animated Knots). Other finishes: tape; thin superglue (Edelrid); liquid whipping with its brush (Star brite); heat-shrink tubing plus a lighter or heat gun (iFixit); an aglet crimper for metal aglets (Printables 'Aglet Crimper').
- **seal**: none: the finish keeps the fibres together but is not a water or air seal. Liquid whipping coats and protects the end 'from weather and abrasion' (Star brite), which is protection, not a watertight seal.
- **load**: Not a load-carrying joint: it adds no strength. A back splice needs only 'about three complete tucks' because 'no load is applied to a back splice' (Animated Knots), and 'A neat end does not restore damaged rope or establish a working load rating' (Ravenox). What matters is how well the finish survives handling and rubbing: a sailmaker's whipping is 'the most secure whipping' (Animated Knots), a common whipping 'will slide off the end of the rope with little provocation' (Wikipedia 'Common whipping'), and a thin hot-cut seal 'does not survive compression or abuse' (Animated Knots). Never rely on any rope, knot or finish you made yourself to hold a person.
- **adjustable**: no: once made it stays where it is; to move or change it, cut it off and finish the end again
- **play_and_precision**: Not applicable for location, but the finished thickness matters: a back splice makes the end 'about twice the thickness of the rest of the rope' (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing') and 'usually prevents the rope's end from passing though blocks and pulleys' (Animated Knots); a free-melted end forms a bulb that may not pass through a hole sized for the cord, which is why makers use a melting form to make a slim tip (Printables 'Paracord Melting (Whipping) Form').
- **access_needed**: end of a tube or shaft: you work on the cut end of the rope, cord or strap. Tape, or a painted ring of liquid whipping, can also go on in the middle of a length before you cut through it (Edelrid; Star brite; Wikipedia 'Whipping knot').
- **materials**: Which fibres can be melted: nylon (polyamide), polyester (Dacron), polypropylene and polyethylene, including ultra-high-molecular-weight polyethylene such as Dyneema (brand) (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'; Animated Knots). Melting points: HMPE 149 °C, nylon 218-254 °C, polypropylene 166 °C and polyethylene 135 °C (SafeWork NSW rigging guide); RopeLab lists a decomposition point of 260 °C for polyester. Which can't: aramid (Kevlar, Technora, Twaron; brands) 'merely charred by heat' (Animated Knots), and natural cotton and hemp 'do not melt into a sealed end' (Ravenox); manila and sisal are natural too and need whipping or a glue finish ⚑ [uncertain: no opened source says manila or sisal can't be melted, though they are natural fibres (SafeWork NSW)]. Mixed ropes (Dyneema or aramid core in a polyester or nylon cover) don't fuse into one piece: 'the core is separated from the cover' (premiumropes), so glue them (Edelrid) or whip them. Twine: waxed polyester whipping twine (premiumropes) or traditional tarred hemp (marline) (Wikipedia 'Common whipping'). Aglets: plastic, copper or brass (Wikipedia 'Aglet'; Commons photo), printed PLA, PETG or flexible filament (Printables), or heat-shrink tubing (iFixit). Liquid whipping works 'on both natural and synthetic fibers' (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'). Webbing (nylon or polyester) is hot-cut or melted the same way (iFixit 'Trim a Webbing Belt'). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **maker_difficulty**: easy for melting, tape, heat-shrink tips and the West Country whipping ('must be the easiest whipping to teach and learn', Animated Knots); moderate for the common whipping and back splice (a little practice); moderate to hard for the sailmaker's whipping on braided rope (needs a needle, a palm and care to space the frapping turns evenly; Animated Knots)

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - hand ropework: whipping, back splice, tape and glue need no machine
  - off-the-shelf part: whipping twine, hot knife, liquid whipping, heat-shrink tubing, plastic or metal aglets
  - 3D print (FDM): printed melting forms that shape a melted paracord tip, printed aglets and rope end caps, and a printed aglet crimper (Printables models below)
  - 3D print (resin): one printed rope-end-cap design soaks the rope tip in resin to stiffen it before the cap goes on (Printables 'Rope End Caps (Aglets)'); a resin-printed cap is possible but no source tested one ⚑ [uncertain] *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **print_in_place**: no: the finish is made on the rope itself, and printed aglets and caps are separate parts glued or screwed on. Among the printed helper tools, one melting-form remix prints already hinged ('prints fully assembled, no extra parts or screws required'; Printables 'Super Deluxe Paracord Melting Form – Print-in-Place Remix'); the original needs two M3 screws for its hinge.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Holes for cord in printed parts: a freely melted paracord end forms a bulb that won't go through a hole sized for the cord; one designer made a melting form because 'I always struggle getting a whipped (melted) paracord end though a reasonable size hole' (Printables 926284). Either make the hole a little bigger than the cord or shape the tip in a form. 2) Melting form use: start with a fresh cut (old melted ends stick), lay the cord in the groove with the cut end out of the wide funnel end, close the form, melt the tip without letting the flame touch it (touching chars it black), then pull the cord slowly through the form from the other side, and trim any wisp with side cutters (Printables 926284 and 1322380). The 'Super Deluxe' form fits 550 paracord of about 3.0-3.4 mm (Printables 1322380). 3) Form material: the author found 'the nylon from the paracord does not stick much to PLA' (Printables 926284); the print-in-place remix is printed flat in PETG at 0.2 mm layers with no supports (Printables 1462797). Work in a well-ventilated area and don't overheat the cord (same). 4) Printed aglets: one design comes in sizes for 2-6 mm laces, printed in PLA or PETG at 0.2 mm layers, 100% infill, no supports; wrap tape round the lace end, test the fit, then glue the lace into the tip (Printables 'Classic aglet 2-6mm'). A craft-cord end leaves 'a small amount of spare clearance' for superglue and prints best with a brim and support (Printables 'Craft Cord End (Aglet)'). A threaded aglet screws onto the lace, with glue optional (Printables 'Threaded Aglet – Screw-On Shoelace Tip'). 5) Rope end caps for 10-14 mm boat ropes: print at very slow speed with inner and outer brim and no supports, in flexible or rigid filament that is 'ideally opaque and UV-resistant'; the rope tip is stiffened with resin, filed to size and pushed in with adhesive (Printables 'Rope End Caps (Aglets)'). 6) Metal aglets: a printed crimper for 5 mm metal aglets prints best standing vertically (Printables 'Aglet Crimper'). 7) Typical failures: a cap or aglet that slips off (no glue, or too loose), a tip charred by the flame, and a wisp of melted plastic left on the tip.
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search for: 'whipping twine' (waxed polyester), 'sail needles' and 'sailmaker's palm' (premiumropes); 'hot knife', 'rope cutter' or 'hot knife rope cutter' with rope and fabric blades (Practical Sailor names Engel, RoMech and Gonytia (brands); Edelrid); 'liquid rope whipping' or 'whip end dip' (e.g. Star brite Dip-It Whip-It (brand)); 'heat shrink tubing' (iFixit); 'aglets', 'lace tips' or 'cord ends', plastic or metal, and an 'aglet crimper' for metal ones (Wikipedia 'Aglet'; Printables); 'thin (low-viscosity) cyanoacrylate' (Edelrid); self-adhesive plastic tape (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot').
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Width of a whipping, side by side: 'about equal to the diameter of the rope' (Ashley, ABOK p. 547, quoted by Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'; Animated Knots says the same for the West Country whipping); 'one and a half times wider than the rope is thick' (Wikipedia 'Common whipping'); 'approximately one and a half times the ropes diameter' (premiumropes).
  - Where to put it: a short distance, about one and a half rope diameters, from the end, then trim the rope end to no less than half the whipping's width beyond it (Wikipedia 'Common whipping'). The common whipping can go 'very close to the end of the rope, which is ideal if the end has already been melted' (Animated Knots).
  - Three to four recommended finishes and when to choose each (tying steps on Animated Knots): West Country whipping (ABOK #3458) when you want the easiest one with no needle, which fails slowly so you notice in time; common whipping (ABOK #3443) for a neat, quick finish with no visible ends, about 8 turns then 8 more over a loop of the twine, though it 'unravels very rapidly' if a turn is cut; sailmaker's whipping (ABOK #3446) for the most secure finish on ropes that are handled hard, with a needle on braided rope; back splice (ABOK #2813) for laid three-strand rope when you have no twine or tools, 'about three complete tucks', as long as the end needn't pass through blocks or holes (Animated Knots).
  - Melt and whip: 'nothing beats adding a properly applied whipping. If you value your ropes, whip and burn all their ends' (Animated Knots).
  - Heat sealing: a quick cut with a heated element 'provides very poor protection'; 'careful, prolonged heating' builds a thicker layer of solid plastic and protects better (Animated Knots). Use 'the cool (transparent) part of a butane lighter flame' (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'), or hold the blue part of the flame close to the end without touching it (Printables 926284).
  - Tape first: wrap the end in a few turns of plastic tape before fusing and cut through the tape for a neater, narrower end (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'); the same tape-and-cut start is used before gluing (Edelrid).
  - Hot knife: test the temperature on scrap first, cut larger ropes halfway from each side and then seal the ends, cut elastic bungee cord in one stroke at a slightly higher temperature so the cover and core fuse together, and cut on sheet metal or another heat-resistant surface (Practical Sailor).
  - Mixed-fibre and kernmantle ropes: where the core won't melt (aramid) whip short of the end, cut the core shorter than the sheath and melt the sheath over it (Animated Knots); for Dyneema or aramid cords, wrap tape, cut through it and soak the cut with thin superglue (Edelrid); 'Ropes with a braided core will always need a double-stitched whipping; a common whipping will slide off too easily' (premiumropes).
  - Liquid whipping: paint a ring where you will cut, let it dry, cut through the middle of the ring, then dip the end about 3/4 inch (about 19 mm) and leave it to dry overnight (Star brite directions).
  - Webbing ends: a 45° pointed tip 'is the easiest to feed through a buckle, but has the highest likelihood of fraying', and a square end 'is the least likely to fray' (iFixit 'Trim a Webbing Belt').
  - Heat-shrink aglet: cover only the frayed part, because tubing over sound lace 'could result in unwanted indents', and keep the flame moving all round the tube (iFixit 'How to Repair Frayed Shoelaces').

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - The plastic tip (aglet) on a shoelace or hoodie drawstring (Wikipedia 'Aglet')
  - The melted, hard end of a paracord bracelet, lanyard or tent guy line (Printables melting forms)
  - The sealed cut end of a backpack strap or webbing belt (iFixit 'Trim a Webbing Belt')
  - Twine whipping on the ends of boat mooring and dock lines (Animated Knots 'Rope Care'; Wikipedia 'Whipping knot')
- **when_to_use**: Every time you cut rope, cord, string, elastic cord or webbing, finish both ends before use; an end left 'un-whipped, unraveling and trailing in the water' is the classic neglect (Animated Knots). Melt the ends of synthetic cord and webbing for a quick finish, and for cords that must thread through printed holes or eyelets shape the tip in a melting form or add an aglet. Whip ropes you value, natural-fibre ropes that can't be melted, and ropes that are handled a lot (Animated Knots). Use a back splice on three-strand rope when you have no twine or tools, or where you want to feel the end by hand, 'as on a bucket lanyard' (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'). Use liquid whipping for many ends at once or for colour-coding (Star brite), and superglue for thin or slippery cords with mixed fibres (Edelrid).
- **when_not_to_use**: Don't heat-seal natural fibres (cotton, hemp) or aramid (Kevlar): they char instead of melting (Animated Knots; Ravenox); whip or glue them. Don't rely on melting alone for ropes run through your hands: a hardened, sharp end can cut (Budworth, quoted by Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'); whip them, or whip and melt. Don't use a back splice where the end must pass through a pulley, block, fairlead or printed hole, because it doubles the thickness (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'); use a whipping. Don't use a common whipping on ends that will be handled, since it 'can slip off the rope easily' (Wikipedia 'Common whipping'); use a sailmaker's whipping. Tape is only a 'temporary or emergency substitute' (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'). Don't use an open flame on unknown fibres or coated rope ('Do not improvise with an open flame around unknown fibers or coatings', Ravenox); follow the rope maker's method. A finish is never a repair for damaged rope (Ravenox): cut back to sound rope or replace it.
- **environment_limits**: Heat: HMPE (Dyneema-type) fibre melts at 149 °C and starts to degrade at a 'critical temperature' of 65.5 °C; polypropylene melts at 166 °C and polyethylene at 135 °C (SafeWork NSW), so a sealed end of these ropes softens near a hot engine or heater, and the same low melting point is why they seal easily. Abuse: a thin hot-cut seal 'does not survive compression or abuse' (Animated Knots). Flexing: where a whipping will flex a lot, soak it with dilute spar varnish or superglue (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot'). Weather: liquid whipping is sold as weather- and abrasion-resistant (Star brite); printed rope end caps for boats should be 'ideally opaque and UV-resistant' (Printables 'Rope End Caps (Aglets)'). Wet natural fibre: manila 'will shrink in length and expand in diameter when wet' (SafeWork NSW), which can loosen or tighten a whipping as it wets and dries ⚑ [uncertain: no source says what this does to a whipping]. Shaking and wear: the square knot ending a West Country whipping 'can shake loose' (Animated Knots). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **safety_notes**: Burns: flames, hot knives and soldering irons burn, and molten nylon is hot and sticky; one maker 'tried rolling the hot end with my fingers (and got burned)' (Printables 926284). A hot-knife blade 'remains hot for a few minutes after use' (Practical Sailor). Wear heat-resistant gloves (ideally thin leather) and work on a heat-resistant surface (Edelrid). Fumes: 'When melting and bonding synthetic fibers, toxic vapors are generated that should not be inhaled' (Edelrid), and 'Melting plastic on the hot knife causes noxious fumes' (iFixit); work outdoors or in a well-ventilated room (Edelrid). Particle filters don't remove the smell; vent outside or use a carbon filter (Practical Sailor). Sharp sealed ends: Geoffrey Budworth calls heat sealing 'lazy and dangerous' after a hardened rope end sliced a tugboat operator's palm 'down to the sinews' as it pulled through his grip (Wikipedia 'Whipping knot', citing The Knot Book, 1985, p. 37). Glue: superglue needs gloves and ventilation (Edelrid); one liquid whipping carries a California Prop 65 warning for ethylbenzene and lead (Star brite). Strength: a finish adds no strength, and no home-made knot, splice or finished rope should ever hold a person.
- **common_failures**: Whipping slides off or unravels: common whippings slide off easily and unravel quickly once a turn is cut (Wikipedia; Animated Knots); use a sailmaker's whipping, bury the ends with a needle, and melt the end too. West Country whipping loosens one half knot at a time (Animated Knots): retie it, or replace it with a better whipping. Melted end crumbles: a quick hot cut leaves too thin a layer (Animated Knots); heat longer and more evenly. Melted end has a fat bulb that won't fit through holes: shape it in a melting form (Printables 926284). End is black and charred: the flame touched the cord (Printables 926284); use the clear part of the flame. Core pulls back into the cover, or the cover slides off the core, on Dyneema- or aramid-cored rope: melting doesn't join different fibres (premiumropes; Edelrid); whip with stitches through the core or glue it. Back splice too bulky: use a whipping instead (Animated Knots). Tape peels off: it is only temporary (Wikipedia). Aglet slides off: glue it, or use a crimped metal aglet (Printables; Wikipedia 'Aglet'). Heat-shrink tip leaves dents in the lace: covered too much sound lace (iFixit).
- **related**: 
  - Stopper knots and cord-end anchors: stop a cord pulling out through a hole; a stopper doesn't stop fraying, so finish the end as well
  - Rope splices (eye splice, short splice, braided-rope splice): the back splice is the simplest splice; other splices make loops and joins
  - Binding knots and seizings (constrictor knot, seizing, cable lacing): a constrictor knot is the quick temporary stop before whipping; a seizing binds two ropes together rather than one end
  - Heat-shrink tubing (heat-shrink sleeve, adhesive-lined heat-shrink): the same tubing makes a quick aglet
  - Heat-sealed seam (heat-sealed plastic film and coated fabric): melting thermoplastic to join two pieces, rather than to seal one end
  - Cyanoacrylate (superglue): thin superglue soaks into and stiffens a cut end
  - Wire-end ferrule (bootlace ferrule, end sleeve): the electrical version, a metal tube crimped over the strands of a wire end
  - Lacing (cord through rows of holes or eyelets): aglets and slim melted tips make laces easy to thread
  - Sewn seams and hand stitches: folding and stitching a hem is the sewn way to stop a fabric or webbing edge fraying
  - Cord lock (spring toggle): often used together with a cord-end cap or a knot on a drawstring end

## 386 Sewn seams and hand stitches (lockstitch, saddle stitch, flat-felled seam)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Joining two pieces of fabric or leather with thread passed through both, by machine (lockstitch) or by hand (running, back or saddle stitch), along a seam folded or bound so the edges don't fray

*Also covers:* Also covers plain, French, lapped and bound seams, seam finishes, casings and pole sleeves, and seam taping and sealing. Heat-welded seams are Heat-sealed seam

### What it is

- **plain_name**: Joining two pieces of cloth or leather by passing thread back and forth through both of them, with a sewing machine or a needle by hand, and folding or wrapping the cut edges so they don't fray
- **term**: Sewn seam (stitched seam), made with a lockstitch, chain stitch or overlock by machine, or a running, back or saddle stitch by hand
- **aliases**: 
  - seam: 'the join where two or more layers of fabric, leather, or other materials are held together with stitches' (Wikipedia 'Seam (sewing)')
  - lockstitch, straight stitch, 'single needle stitching' (Wikipedia 'Lockstitch'); the FAA calls it the '301 lockstitch' and the zigzag versions stitch types 304 and 308 (FAA Parachute Rigger Handbook)
  - chain stitch (ISO 4915 stitch 101) and double chain stitch (Wikipedia 'Chain stitch')
  - overlock, serging, overedging or merrowing (Wikipedia 'Overlock')
  - running stitch or straight stitch; basting or tacking stitch (long temporary running stitches) (Wikipedia 'Running stitch')
  - backstitch (Wikipedia 'Backstitch')
  - saddle stitch, hand stitching or two-needle stitching (a variant is ISO 4915 stitch 201) (Wikipedia; Tandy Leather)
  - plain seam, French seam, flat or abutted seam, flat-felled seam (true flat-felled, faux flat-felled), lapped seam, bound seam, Hong Kong finish, French/felled hybrid (Wikipedia 'Seam (sewing)'; Ripstop by the Roll)
  - seam finish: pinking, zigzag, overcasting, overlocking, bias binding (Wikipedia)
  - seam allowance, also called inlay (Wikipedia 'Seam allowance')
  - casing, channel, hem tunnel, drawstring channel, pole sleeve, pole pocket (Wikipedia 'Drawstring'; Ripstop by the Roll; Banners.com)
  - seam sealing and seam taping; seam sealers such as Seam Grip WP, FC and SIL, or SilNet (brands) (Gear Aid; Six Moon Designs)
  - topstitch, rolled hem (Ripstop by the Roll)
  - standard: ASTM D6193 'Standard Practice for Stitches and Seams' (Wikipedia)
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware, seals & pass-throughs
- **one_liner**: Two pieces of fabric or leather are held together by a line of thread stitched through both, by machine (usually a lockstitch) or by hand (running, back or saddle stitch), with the cut edges folded in, wrapped or overstitched so they can't fray.
- **complexity**: A few parts: the two pieces of fabric or leather, the thread, and sometimes a binding strip, seam tape or seam sealer. Tools: a needle (two for saddle stitching), or a sewing machine with a needle and a bobbin; for leather an awl, pricking iron, stitching groover, mallet and stitching pony (Tandy Leather).
- **how_it_works**: Thread passes through both layers again and again, so the layers can't slide apart without breaking or pulling out many stitches: they interlock (a form-fit). A machine lockstitch uses two threads, one from the needle and one from a bobbin underneath, which 'lock' (entwine) together inside the hole in the fabric, ideally in the middle of its thickness (Wikipedia 'Lockstitch'). A hand saddle stitch uses one thread with a needle at each end, sewn through the same line of holes from both sides, so if one side breaks only that side loosens (Wikipedia 'Saddle stitch'). The seam type decides where the raw cut edges go: a plain seam leaves them inside and needs a seam finish; French and flat-felled seams fold them inside the seam so they are fully enclosed (Wikipedia 'Seam (sewing)'). The needle holes still let water through, so seams in waterproof fabric are sealed with a liquid sealer or a heat-applied tape (Six Moon Designs; Extremtextil).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: joins two pieces of fabric, leather or webbing along a line
  - seal it, or pass cables or fluid through: a seam-sealed or taped seam in coated fabric keeps rain out (Six Moon Designs; Gear Aid)
  - let it move (turn, slide or bend): a casing or pole sleeve is a sewn tunnel that a drawstring, elastic cord or rod slides through (Wikipedia 'Drawstring')
- **motion**: flex: the joined fabric stays soft and bends at the seam; nothing slides, except a drawstring or pole running through a sewn casing
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 between the joined edges, which are held together along the seam line; the cloth itself still bends and drapes freely.
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - none: a seam simply holds. Two related points: a broken saddle stitch 'loosens only one side of the stitch' (Wikipedia 'Saddle stitch'), while a single-thread chain stitch 'is easily unravelled', which is used on purpose to close sacks that are torn open with one pull (Wikipedia 'Chain stitch')
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the thread passes through holes in both layers and is locked in place, so the layers can't separate without the thread breaking or tearing out
  - material bond (glue, solvent or melting): only for the waterproofing step, where a seam sealer or heat-applied seam tape bonds over the stitches (Gear Aid; Six Moon Designs)
- **input_output**: not applicable: a seam joins fabric and passes no motion on
- **power**: passive (no power needed). A sewing machine needs power only while sewing.
- **removability**: semi-permanent: stitches can be picked out with a seam ripper (FAA Parachute Rigger Handbook lists it among the rigger's tools), but the needle holes stay in the fabric or leather; a sealed or taped seam is effectively permanent.
- **reuse_cycles**: a few times: a seam can be unpicked and resewn, but each time leaves more holes and the thread is replaced
- **tools_needed**: Hand: needle, thread, scissors, pins or sewing clips. Machine: a lockstitch sewing machine (or an overlocker for finishes) and a seam ripper; an iron to press seam allowances flat (Wikipedia 'Seam (sewing)'). Leather saddle stitching: two needles, waxed thread, a stitching groover, a pricking iron and mallet, an awl and a stitching pony (Tandy Leather). A hand sewing awl makes a lockstitch by hand (Wikipedia 'Lockstitch'). Sealing: a small brush or applicator and seam sealer (Six Moon Designs).
- **seal**: none for an ordinary seam: the needle holes leak. French and flat-felled seams give 'enhanced' water resistance (Ripstop by the Roll), cotton-wrapped (corespun) thread swells when wet to help close the holes (Extremtextil), and the seam becomes watertight only when it is sealed with a compatible liquid sealer or seam tape (Six Moon Designs; Gear Aid).
- **load**: Medium. A well-designed seam can be stronger than the fabric: for parachute canopies 'The strength of the seam needs to be greater than the strength of the fabric' (FAA Parachute Rigger Handbook, p. 2-25). It is strongest when pulled along the fabric (the stitches share the load in shear) and weakest at a single point: concentrated pulls make seams in slippery fabric 'pull apart under heavy strain' (Extremtextil). Thread strengths, side by side: the FAA lists V-T-295 nylon thread as E 8.5 lb, FF 16 lb, 3-cord 24 lb and 5-cord 40 lb (p. 3-14), but in its harness-failure example (p. 5-19) gives '45 pounds for 5-cord nylon', against about 10 lb for the cotton thread wrongly used. For dense load-bearing joints in straps, see 'Load-bearing stitch patterns'. Never use a home-sewn seam to hold a person.
- **adjustable**: no: a seam is fixed once sewn. A drawstring in a sewn casing adjusts how tightly the fabric is gathered (Wikipedia 'Drawstring').
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable for motion, but accuracy matters: a consistent seam allowance and pressing before the final stitching give a consistent finished seam (Wikipedia 'Seam (sewing)'); thread tension should place the lock in the middle of the material (Wikipedia 'Lockstitch').
- **access_needed**: both sides: the needle passes through and back, so you need both faces of the work (under the presser foot of a machine, or reachable by hand); a hand sewing awl still needs both sides.
- **materials**: Joins woven and knitted fabrics, canvas, leather, webbing, coated fabrics (silicone-coated silnylon and silpoly, polyurethane-coated nylon and polyester) and laminates (X-Pac, Dyneema Composite Fabric; brands). Laminates don't 'self-heal' round needle holes the way woven fabric does, so use longer stitches (Ripstop by the Roll). Thread: UV-treated polyester is 'the most common thread for outdoor applications', though it fades and loses strength in long sun; nylon is very strong but not recommended outdoors; PTFE or fluoropolymer 'lifetime' threads are unaffected by UV (Sailrite). Waxed polyester, linen or hemp thread for saddle stitching leather (Tandy Leather). Cotton thread is weak for load-bearing work (FAA). Rigid 3D prints can't be sewn through, except thin TPU; use sewing holes instead (see 'Sew-on part').
- **maker_difficulty**: easy to moderate: a hand running or back stitch and a plain machine seam are beginner work ('Technical Difficulty: Beginner', Ripstop by the Roll); French seams and saddle stitching need practice; Ripstop by the Roll rates the flat-felled seam 'Pro'.

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - hand sewing: running, back, overcast and saddle stitches with a needle, or a lockstitch with a sewing awl
  - machine sewing: a home lockstitch machine for straight, zigzag and stretch stitches; an overlocker (serger) for seam finishes
  - off-the-shelf part: thread, needles, bias binding, seam tape and seam sealer
  - 3D print (FDM): printed sewing aids rather than the seam itself: magnetic seam guides, bias tape makers, sewing clips, hemming feet, leather stitching markers and stitching ponies (Printables models below)
- **print_in_place**: no: a seam is sewn, not printed. Printed sewing aids are separate tools; a printed sewing clip uses a scrap of filament as its hinge pin and a piece of silicone as its spring (Printables 'Sewing Clip').
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: The seam itself can't be printed, and rigid prints can't be sewn through (thin TPU is the exception); to sew fabric to a print use sewing holes, slots or loops, covered in 'Sew-on part', or print onto the fabric ('Printing onto fabric'). Useful printed aids: 1) Magnetic seam guide: a printed fence held to the machine bed by magnets keeps the seam allowance even; one uses four glued-in 8 x 3 mm magnets and a notch to clear the plate screw (Printables 'Sewing Machine Seam Guide'), another has two slots for 10 x 2 mm (or other) magnets, printed in PLA+ at 0.2 mm layers with no supports, with an OpenSCAD file to resize it (Printables 'Sewing Machine Seam Guide with Double Magnet Slot'). Check the machine maker's advice before putting magnets near an electronic machine ⚑ [uncertain: no source opened on this]. 2) Bias tape maker: pulls a fabric strip through a printed funnel to fold it into binding for bound seams; one 5 cm version prints in its saved orientation with no supports (Printables 'Bias Tape Maker 5cm'). 3) Sewing clips: hold slippery layers together without pin holes, which matters on coated fabrics, where pins should go only in the seam allowance (Extremtextil); a printed clip uses under 1 g of PLA at 0.2 mm (Printables 'Sewing Clip'). 4) Narrow hemming foot: printed 'left side down to have a smooth bottom which won't snag the textile', with supports everywhere (Printables 'Sewing Machine Narrow Hemming Foot'). 5) Leather: fixed-width stitching markers (2-5 mm) mark the stitch line like wing dividers, though a PLA tip wears (Printables 'Leather stitching marker'); a printed stitching pony works for small items, but its designer says 'you're probably better off buying a wooden one' (Printables 'Stitching Pony for Leather'); a printed sewing awl handle takes standard awl needles (Printables 'Sewing Awl'). 6) Orientation: print tools that take load so the force runs along the layers, since FFF prints 'are stronger in one direction than another' (Prusa). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search for: 'UV bonded polyester thread' (outdoor work), 'bonded nylon thread' (indoor upholstery), 'PTFE thread' or 'lifetime thread' (Sailrite); 'corespun thread' and 'Microtex needle' (silnylon; Extremtextil); 'waxed polyester thread', 'linen thread', 'pricking iron', 'stitching chisel', 'stitching groover', 'stitching pony', 'harness needles' (leather; Tandy Leather); 'sewing awl' (hand lockstitch); 'double-fold bias tape' (bound seams; Wikipedia); 'seam ripper'; 'seam sealer' for the matching coating (silicone or polyurethane) and 'seam tape' (Gear Aid; Six Moon Designs); 'sewing clips' (Extremtextil names Wonder Clips (brand)).
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Seam allowance: home sewing patterns use 1/4 to 5/8 inch (6.4 to 15.9 mm); 5/8 inch (1.5 cm) makes sure fabrics that ravel easily are 'safely caught'; industry uses 1/4 inch on curves and hidden seams up to 1 inch or more where fitting is needed; curved seams take smaller allowances (Wikipedia 'Seam allowance').
  - French seam, two sets of figures side by side: sew 1/4 inch (0.6 cm) wrong sides together, trim to 1/8 inch (0.3 cm), press, then sew 1/4 inch right sides together (Wikipedia 'Seam (sewing)'); Ripstop by the Roll starts with a basting stitch 1/8-1/4 inch from the edge and allows 3/4-2 inches of seam allowance.
  - Which seam to choose (Ripstop by the Roll strength ranking): simple (plain) seam 'Mediocre', exposed edges; rolled hem for drawstring channels and hammocks; French seam 'Stronger that a simple seam, weaker than flat felled, and hybrid', for apparel; flat-felled seam for tarps, bags and apparel; French/felled hybrid 'Strongest', for bags and packs. On silnylon, Extremtextil found that a double flat seam 'has proven to be the best type of seam'.
  - Stitch length: about 8 stitches per inch is average; use a longer stitch (about 4-8 per inch) on laminates that don't self-heal (Ripstop by the Roll); parachute repairs use 7-11 stitches per inch (FAA); 'Too many stitches per inch dramatically affects the strength of the seam by perforating the material' (FAA Parachute Rigger Handbook, p. 2-25).
  - Thread and needle: the lighter the fabric, the smaller the thread and needle, so the needle doesn't damage the weave (FAA, p. 2-25); for silnylon use a thin, sharp Microtex or jeans needle, size 70 or 80 with Tex-75 corespun thread (Extremtextil). Outdoors use UV-treated polyester or PTFE thread, not nylon (Sailrite).
  - Saddle stitch: cut thread 'about five times longer than the segment' to be stitched, finish with two or three backstitches, then melt polyester thread ends or set linen and hemp ends with a drop of glue (Tandy Leather). Tandy says to trim poly thread '1/8" (1 mm)' from the work; these figures don't agree (1/8 inch is about 3 mm), so treat them as 'a few millimetres'.
  - Repairs: overstitch a broken seam at least 4-6 inches (100-150 mm) beyond each end of the damage (FAA Parachute Rigger Handbook, p. 7-15).
  - Seam sealing: 'only one thing sticks to silicone: silicone', so silicone-coated fabric needs a silicone sealer, and polyurethane sealers and seam tapes won't stick to it (Extremtextil; Six Moon Designs). Where to seal, side by side: Extremtextil seals pure seams such as ridge seams from the outside and reinforcements from the inside; Six Moon Designs says its butt seams 'must be sealed from the outside'; Gear Aid recommends sealing from the inside 'mainly for aesthetic reasons'. A home-made silicone sealer is one part mineral spirits to one part silicone caulk, cured 8-12 hours (Six Moon Designs).
  - Point loads on light fabric: eyelets are 'generally unsuitable' on silnylon because the pull can't spread; sew on webbing or ribbed tape loops 15-25 mm wide over rounded reinforcement patches instead (Extremtextil).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - The inside leg seam of jeans, a flat-felled seam made on a double-needle machine (Wikipedia 'Seam (sewing)')
  - The overlocked seam edges inside shop-bought clothes: on mass-produced clothing, plain seams are 'usually finished with an overlock stitch' (Wikipedia 'Seam (sewing)')
  - The saddle-stitched edge of a leather wallet, belt or dog collar (Wikipedia 'Saddle stitch')
  - The casing round the top of a drawstring bag or the waist of tracksuit trousers, and the taped seams inside a rain jacket or tent fly (Wikipedia 'Drawstring'; Six Moon Designs)
- **when_to_use**: Whenever you join fabric, leather or webbing and want the join to stay soft and flexible: bags, pouches, covers, tarps, flags, clothing, padding and fabric parts of a project. Use a machine lockstitch for long seams, hand running or back stitches for short repairs (backstitch 'strongly and permanently attaches two pieces of fabric', Wikipedia 'Backstitch'), and a saddle stitch for leather, where it ends up 'stronger than a machine stitch' and a cut thread doesn't unravel the whole line (Tandy Leather). Choose an enclosed seam (French or flat-felled) for fabrics that fray easily, things that are washed often, or outdoor gear (Ripstop by the Roll). Sew a casing or pole sleeve to carry a drawstring, elastic or a rod (Ripstop by the Roll's rolled hem 'Drawstring, channel').
- **when_not_to_use**: Not for anything that holds a person (harnesses, climbing slings, swings): use rated, professionally made gear; the FAA describes a harness that failed because cotton thread was used instead of the required nylon (FAA Parachute Rigger Handbook, p. 5-19). Not for heavy load in straps by plain seams: use a load-bearing stitch pattern (see that entry). Not for welded fabrics: plastic film and coated fabrics that must be fully airtight or watertight are better heat-sealed (see 'Heat-sealed seam'). Not directly into rigid 3D prints: use sewing holes or loops ('Sew-on part') or print onto the fabric. Where no sewing is wanted, iron-on fusible web or a flexible fabric glue can join fabric, though they can peel or stiffen (see those entries). Avoid a chain stitch where a broken thread must not unravel the seam (Wikipedia 'Chain stitch'). Avoid nylon thread for anything that lives in the sun (Sailrite).
- **environment_limits**: Sunlight: nylon thread 'does not have the UV resistance of polyester thread and will not last as long outside'; polyester also loses 'much of its strength' after long sun exposure and its colours fade to white; PTFE lifetime threads are 'unaffected by exposure to UV rays' (Sailrite). Wet: silnylon 'stretches slightly when wet', so allow for re-tensioning (Extremtextil); cotton-wrapped thread swells to help seal holes (Extremtextil). Sealers: Seam Grip WP 'is unaffected by heat or solvents', while the water-based Seam Grip FC 'can be broken down by solvents' (Gear Aid); sealers need curing time, 8-12 hours or more in cold, humid weather (Six Moon Designs). Abrasion: cutting a groove for leather stitches keeps them flush and protects them from friction wear (Tandy Leather).
- **safety_notes**: Sharp tools: needles, awls, pricking irons struck with a mallet, knives and rotary cutters can stab or cut; keep fingers clear of a sewing machine's needle while it runs ⚑ [uncertain: no source opened gives sewing-machine safety rules]. Heat: melting polyester thread ends with a lighter (Tandy warns 'Be careful not to burn your project') and hot-knife cutting of synthetic fabric can burn; a hot-knife blade stays hot for minutes after use (Practical Sailor). Fumes: work in a well-ventilated area when using solvent-thinned seam sealers (Six Moon Designs uses mineral spirits). Drawstrings: drawstrings in children's clothing can catch and strangle; the US CPSC issued guidelines in 1996 after reports of deaths (Wikipedia 'Drawstring'), so don't put hood or neck drawstrings in casings on children's clothes. Magnets: printed magnetic seam guides contain small strong magnets, which are dangerous if swallowed; keep them away from small children. Never trust a home-sewn seam to hold a person. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **common_failures**: Raw edges fray and the seam pulls out: use a seam finish (overlock, zigzag, pinking, binding) or an enclosed seam (Wikipedia 'Seam (sewing)'). Seam puckers or 'draws': both thread tensions too tight; skipped stitches: machine out of time (FAA troubleshooting chart, p. 6-23). Lock not in the middle of the fabric: upper and lower tensions unbalanced (Wikipedia 'Lockstitch'). Too many stitches per inch perforate and weaken the fabric (FAA). Slippery coated fabric pulls apart at the seam: sew through several layers (rolled hem, double flat seam) and spread loads (Extremtextil). Seam leaks: seal it with the right sealer for the coating; the wrong one won't stick (Extremtextil; Six Moon Designs). One broken machine stitch lets the line unravel ('if one side of a stitch is broken the entire length of the stitch will be compromised', Tandy Leather): backstitch the ends and use a saddle stitch for leather. Thread rots in the sun: use UV-treated polyester or PTFE (Sailrite). An extra layer of fabric caught in the seam by mistake (FAA, a 'common mistake' with three panels meeting).
- **related**: 
  - Load-bearing stitch patterns for straps and webbing (bar tack, box-X stitch): the dense stitch blocks for straps that carry real load
  - Sew-on part (sewing holes, loops and slots on a rigid part): how to sew fabric onto a 3D-printed or rigid part
  - Heat-sealed seam (heat-sealed plastic film and coated fabric): joins coated fabric or film by melting, with no needle holes to seal
  - Iron-on fusible web (heat-activated adhesive web, hem tape): the no-sew way to hem or join fabric with heat
  - Elastomeric solvent glue (flexible craft glue) and contact adhesive: glued fabric joins instead of stitches
  - Folded seam (hem, lock seam, Pittsburgh lock): the sheet-metal version of a folded, enclosed seam
  - Lacing (cord through rows of holes or eyelets): a cord through holes that can be undone and re-tightened, unlike a sewn seam
  - Conductive thread (sewn circuit, e-textile stitching): stitching that carries electric current
  - Cord lock (spring toggle): holds the drawstring that runs in a sewn casing
  - Bead-in-groove fabric edge (keder, boltrope, screen spline): a sewn-in or welded bead that slides into a track
  - Printing onto fabric (fabric embedded in a 3D print): joins plastic to fabric without sewing
  - Rope-end finishing (whipping, heat-sealing, back splice, aglet): melting or hemming the end of a strap or tape to stop fraying

## 387 Lacing (cord through rows of holes or eyelets)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* A cord or leather thong threaded back and forth through rows of holes, eyelets or hooks on two edges to pull them together, as on shoes, leather pouches or a cover laced onto a frame, and loosened and tightened again later

*Also covers:* Also covers criss-cross and edge lacing, lace length, and lacing printed panels together. Cable lacing for wire looms is in Binding knots and seizings.

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A cord, shoelace, elastic cord or leather strip threaded back and forth through a row of holes, metal rings or hooks along two edges, then pulled tight to draw the edges together, like the laces on a shoe; you can loosen it, open it and tighten it again
- **term**: Lacing (laced joint: a lace or thong passed through holes, eyelets or hooks on two edges)
- **aliases**: 
  - lace-up, lacing, shoelacing, bootlacing (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces')
  - criss-cross lacing, straight-bar (bar) lacing, bowtie lacing, checkerboard lacing, cobwebbing (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'; Polster, Nature 2002)
  - edge lacing, leather lacing or thonging: running stitch, double running stitch, loop stitch and cross whip stitch (e-scoutcraft); whipstitch and double loop lacing (Tandy Leather)
  - drawstring: a lace pulled through a casing or 'laced through holes, which may be lined with eyelets'; 'A shoelace is a drawstring' (Wikipedia 'Drawstring')
  - corset lacing, including 'bunny ears' lacing from the middle (Wikipedia 'Corset')
  - lamellar lacing: small plates 'punched and laced together, typically in horizontal rows' (Wikipedia 'Lamellar armour')
  - marling: a row of marling hitches, 'a knot that is used in series to lash long bundles (as rolled or folded awnings, hammocks, or sails)' (Merriam-Webster)
  - shock-cord lacing of covers over lacing knobs, cover clips or hooks; e.g. Stayput (brand) (Sailrite)
  - lace hooks, speed hooks, lace locks, lace-locking systems (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces')
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware, tensioning, reels & take-up
- **one_liner**: A lace is threaded back and forth through matching holes, eyelets or hooks on two edges and pulled tight, so many short crossings share the pull and draw the edges together; a knot, bow or lace lock holds it, and it can be loosened and re-tightened at any time.
- **complexity**: A few parts: the lace (shoelace, cord, paracord, elastic shock cord, leather thong or waxed thread), a row of holes on each edge, often lined with eyelets or grommets or replaced by hooks or knobs, aglets on the lace ends, and a knot, bow, cord lock or lace lock to hold the tension (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'; Wikipedia 'Grommet'; Sailrite).
- **how_it_works**: Each pass of the lace across the gap between the two edges pulls them towards each other, and pulling on the lace ends shares that pull among all the crossings, so a long opening closes evenly (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'). The holes are the anchor points: an eyelet or grommet lining a hole 'prevents the cord from tearing through the hole' (Wikipedia 'Grommet'). Friction where the lace bends through each hole helps it stay tight while you tie it off; a knot, bow or lace lock then holds the ends, and a bow 'can be readily loosened by pulling one or both of the loose ends' (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'). In leather edge lacing the lace instead wraps over the edge and through holes in two layers, binding them like a coarse seam that is usually left in place (e-scoutcraft).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: holds two edges of leather, fabric, sheet or printed panels together
  - tighten and hold tension: pulling the lace draws the edges together, and a knot, cord lock or lace lock holds the tension (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces')
  - lock, and release by hand: untie the bow or open the lace lock and loosen the lace to open it again
- **motion**: none once tied: the edges are held; while you adjust it, the lace slides through the holes
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 between the laced edges once tight (they can still bend, as leather and fabric do); loosening the lace lets the edges separate again.
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds): pulling the lace takes up slack along the whole opening, and a knot, cord lock or lace lock holds it
  - quick-release: a bow (slipped reef knot) comes undone with a pull on a loose end (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces')
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the lace passes through holes or round hooks in both edges, so the edges can't separate without the lace
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): friction in the holes and in the knot, or the grip of a cord lock or lace lock, holds the tension; a knot holds because the crimped lace 'cannot make itself smaller and slide though the knot' (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces')
- **input_output**: not applicable: lacing joins edges and passes no motion on
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable: shoe-type lacing is untied and unthreaded by hand with no tools (tool-free quick release when tied with a bow). Leather edge lacing is semi-permanent: its ends are tied off and glued, or tucked under the loops (e-scoutcraft).
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: the edges and eyelets last; the lace wears and is replaced when it frays or its aglets break
- **tools_needed**: None to lace, tighten or untie. To make the holes: a hole punch, or a thonging (lacing) chisel driven with a mallet on a hardwood block, and a large-eyed needle for the lace (e-scoutcraft); an eyelet or grommet setting punch, or a press with punch and anvil (Wikipedia 'Grommet'); a screwdriver for screw-on lacing knobs and hooks (Sailrite).
- **seal**: none: there are gaps between the crossings, and nothing makes it seal ⚑ [uncertain: a flap behind the lacing, like a shoe's tongue, can cover the gap, but no opened source discusses it] *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **load**: Light to medium, shared along all the holes. The weak points are the lace itself and the hole edges, which tear out first in thin material; eyelets or grommets stop the cord tearing through (Wikipedia 'Grommet'), and in very light fabric such as silnylon eyelets are 'generally unsuitable' because the pull can't spread, so sewn-on webbing loops are used instead (Extremtextil). Corners carry the most stress on a laced-on banner, so grommets go in every corner first (Banners.com). Strong in pulling the edges together; it can't push them apart or stop them flexing. Criss-cross and straight lacing are the strongest shoe lacings (Polster, Nature 2002). Never use home-made lacing to hold a person.
- **adjustable**: yes: tighten or loosen the lace at any time, and change the lacing pattern to adjust the fit (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'). A corset could be left 'as adjusted' and taken on and off by a separate front opening (busk) (Wikipedia 'Corset'), a good pattern for any laced cover: set the lacing once and open it some other way.
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable for location; the edges are only held as tightly as the lace is pulled, and laces stretch and settle, so laced covers and panels may need re-tightening ⚑ [uncertain: no source opened measures this]. Elastic laces with fat 'knotty' sections can set different tensions along the opening (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **access_needed**: both sides to thread the lace through the holes the first time; afterwards tightening, tying and untying are done from the outside only
- **materials**: Laces: cotton, jute, hemp or leather traditionally; modern laces 'often incorporate various synthetic fibers', which are more slippery and come undone more easily but wear and rot less (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'); polyester is the most common lace yarn (same); flat laces 'stay tied more securely than those with a round cross-section' (same). Leather lacing about 1/8 inch (3 mm) wide, waxed thread or craft strips (e-scoutcraft). Elastic shock cord for covers (Sailrite). Edges: leather, canvas and fabric (with eyelets or grommets of metal, plastic or rubber; Wikipedia 'Grommet'), vinyl banners (Banners.com), plates of iron, steel, leather, bone or bronze in lamellar armour (Wikipedia 'Lamellar armour'), and 3D-printed panels in PLA, PETG or TPU (Printables models below).
- **maker_difficulty**: easy: holes and a cord; the only care is even hole spacing, a smooth edge to each hole, and enough lace

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - leatherwork: holes punched or chiselled with a thonging chisel, laced by hand (e-scoutcraft)
  - off-the-shelf part: laces, leather lace, eyelets and grommets, lace hooks, lace locks, shock cord, lacing knobs and cover clips
  - 3D print (FDM): rows of holes in printed panels or plates, printed eyelets, hooks and lace locks (Printables models below)
  - laser cut or CNC: rows of holes in sheet, leather or panels ⚑ [uncertain: no source opened describes laser-cut lacing holes] *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **print_in_place**: no: the holes print with the part, but the lace is threaded afterwards; printed lace locks and eyelets are separate small parts
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Hole size: make each hole big enough for the lace and its aglet or melted tip to pass; one printed lace lock is chosen by measuring the aglet diameter (Printables 'Springless Lace Lock (Parametric)', sizes for 2-8 mm laces), and another uses 4.1 mm holes, which it says fit 'almost all lace types, flat or round' (Printables 'Side Lace Lock'). For holes in printed parts more generally (sizes, edge distance, deburring), see 'Sew-on part' and 'Stopper knots and cord-end anchors'. 2) Edge distance and walls: leave a solid bridge of plastic between hole and edge; walls 'thinner than one nozzle perimeter are not printable' (Prusa), so allow several perimeters. 3) Orientation: print panels flat, so the holes run vertically and the pull of the lace runs along the layers, because FFF prints 'are stronger in one direction than another' (Prusa). 4) Flexible plates: one set of lamellar plates is printed in TPU (slow, 4 walls, 20% gyroid infill) and tied together with paracord (Printables 'Lamellar Armor Plates for HEMA/SCA/LARP'); flexible plates take a laced row's bending better than rigid ones ⚑ [uncertain: inferred, not tested by a source]. Treat printed plates as costume, not tested protective gear. 5) Printed eyelets and hooks: a printed shoe eyelet comes in glue-in and click-in versions, printed in PLA at 0.1 mm layers with 20% infill (Printables 'Shoe Eyelet'); a replacement boot lace hook printed in a polycarbonate blend and fixed with a 2 x 8 mm self-tapping screw had worked 'for over a year' (Printables 'shoe lace hook'). 6) Lace locks print without supports in PLA, PETG or TPU (Printables 'Side Lace Lock'); one maker says to print theirs in PETG (Printables 'Shoelaces lacing system'). 7) Smooth hole mouths: chamfer or sand hole edges so the lace doesn't fray on sharp printed edges ⚑ [uncertain: no source tested lace wear on printed holes]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search for: 'shoelaces' (flat, round, waxed, elastic), 'leather lace' or 'leather thong', 'lacing needle', 'thonging chisel' or 'lacing chisel' (e-scoutcraft; Tandy Leather); 'eyelets', 'grommets' and an 'eyelet setting tool' or 'grommet press' (Wikipedia 'Grommet'); 'lace hooks' or 'speed hooks' (boots and skates; Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'); 'lace locks' and 'cord locks' (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'); 'aglets'; 'shock cord' (bungee cord), 'shock cord lacing knob', 'shock cord cover clip', 'shock cord hook' and 'tent wall hook' for laced covers, e.g. Stayput (brand) (Sailrite); 'bungee balls' for banners (Banners.com).
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Leather edge lacing holes: 'close to the edge (1/16" to 1/8")' (about 1.5-3 mm) 'and evenly spaced'; drive a thonging chisel through the work on a hardwood block and put its first prong in the last hole to keep the spacing even (e-scoutcraft).
  - How much leather lace, by stitch: running stitch about 1.5 times the length to be laced, double running stitch about 3 times, loop stitch about 3.5 times and cross whip stitch about 6 times (e-scoutcraft). Double loop lacing: 'about 7 times the length of the edge you're lacing' (a leatherworker.net member) or '7-8 times the length of the item being stitched' (a Tandy guide quoted on the same forum thread). Lace in arm-length pieces of about 5-6 ft and splice, rather than one long piece that wears and stretches as you pull it through (same thread).
  - Shoelace length, as a rough guide (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'): 2 holes 45 cm, 3: 65 cm, 4: 75-85 cm, 5: 85-90 cm, 6: 100 cm, 7: 110 cm, 8: 120 cm, 10: 130 cm, 12: 150 cm, 14: 180 cm, 16: 200 cm ⚑ [uncertain: the table is headed 'No of holes' and doesn't say whether it means holes or pairs of eyelets].
  - Which shoe-type lacing to choose: criss-cross lacing is 'The most common lacing method' and 'one of the strongest and most efficient' (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'); criss-cross and straight lacing are the strongest, while bowtie lacing is the shortest (Polster, Nature 2002); straight-bar lacing suits closed (Oxford-style) openings, where the crossovers of criss-cross lacing 'prevent the sides of the shoe from coming together in the middle' (Wikipedia); checkerboard lacing is 'very difficult to tighten or loosen' (Wikipedia).
  - Tying off: a bow tied as a reef knot holds; tied as a granny knot it 'is much less secure'; if the loops lie across the opening it is probably a reef knot, if they lie along it a granny (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'). Double-wrapped bows hold better (same). See 'Basic knots' for the reef knot (ABOK #1204).
  - Lacing a banner or cover through grommets: a grommet in every corner first, since corners 'carry the most stress', then every 24-36 inches (about 0.6-0.9 m) along the edge, or every 18-24 inches in windy places, 'since wind load pulls harder on wider gaps'; a double-folded hem 'keeps the grommet from tearing out under load' (Banners.com).
  - Leather lace care: slightly dampen leather lace to stop it twisting, and keep the tension even to avoid uneven loops (Tandy Leather). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Laced shoes and boots, with aglets on the lace ends and eyelets or hooks on the sides (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces')
  - A leather pouch, wallet or knife sheath with a laced edge (Tandy Leather; e-scoutcraft)
  - A boat, trailer or barbecue cover held down with shock cord laced over hooks or knobs (Sailrite)
  - A banner laced to a frame or fence with rope or bungee cord through its grommets (Banners.com)
- **when_to_use**: When two edges must be pulled together firmly but opened again later, or when the fit must be adjustable: footwear, gaiters, bags and pouches, corsets and other fitted clothing (Wikipedia 'Corset'), covers laced onto a frame or a boat, banners and screens on frames, rolled sails, awnings and hammocks lashed with a row of marling hitches (Merriam-Webster), and printed panels, plates or costume armour joined into a flexible sheet (Wikipedia 'Lamellar armour'). It is cheap, needs no special hardware, spreads the pull along many points, and is easy to repair with a new lace. For leather, edge lacing is a decorative and durable way to close an edge (Tandy Leather).
- **when_not_to_use**: For quick, frequent opening with one hand, a zipper, hook-and-loop, buckle or a ratcheting reel (dial) closure is faster (see those entries). For a permanent, flat, flexible join use a sewn seam. For thin, slippery fabric such as silnylon, avoid eyelets and sew on webbing loops instead (Extremtextil). For binding wire looms use cable lacing (see 'Binding knots and seizings'). Not for anything that holds or protects a person: lacing and printed plates are not rated or tested for that. Not for children's hood or neck drawstrings, which are a strangulation hazard (Wikipedia 'Drawstring').
- **environment_limits**: Moisture: natural-fibre laces can rot, while 'smooth synthetic shoelaces ... are less susceptible to rotting from moisture' (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'). Slippery synthetic laces come undone more easily; waxing and silicone finishes increase friction (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'). Wind: wind load pulls harder on wider gaps between grommets, so space them closer outdoors (Banners.com). Silnylon 'stretches slightly when wet', so laced or tensioned silnylon may sag and need re-tensioning (Extremtextil). Sunlight and heat: synthetic cords and printed panels (especially PLA) weaken in sun and soften in heat ⚑ [uncertain: no opened source gives figures for laces]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **safety_notes**: Drawstrings and long laces on children's clothing can catch on playground equipment, bus doors or cribs and strangle; the US CPSC issued guidelines in 1996 and recorded 22 deaths and 48 non-fatal incidents from 1985 to 1999 (Wikipedia 'Drawstring'). Untied or loose laces can trip people, which is why lace-locking systems are used in sport (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'). Tensioned shock cord that slips off a hook can snap back towards the face ⚑ [uncertain: no source opened]. Chisels, punches and mallets used to make lacing holes can cut or crush fingers ⚑ [uncertain]. Never use home-made lacing to hold or protect a person; printed plates laced into 'armour' are costume, not protective equipment. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **common_failures**: Comes undone: a granny knot instead of a reef knot, round or slippery synthetic laces, or a single bow; use a reef-knot bow, a double-wrapped bow, flat or cotton laces, or a lace lock (Wikipedia 'Shoelaces'). Lace end frays and won't thread: the aglet has broken (see 'Rope-end finishing'). Holes tear out: holes too close to the edge or material too thin; line them with eyelets or grommets (Wikipedia 'Grommet'), use a double-folded hem and closer spacing (Banners.com), or sew on reinforcement loops (Extremtextil). Lace wears and stretches while lacing a long edge: work in shorter pieces and splice (leatherworker.net). Leather lace twists or loops come out uneven: dampen the lace and keep the tension even (Tandy Leather). Edges won't close in the middle: criss-cross lacing on a closed opening; use straight-bar lacing (Wikipedia). Lace frays at sharp printed hole edges ⚑ [uncertain]: chamfer and smooth them. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **related**: 
  - Eyelets and grommets (for fabric, leather and sheet): the reinforced holes that lacing usually passes through
  - Ratcheting reel closure (dial lacing system): a dial that winds a thin lace or cable to tighten a laced opening with one hand
  - Cord lock (spring toggle): holds a lace or drawstring at any length instead of tying a knot
  - Rope-end finishing (whipping, heat-sealing, back splice, aglet): aglets and melted tips that keep lace ends from fraying and make threading easy
  - Basic knots (overhand knot, half hitch, reef knot): the bow that ties off a lace is a slipped reef knot
  - Sewn seams and hand stitches: a permanent seam with thread, where lacing is a removable join with a lace
  - Binding knots and seizings (constrictor knot, seizing, cable lacing): cable lacing ties wire bundles with lacing cord or tape
  - Zipper (slide fastener) and Hook-and-loop fastener: faster ways to close an opening, with less adjustment
  - Basic strap and garment closures (prong buckle, button, toggle and loop): other simple ways to close clothing and bags
  - Sew-on part (sewing holes, loops and slots on a rigid part): hole sizes and edge distances for cord and thread through printed parts
  - Elastic band and elastic cord springs: shock cord used as a stretchy lace for covers
