## 388 Rope splices (eye splice, short splice, braided-rope splice)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Joining a rope to itself or to another rope by tucking its strands back in, or burying its end inside its hollow core, to make a loop or join that keeps nearly all the rope's strength and is slimmer than a knot

*Also covers:* Also covers long, back and ring splices, the number of tucks in slippery rope, and the soft shackle (a rope loop closed by a knot). Safety: home-made splices aren't rated; never for climbing or lifting people

### What it is

- **plain_name**: Weaving a rope's own strands back into itself, or sliding its end up inside its hollow middle, to make a permanent loop in the end, join two ropes end to end, or stop the end fraying, all without tying a knot
- **term**: Rope splice (splicing): tuck splices in laid (three-strand) rope, i.e. the eye splice, short splice, long splice, back splice and chain or ring splice; and bury splices in braided rope, i.e. the long-bury (finger-trap) splice and the locked Brummel splice
- **aliases**: 
  - eye splice, sailmaker's eye splice: a permanent loop in a rope end (ABOK #2725 per Animated Knots; Wikipedia's infobox gives ABOK #17, #2745, #2754) (Wikipedia 'Eye splice'; Animated Knots 'Eye Splice')
  - short splice: joins two rope ends, about twice as thick as the rope (ABOK #2634) (Animated Knots 'Short Splice'; Wikipedia 'Rope splicing')
  - long splice: joins two rope ends with almost no bulge, so the rope still runs through pulleys (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'; Samson 'Long Splice')
  - back splice, end splice, crown-and-back splice: tucks the strands back into the end to stop fraying (ABOK #2813) (Animated Knots 'Back Splice'; Wikipedia 'Rope splicing')
  - ring splice: attaches a rope end to a ring or sail clew (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing', citing Merry, The Splicing Handbook, p. 27)
  - chain splice, rope-to-chain splice: a small Flemish eye (ABOK #2751) with extra tucks, joining rope to anchor chain (Animated Knots 'Chain Splice'; Samson)
  - Flemish eye; cut splice; horseshoe splice (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing', 'Eye splice')
  - bury splice, long-bury splice, unlocked Brummel, finger-trap splice: the end pushed up inside hollow braided rope (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice')
  - locked Brummel splice, Brummel eye splice; McDonald Brummel (Animated Knots 'Brummel Eye Splice', 'Splicing Knots')
  - tuck-bury splice, end-for-end splice, double-braid (braid-on-braid) eye splice: rope makers' names for braided-rope splices (Samson 'How to splice rope'; Milne and McLaren 2006)
  - soft shackle, rope shackle: a spliced loop of strong fibre rope closed by a stopper knot (Animated Knots 'Soft Shackle'; Wikipedia 'Shackle')
  - whoopie sling: an adjustable spliced sling, e.g. for hammock straps (Animated Knots 'Whoopie Sling')
  - rope grommet: an endless rope ring made by re-laying a strand (Wikipedia 'Grommet')
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware, clamping, magnetic & friction connections
- **one_liner**: A rope splice opens up the end of a rope and weaves its strands over and under the strands of the main rope, or, in hollow braided rope, pulls the end up inside the rope, to make a permanent loop, a join between two ropes or a neat end that keeps most or all of the rope's strength, where a knot typically loses 20-40% (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing').
- **complexity**: Single piece: only the rope itself. Often finished or fitted with a few small extras: tape or whipping twine to hold the strands, stitching twine to lock a braided splice, and a thimble (a grooved metal or plastic liner) inside the eye. Tools: a fid or marlinspike, a knife and a lighter or hot knife.
- **how_it_works**: In three-strand (laid) rope you untwist the end, bend it back to form the loop or lay it against the other rope, and tuck each loose strand over one strand and under the next of the main rope (standing part), against the twist, several times round; friction between the interwoven strands holds them and hands the load over gradually from the tail to the standing part (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'; Animated Knots 'Eye Splice'; Samson 3-Strand Eye Splice). In hollow single-braid rope (for example HMPE such as Dyneema, brand) the tapered end is pushed up the hollow centre; when the rope is pulled its braid gets longer and narrower like a finger trap and grips the buried tail along its whole length, and a Brummel lock (passing the rope through itself) or a line of stitches stops the tail sliding out when the rope is slack (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice', 'Brummel Eye Splice'; Wikipedia 'Eye splice'). Because the load is passed on along the length of the splice instead of through sharp bends as in a knot, a well-made splice breaks at the end of the splice at close to the rope's own strength, provided there are enough tucks or a long enough bury (Milne and McLaren 2006; Animated Knots 'Splicing Knots').

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: makes a permanent loop (eye) in a rope end, attaches a rope to a ring, thimble, shackle or chain, or joins two ropes end to end (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'; Animated Knots 'Splicing Knots')
  - tighten and hold tension: only the adjustable spliced versions, such as the whoopie sling used 'to tension a hammock' (Animated Knots 'Whoopie Sling')
  - lock, and release by hand: only the soft shackle, a spliced rope loop closed by a knot that is opened and closed by hand like a metal shackle (Animated Knots 'Soft Shackle'; Wikipedia 'Shackle')
- **motion**: none: the splice is a fixed joint; the rope on either side stays flexible. A whoopie sling's adjustable section slides along the rope until it is loaded (Animated Knots 'Whoopie Sling').
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 at the splice itself (the rope either side bends freely); 1 for the sliding part of an adjustable whoopie sling while it is unloaded.
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - self-locking (holds under load, won't back-drive): a bury splice in hollow braid grips harder the harder it is pulled, because the outer braid narrows onto the buried tail, finger-trap style; 'the strain is progressively transferred from the standing end to the two lines of the eye' (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice'; see Woven self-tightening grip)
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds): the whoopie sling's adjustable loop is pulled to length and holds once loaded (Animated Knots 'Whoopie Sling')
  - quick-release: only the soft shackle, whose knot is pushed back out through its loop when it is unloaded (Animated Knots 'Soft Shackle'; Wikipedia 'Shackle')
  - none: plain eye, short, long and back splices simply hold
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): the interwoven strands squeeze each other, and rope tension tightens the tucks; too few tucks and the splice 'can simply unravel' under load (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'; Milne and McLaren 2006)
  - jamming (grains or a braid lock up under load): in hollow braid the outer braid narrows and grips the buried tail along its length (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice')
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): a Brummel lock, the tucked strands of a chain splice round a chain link, and the soft shackle's knot trapped in its loop hold by shape when the rope is slack (Animated Knots 'Brummel Eye Splice', 'Chain Splice', 'Soft Shackle')
- **input_output**: not applicable
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: semi-permanent: a splice 'is intended to be permanent' and 'Undoing a splice and re-making it takes much more time than doing the same with most knots' (Animated Knots 'Splicing Knots'); if removed it 'leaves a distortion of the rope' (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'). A worn chain splice is cut off and remade a few inches further up the rope (Animated Knots 'Chain Splice'). Exceptions: the soft shackle opens by hand (tool-free quick release) and the whoopie sling adjusts by hand.
- **reuse_cycles**: once: a splice is made to stay, and is cut off and remade when worn rather than undone (Animated Knots 'Chain Splice', 'Splicing Knots'). The finished soft shackle or whoopie sling is opened, closed or adjusted many times in normal use.
- **tools_needed**: Tape and a sharp knife; a lighter, hot knife or whipping twine to stop the strand ends fraying; a fid (a smooth tapered spike of wood, bone, aluminium or plastic: Swedish fid, tubular push fid, uni-fid or pulling fid) or a steel marlinspike to open the strands (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing', 'Fid'). In small, soft three-strand rope, masking tape wrapped on each strand end, a pencil or a ball-point pen can do instead (Animated Knots 'Eye Splice'). Braided-rope splices also use a marker, measuring tape, a wire pusher or splicing needle, and stitching twine and needle for the lock stitch (Wikipedia 'Eye splice'; Animated Knots 'Whoopie Sling'; Samson 12-Strand Class II Eye Splice); 'Often a hammer and winch are used as well for tougher splices' (Wikipedia 'Eye splice').
- **seal**: none
- **load**: Medium to heavy, in a straight pull only; weak where the eye bends over a sharp edge or a thin pin. Strength figures differ, so side by side: 'a knot typically reduces the strength by 20-40%' while 'a splice is capable of attaining a rope's full strength' (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'); one study found 'Splices average 25-40% of rope strength decay', while 'Literature and reference sources typically attribute only a 5% strength decay' (Wikipedia 'Eye splice', citing Diamond 2007); Samson says splicing 'in most cases ... maintains 100% of the specified rope strength', and its 12-strand Class II eye splice keeps '90% to 100% of average new rope strength' (Samson); Animated Knots expects '90 - 100%' for a long-bury splice in hollow braid. In tests on polyester rope, three-strand eye splices with 3, 4 or 8 tucks reached about full rope strength, 2-tuck splices slipped at 73%, and 4-tuck splices in 6 mm and 12 mm rope reached only 63% and 83%; braid-on-braid splices with a long enough overlap reached about 100%, but the shortest overlap slipped at 15-24% (Milne and McLaren, Sports Engineering 2006). For comparison, knots in the same tests kept 62-66% (bowline) and 76-84% (figure-eight loop). A soft shackle, with several rope parts sharing the load, typically breaks at about 175% of the line's strength (Animated Knots, citing Evans Starzinger; see Shackle and quick link for other tests). Never for holding people.
- **adjustable**: no for ordinary splices: the eye size and rope length are set when the splice is made. Yes for a whoopie sling or Grog's sliding splice, whose loop slides along the rope to change length and then holds (Animated Knots 'Whoopie Sling', 'Splicing Knots').
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable. Note for matched pairs: a long bury shortens and thickens the rope by an amount there is 'no reliable formula for predicting', so make and load a test splice, measure it, then make the second one the same (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice').
- **access_needed**: not applicable: it is made on a free end of the rope, usually before the rope is fitted; the locked Brummel is 'much easier to tie when both ends are available' (Animated Knots 'Brummel Eye Splice').
- **materials**: Three-strand (laid) rope in natural fibre (manila, hemp, cotton) or synthetic fibre (nylon, polyester, polypropylene and other olefins): tuck splices such as the eye, short, long, back and chain splices (Wikipedia 'Eye splice'; Samson Class I instructions). Eight-strand plaited rope: tuck splices with the strands handled in pairs (Wikipedia 'Eye splice'). Hollow single braid, usually 12-strand, in HMPE (Dyneema, Spectra, Amsteel, brands), LCP (Vectran, brand) or aramid (Kevlar, Technora, brands): bury and Brummel splices, soft shackles and whoopie slings (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice', 'Whoopie Sling'; Samson Class II). Double braid (a braided cover over a braided core): rope makers' splices measured in fid lengths, and for high-performance cores only the core carries the load (Wikipedia 'Eye splice'; Samson). Modern high-modulus fibres 'are often slippery and cannot be secured with customarily trustworthy knots and splices', which is why they need long buries (Animated Knots 'Splicing Knots'). Sheathed (kernmantle) climbing rope and paracord-style cord are not normally spliced at home ⚑ [uncertain]. Thimbles are galvanised or stainless steel or nylon (Printables 934812). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **maker_difficulty**: moderate: a three-strand eye splice or a bury splice in hollow braid needs only a fid, patience and practice, but the locked Brummel 'tends to be confusing and repetition is required' (Animated Knots), and double-braid splices need careful measuring with the rope maker's instructions (Samson). In tests, three-strand splices made by inexperienced people were as strong as each other; the authors judged a professional's splice unlikely to be stronger 'so long as the splice geometry was correctly formed' (Milne and McLaren 2006).

### Making it

- **processes**: off-the-shelf part: rope, spliced by hand, 3D print (FDM): splicing fids, pull fids, thimbles and pulleys or blocks used with spliced rope (see make_it_yourself)
- **print_in_place**: no: the splice is made in rope; only the splicing tools and the thimbles or blocks it fits are printed.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Splices aren't printed, but printing helps around them. 1) Splicing fids: one design (3, 4, 6 and 8 mm sizes, 'Ideal for Dyneema') prints in PLA, PETG or ABS but not glass- or carbon-filled filament, which makes it 'too rough'; it suggests 0.05 mm layers, 50% infill, 3 perimeters and slow speed, printing upright (no supports, 'but not as strong') or lying flat with supports under the tip, sanding with 100-600 grit, and keeping 'the tip blunt to prevent it from catching on rope fibers' (Printables 999397). 2) A pull fid can be a printed handle with a loop of stainless steel wire wrapped tightly round it and taped (Printables 640920). 3) Printed thimbles: a parametric design (6-12 mm rope, STEP file to resize) recommends 'PETG or another though [tough] filament like ASA or TPU', notes that infill matters less than perimeters, and warns 'I haven't tested these thimbles for load bearing uses!' (Printables 934812); use metal thimbles for anything important ⚑ [uncertain]. 4) Printed pulleys and blocks are often hung from a soft shackle: one design for up to 6 mm rope uses a 3 mm Dyneema soft shackle and was tested to 100 kgf for 3 days in the sun, with a destructive test suggesting about 150 kg breaking force (Printables 552267). 5) Where a spliced eye bears on a printed part, give it a large, smooth, rounded surface or a thimble so it can't chafe or cut the rope, and size the eye so its two legs meet at no more than 60 degrees over the pin (the OSHA rule for lifting slings) ⚑ [uncertain for printed parts]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Rope sold by construction (three-strand laid, eight-strand plaited, 12-strand single braid, double braid) and fibre; splicing fids (Swedish fid, tubular fid, uni-fid, pulling fid), marlinspike, wire splicing needles and pushers, whipping and stitching twine; thimbles (round and teardrop, steel or nylon); ready-made soft shackles, whoopie slings and spliced eyes from riggers and rope makers; free splicing instructions from rope makers such as Samson, New England Ropes and Yale Cordage (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing', 'Fid', 'Eye splice'; Samson 'How to splice rope'; Animated Knots 'Whoopie Sling').
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Tucks in a three-strand eye splice, side by side: at least 3 in natural fibre and 5 in synthetics (Toss, The Complete Rigger's Apprentice p. 83, via Wikipedia 'Eye splice'); in slippery synthetics 'a minimum of five complete tucks is required', and seven for 'mooring, towlines, and other long term or critical applications' (Animated Knots 'Eye Splice', after ABOK #2725); Samson's eye splice for nylon, polyester and olefin rope is one round of tucks then '4 more complete tucks' (Samson 3-Strand Class I Eye Splice); the legal minimum for US lifting slings is 3 full tucks in manila and 4 in synthetic rope (OSHA 29 CFR 1910.184(h)(3)); in tests on polyester rope, 2-tuck splices slipped at 73% of the rope's breaking load while 3, 4 and 8 tucks all reached full strength (Milne and McLaren 2006).
  - Tucks in a short splice: natural fibres 'hold well with three tucks each side', synthetics need at least five (Animated Knots 'Short Splice', ABOK #2634); OSHA's minimum for slings is 6 full tucks (3 each side) in manila and 8 (4 each side) in synthetic rope.
  - How much to unlay: about 3 rope diameters per tuck, e.g. 7.5 in for five tucks in 1/2 in rope and 10.5 in for seven (Animated Knots 'Eye Splice'; Wikipedia 'Eye splice'); Samson counts back 16 picks for its eye splice; a seven-tuck chain splice needs at least 21 diameters (Animated Knots 'Chain Splice').
  - Tails: on slings, don't trim strand tails flush; leave at least 6 rope diameters beyond the last full tuck (rope under 1 in) or taper them in with at least 2 extra tucks (OSHA 1910.184(h)(3)(iii)). If tails are too long it is 'far less trouble to make another tuck than to cut them', and melted ends add a little security; true tapering of single strands in slippery modern rope 'should probably never be attempted by amateur' splicers: instead cut out one strand, tuck once more with two, then once more with one (Animated Knots 'Eye Splice').
  - Tuck against the lay (twist) of the rope and keep each strand's own twist; 'rope strength can be lost if the strands are twisted incorrectly' (Samson 3-Strand Class I Eye Splice; Animated Knots 'Eye Splice').
  - Bury length in hollow braid, side by side: 72 rope diameters for slippery HMPE (Dyneema, Spectra) and about half that for polyester (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice', citing Brion Toss and Starzinger); at least 60 diameters, with DSM advising 60 times for coated and 100 times for uncoated Dyneema, e.g. 36 cm in 6 mm coated rope (Wikipedia 'Eye splice'); for a whoopie sling's first bury about 40 diameters in high-modulus fibre or 25 in polyester, nylon or polyolefin (Animated Knots 'Whoopie Sling', after Atlantic Braids).
  - Braid splices: taper the tail, because a square end 'creates a weak point'; stitch it (a zigzag right through rope and tail, then back at 90 degrees) or lock it, because otherwise 'movement, shaking, and inquisitive fingers' can pull it out; whip the throat of the eye for the greatest security (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice'). Samson's lock stitch: at least 3 complete stitches on each side, each crossing 2 picks, starting no more than 1/4 fid length below the splice entry (Samson 12-Strand Class II Eye Splice).
  - Fid lengths: rope makers' instructions measure in fid lengths, typically 21-22 rope diameters (a short fid is 1/3 and a long fid 2/3 of that) (Wikipedia 'Fid').
  - Short or long splice: the short splice is about twice the rope's thickness and the stronger of the two; the long splice is only slightly thicker and still passes through pulleys but 'sacrifices some of the strength' (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'). Samson's long splice unlays at least 36 picks, swaps strands over at least 25 picks and tucks each strand 4 times (Samson 3-Strand Class I Long Splice); Animated Knots calls it hard to control in slippery nylon.
  - Back splice: about three tucks are enough because it carries no load; it makes the end about twice as thick and usually stops the rope passing through blocks, so a whipping is usually better (Animated Knots 'Back Splice'; Wikipedia 'Rope splicing').
  - Lifting slings (US rules): at least 10 rope diameters of clear rope between eye splices, eyes big enough that the included angle at the splice is no more than 60 degrees over the load or support, 'Knots shall not be used in lieu of splices', and repaired or reconditioned fibre rope slings are prohibited (OSHA 1910.184(h)).
  - Soft shackle: the stopper (diamond) knot must be pulled extremely tight, until it 'should feel like a wooden ball', or it may slip; trim the tails to about 1/4 in only if it is really tight, otherwise leave at least 1 in; Animated Knots has a calculator for rope length and mark positions from the rope diameter and loop size (Animated Knots 'Soft Shackle', after Allen Edwards).
  - Size the rope with a design factor: breaking strength 5 to 12 times the working load, 12:1 for critical loads (Cordage Institute CI 1401-15, quoted by Animated Knots 'Safety').

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A loop spliced into the end of a boat's mooring or dock line, often round a metal or plastic thimble (Wikipedia 'Eye splice'; Samson)
  - Hammock straps with adjustable whoopie slings (Animated Knots 'Whoopie Sling')
  - A rope spliced straight onto a snap shackle or an anchor chain (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'; Animated Knots 'Chain Splice')
  - Church bell ropes, which are traditionally spliced (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing', citing Beech, Splicing Bell Ropes Illustrated)
- **when_to_use**: Use a splice when a loop or join must stay put for a long time, keep nearly all of the rope's strength, and be slim and neat: a permanent eye on a mooring line, tow rope or anchor rode (with a thimble if it fixes to a shackle or chain), a rope spliced to a chain or ring, joining two ropes (short splice) or a join that must run through a pulley (long splice) (Wikipedia 'Rope splicing'; Animated Knots 'Short Splice', 'Chain Splice'; Samson). In slippery modern rope such as Dyneema, where ordinary knots slip and kink the fibres down to 'a mere 30%' of strength, a bury or Brummel splice is the right termination (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice', 'Splicing Knots'). Makers use them to hang printed pulleys and blocks from soft shackles, as lightweight soft shackles instead of metal ones, and as adjustable whoopie slings (Animated Knots 'Soft Shackle', 'Whoopie Sling'; Printables 552267). A splice is permanent and, unlike a bowline, has no 'tendency to shake undone when not loaded' (Wikipedia 'Eye splice').
- **when_not_to_use**: Never use a home-made splice for climbing, arborist work, rescue, lifting people, a swing or hammock that holds a person, or a load over people; buy rated, tested slings and terminations instead (Animated Knots 'Safety'; OSHA 1910.184). Don't splice when you need to undo and re-tie often: use a knot such as a bowline or figure-eight loop (Fixed loop knots) or a bend (Bends for joining two cords) (Animated Knots 'Splicing Knots'). A short splice is 'useless for any running rigging because the splice will be too fat to pass through any blocks' (Animated Knots 'Short Splice'). A back splice is bulky; use whipping or heat-sealing to stop fraying (Rope-end finishing) (Animated Knots 'Back Splice'). For steel wire rope, use a thimble with clips or a crimped sleeve (Wire-rope terminations). For a quick clamp-on grip on a cable, use a woven mesh grip (Woven self-tightening grip).
- **environment_limits**: Water: nylon 'absorbs water and is 10-15% weaker when wet' (Wikipedia 'Rope'). Sunlight and heat: polypropylene has 'limited resistance to ultraviolet light, friction, and heat', and some synthetics are damaged by UV (Wikipedia 'Rope'). HMPE (Dyneema) soft shackles are weakened by heat, 'also heat caused by friction', and can be 'cut by sharp edges, burned, or deteriorate in some environments' (Wikipedia 'Shackle'). Temperature: US rules allow fibre rope slings from -20 to +180 °F (about -29 to +82 °C) without lowering their working load (OSHA 1910.184(h)(2)). Movement and vibration while slack can draw an unlocked buried tail out of a braided splice (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice'). Chafe: chain splices see heavy load and chafing and should be inspected, though chain on a little-used anchor may rust before the splice wears (Animated Knots 'Chain Splice'). Natural-fibre rope rots more readily than synthetic rope (Wikipedia 'Rope').
- **safety_notes**: Home-made splices aren't rated or tested: never use one for climbing, arborist work, lifting people or anything that would hurt someone if it let go. 'Wrongly handled, gripped, or tied, rope can kill, maim, or burn'; for live or critical loads rope makers' guidance is a design factor of 12:1 (Animated Knots 'Safety', quoting Cordage Institute CI 1401-15). In US workplaces, spliced fibre rope lifting slings must meet minimum tuck and tail rules plus the maker's recommendations, and repaired slings are banned (OSHA 1910.184(h)). Rope under load stores energy and snaps back if it breaks: never stand in a loop or bight that may suddenly tighten (Animated Knots 'Safety'); a soft shackle is 'less likely to cause injury if it strikes a crew member' than a metal one (Animated Knots 'Soft Shackle'). Burns from lighters and hot knives when sealing strand ends; hard, heat-sealed ends can be sharp enough to slice a hand (Budworth, quoted in Wikipedia 'Rope'). Sharp fids and marlinspikes can stab the hand holding the rope ⚑ [uncertain]. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **common_failures**: Slipping out under load because there are too few tucks (2-tuck splices slipped at 73% of breaking load in tests) or too short a bury or overlap (the shortest braid-on-braid splices slipped at 15-24%, at very unpredictable loads) (Milne and McLaren 2006): use the tuck counts and bury lengths in rules_of_thumb, more for slippery rope. The buried tail working out of a braided splice while slack, from movement or fiddling: taper, stitch or lock it and whip the throat (Animated Knots 'Long Bury Splice'). A weak point at a square-cut tail: taper it (Animated Knots). Tapered strand ends in slippery rope getting 'dislodged' and making the splice untidy: drop whole strands instead (Animated Knots 'Eye Splice'). Strength lost because strands were twisted wrongly or tucked with the lay instead of against it (Samson). Chafe or cutting at the eye where it sits on a chain, shackle or sharp edge: fit a thimble (Wikipedia 'Eye splice'; Samson). A soft shackle's knot slipping because it wasn't pulled tight enough (Animated Knots 'Soft Shackle', after Allen Edwards). Heat from friction weakening HMPE (Wikipedia 'Shackle').
- **related**: 
  - Fixed loop knots (bowline, figure-eight loop, alpine butterfly): quicker loops that untie, but weaker (62-84% in the same tests) and the bowline can shake loose when slack
  - Bends for joining two cords (sheet bend, double fisherman's): the knotted, removable way to join two ropes that a short splice does permanently
  - Rope-end finishing (whipping, heat-sealing, back splice): the back splice is one of several ways to stop fraying
  - Woven self-tightening grip (finger-trap grip): explains why a buried splice grips, the same braid-narrowing principle
  - Shackle and quick link: the metal links a spliced eye usually connects to; the soft shackle is its rope version, with more test figures
  - Wire-rope terminations (wire-rope clip, thimble, crimped sleeve): the equivalent loops for steel wire rope, including thimbles
  - Binding knots and seizings: a seizing binds two parts of a rope together without interweaving strands
  - Tensioning and adjustable hitches (trucker's hitch, taut-line hitch): knotted ways to tension a line instead of a whoopie sling
  - Load-bearing stitch patterns for straps and webbing: the sewn equivalent for flat webbing
  - Stopper knots and cord-end anchors: other ways to fix a cord end to a part

  - [Brummel Eye Splice (Animated Knots by Grog)](https://www.animatedknots.com/brummel-eye-splice-knot): Step-by-step animation of a locked Brummel eye splice in hollow-braid rope *(licence: link only (© Grog LLC))*
  - [Soft Shackle (Animated Knots by Grog)](https://www.animatedknots.com/soft-shackle): Animation of making a soft shackle in hollow-braid rope, with a length calculator and a photo of one joining jib sheets *(licence: link only (© Grog LLC))*

## 392 Binding knots and seizings (constrictor knot, seizing, cable lacing)

*knots, lashings & sewn joints.* Knots and wrappings that squeeze tightly round a bundle, a bag neck, a hose, or two ropes side by side to hold them together, like a hose clamp made of string

*Also covers:* Also covers the double constrictor, holding parts for gluing, and cable lacing of wire looms. Safety: seizings aren't for heavy or critical loads.

### What it is

- **plain_name**: Knots and wrappings of string pulled so tight round a bundle, the neck of a bag, a hose, or two ropes lying side by side that they squeeze everything together and won't slip, like a hose clip made of string
- **term**: Binding knots and seizings: the constrictor knot (with the double constrictor), seizings, and cable lacing of wire bundles
- **aliases**: 
  - constrictor knot (ABOK #1249; Animated Knots cites ABOK #1188, p. 216), historically 'gunner's knot' (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'; Animated Knots)
  - double constrictor knot (ABOK #1252), slipped constrictor knot (ABOK #1250) and cross constrictor knot (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot')
  - strangle knot (ABOK #1239): a close relative with the ends coming out at the edges; each half of a double fisherman's knot (Wikipedia 'Strangle knot')
  - miller's knot, sack knot, bag knot (ABOK #1241-#1244): easier binding knots for the neck of a sack (Wikipedia 'Miller's knot'; Animated Knots)
  - boa knot: a modern binding knot (Peter Collingwood, 1996) that holds even when the bundle is cut off close to it (Wikipedia 'Boa knot')
  - transom knot (ABOK #1182, #1255): a light binding for two sticks crossing at a right angle, e.g. kite spars (Wikipedia 'Transom knot')
  - seizing, round seizing, throat seizing, flat seizing, racking seizing (racked turns) (Wikipedia 'Seizing'; ropeworks.biz; Encyclopaedia Britannica 1910 via ClipArt ETC)
  - cable lacing, harness lacing, lacing cord, lacing tape, running lockstitch, 'cableman's knot', marline hitching (ABOK #3115) (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing'; Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962)
  - spot tie, string tie: a single tie of lacing tape, a clove hitch topped by a square (reef) knot (NASA-STD-8739.4A; AeroElectric Connection)
  - box stitch, Chicago stitch and Kansas City stitch: telephone-exchange styles for lacing cables to a rack (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing')
- **family**: knots, lashings & sewn joints
- **other_families**: electrical connections, clamping, magnetic & friction connections
- **one_liner**: A group of knots and wrappings that squeeze round something to hold it together: the constrictor knot grips a bundle, bag neck or hose so hard it may have to be cut off, a seizing binds two ropes side by side with many turns of small twine, and cable lacing ties wire bundles into a neat loom with a row of locked stitches.
- **complexity**: Single piece: a length of twine, small cord or flat lacing tape, plus the bundle, rope or hose it is tied round. A seizing is many turns of one piece of twine; cable lacing is one long piece of lacing tape (or many short spot ties).
- **how_it_works**: A constrictor knot is a turn round the object with an overhand knot trapped under a riding turn (a turn that crosses over it); pulling the ends tightens the overhand, and the riding turn presses down on it so it can't work loose (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'). It needs a curved surface for the riding turn to grip the knot underneath, so it 'fails when tied against a flat surface' (Animated Knots). A seizing uses string or small cord to 'produce friction and leverage to immobilize larger ropes': a layer of tight wrapping turns round both ropes, a layer of riding turns in the grooves between them, then frapping turns between the ropes that cinch the wraps together (Wikipedia 'Seizing'; ropeworks.biz). Cable lacing runs one cord along a wire bundle, wrapping it at short intervals, with each wrap locked by a lockstitch so it can't slacken; stand-alone spot ties do the same job one at a time (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing'; NASA-STD-8739.4A §9.2).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - join and hold: binds a bundle of sticks, wires or rope strands, closes a bag neck, holds a hose on a pipe or holds parts together while glue sets
  - tighten and hold tension: it is pulled up hard round the object and keeps squeezing
- **motion**: none: a good binding holds everything still; slipping along the object counts as failure.
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 at the binding: the bound parts can't slide or turn against each other, though the bundle as a whole can still bend.
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - self-locking (holds under load, won't back-drive): in a constrictor the riding turn clamps the overhand knot, so it won't loosen by itself and is 'difficult or impossible to untie once tightened' (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'); each lockstitch in cable lacing locks its own turn (Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962: the 'cableman's knot ... is self locking')
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds): the knot or wrap is pulled up tight and holds that squeeze
  - quick-release only in the slipped form: a slipped constrictor or miller's knot pulls undone with its loop, though a slipped constrictor 'can still be very difficult to release' when worked tight (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'; Animated Knots 'Miller's Knot')
- **holding_principle**: friction or clamping (force-fit): turns of cord squeeze the bundle, and friction between the turns and the object holds it (Wikipedia 'Seizing'; 'Constrictor knot')
- **input_output**: not applicable
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: semi-permanent (can be undone but may damage parts): a tight constrictor usually has to be cut, by cutting the riding turn, and 'the underlying wraps will protect the bound object from being damaged by the knife' if you don't cut too deep (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'; Animated Knots). Seizings 'semi-permanently bind' and are cut away when no longer needed, leaving the rope undamaged (Wikipedia 'Seizing'; ropeworks.biz). Spot ties, plastic straps and lacing used only to hold wires temporarily are removed before a braid is applied (NASA-STD-8739.4A §9.4.5).
- **reuse_cycles**: once: the cord is normally cut off; slipped forms and loosely tied miller's knots can be untied and retied a few times.
- **tools_needed**: None for the knot itself. A sharp knife to cut it off; two rods or spikes as handles (tied on with marlinespike hitches) to pull it really tight 'without injuring the hands' (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'); a pick or marlinspike to loosen a jammed one. For telephone-style lacing stitches, a cable sewing needle (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing'); for long harnesses, a harness forming board with pegs or springs to hold the wires (Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962).
- **seal**: none: it squeezes but doesn't seal; as a temporary hose clamp it can only slow a leak, so fit a proper hose clamp.
- **load**: Electrical: not a conductor (lacing only bundles and supports wires and carries no current). Mechanical load: light. A binding is strong at squeezing round the object, which is why a constrictor 'has been used as a temporary hose clamp' and can hold parts for gluing (Animated Knots), but seizings 'are not recommended for heavy loads for critical use as strain reduces the diameter of the main rope and can permit slippage even with proper construction' (Wikipedia 'Seizing'). The constrictor is 'extremely secure when tied tightly around convex objects' but fails on flat surfaces (Wikipedia; Animated Knots). Weak along the object if it is smooth or tapered and the binding can slide off the end (Wikipedia: use a boa knot when the object is cut close). No tested strength figures for binding knots or seizings were found ⚑ [uncertain]. Never use them to hold a person. *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **adjustable**: no: once pulled tight a constrictor or seizing can't be eased or tightened; untie or cut it and tie a new one. Cable lacing can have wires added only by cutting and relacing, though spot ties can be added (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing' shows a Chicago stitch with cables added).
- **play_and_precision**: not applicable
- **access_needed**: end of a tube or shaft: the twisting and folding methods tie the constrictor in the bight and drop it over a free end (Wikipedia; Animated Knots); without a free end you need room to pass the cord right round the object and tie it with the end (Animated Knots 'Rope End Method').
- **materials**: Cord: twine and other small cord, because 'the binding force is concentrated over a smaller area' (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'); seizing twine about 1/8 to 1/12 of the rope's diameter (ropeworks.biz). Lacing: traditionally waxed linen cord; modern flat lacing tapes in nylon, polyester, PTFE (Teflon), fibreglass and Nomex with coatings that help knots hold (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing'); flat ribbon is preferred to round cord 'to reduce the possibility of cutting into wire insulation' (Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962); waxed polyester flat lace has 'excellent longevity' under a car or aircraft engine cowl, where nylon cable ties suffer from ozone, hydrocarbons and embrittlement (AeroElectric Connection). Slippery or waxed twine calls for the double constrictor (Wikipedia). Objects: works best on round or convex things such as rope, hose, tubing, stick bundles, bag necks and wire bundles; poor on flat or square-edged objects unless the knot sits over a corner (Wikipedia; Animated Knots). Soft items can be damaged or disfigured (Wikipedia).
- **maker_difficulty**: easy to moderate: easy for the constrictor and spot ties (one knot, no tools); moderate for neat seizings and long runs of cable lacing, which take practice to keep even and tight.

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part: twine, whipping or seizing twine, waxed lacing tape, tied by hand
  - 3D print (FDM): printed helpers such as lacing-tape spools, wire harness jigs and tool handles, and printed parts with round bosses or grooves for a binding to sit in
- **print_in_place**: no: the knot is tied in cord; only helpers and the parts it is tied round can be printed.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Bindings are tied, not printed, but they are handy on printed parts. 1) Give the binding a round surface: a constrictor 'fails when tied against a flat surface' (Animated Knots), so add a round boss, or on a square part arrange the knot so its overhand sits over a corner with the riding turn on top (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'). 2) A shallow groove round the boss stops the binding sliding off the end ⚑ [uncertain]. 3) To hold printed parts while glue sets, pass the cord round them and tie a constrictor with the end (Animated Knots 'Rope End Method'); put a scrap of card or cloth under it on thin or soft prints, because it 'can damage or disfigure items it is tied around' (Wikipedia); the padding is general advice ⚑ [uncertain]. 4) For wiring looms on printers and robots, lay the wires out on a jig first, as the US Navy did on pegboards and spring boards (Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962); printed harness jigs exist (Printables 1141118). 5) A printed lacing-tape spool holds the tape in your palm while you lace, with a small end that fits a drill chuck for fast winding and a cone end to help tie knots as you go (Printables 973050). 6) Don't lace so tight that the tape digs into soft printed cable clips or wire insulation: tight enough to stop slipping 'but should not cut into the insulation' (Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search catalogues for: 'waxed lacing tape' or 'flat braided lacing tape' (nylon, polyester, Nomex, PTFE or fibreglass; Wikipedia 'Cable lacing'), 'lacing cord' (round cord sizes #4 to #12 by bundle size; Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962), 'whipping twine' and 'seizing twine', 'cable sewing needle' or 'cable lacing needle' (Wikipedia names Jonard (brand) needles), 'fid' and 'marlinspike', and a 'harness board'. Alternatives sold for the same jobs: cable ties, hook-and-loop cable ties, and worm-drive or spring hose clamps.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Constrictor: tie it on a curved (convex) surface; it 'fails when tied against a flat surface' (Animated Knots). On a square object, put the overhand part over a corner with the riding turn on top, or finish with an extra overhand knot where there are gaps under it (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot').
  - Use the double constrictor for very slippery twine, especially waxed; 'Adding more than one extra riding turn does not add to its security' (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot').
  - To remove a jammed constrictor, cut the riding turn on top of the other turns, which protect the rope or object under the blade (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'; Animated Knots).
  - Constrictor whipping: a series of constrictors tied from the end of a rope back towards the standing part makes a temporary whipping that Animated Knots' contributor found 'virtually indestructible' (Animated Knots 'Rope End Method').
  - Seizing (from a hobbyist rope-work site): twine 1/8 to 1/12 of the rope's diameter, about 3 ft of twine per inch of rope diameter; wrap a width equal to the rope's diameter; lay riding turns in the grooves 'not so tight as to separate the wrapping turns'; finish with frapping turns between the ropes and a square knot; use 3 to 4 seizings to hold an eye, with racked turns at the first one where the strain is greatest (ropeworks.biz).
  - Cable lacing spacing, side by side: NASA allows at most 19.1 mm between ties on bundles up to 6.4 mm across, 38.1 mm up to 12.7 mm, 50.8 mm up to 25.4 mm and 76.2 mm on larger bundles, with a tie immediately before and after every breakout (NASA-STD-8739.4A Table 9-1, §9.2.8); the 1962 Navy manual puts lock stitches at 'approximately 1/2-inch intervals' (Bureau of Naval Weapons); AeroElectric suggests running ties 'at 1 to 2 inch intervals' for hobby aircraft wiring (AeroElectric Connection).
  - Spot ties: a clove hitch followed by a square knot, 'or other non-slip knots', with the ends trimmed (NASA-STD-8739.4A §9.2.2, §9.2.5); a surgeon's knot is an acceptable substitute for the square knot (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing', citing NASA). AeroElectric uses a 12 in or longer piece and trims the ends to 1/4-1/2 in.
  - For continuous lacing, cut cord about two and a half times the harness length, start with a wrap of about twelve turns locked by a lock stitch, and always pass the cord over the loop, 'never under', so each stitch locks (Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962). Use lockstitches (marline hitching, based on overhand knots); 'A series of half-hitches, while visually similar, is not recommended' (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing').
  - How tight: lacing 'should be sufficiently tight to minimize slippage but should not cut into the insulation' (Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962); NASA's rule for cable straps is tight enough not to slide, but not so tight 'as to cause noticeable indentation or distortion of the wires' (NASA-STD-8739.4A §9.6.4).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Tying the neck of a sack or bin bag tightly shut (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Miller's knot')
  - A string tie used as an emergency hose clamp, e.g. on a split radiator hose (Animated Knots; AeroElectric Connection)
  - Neatly laced wire bundles in old telephone exchanges, vintage radios and spacecraft (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing')
  - The eye at the end of a sailing boat's stay held by round seizings, and a quick binding on a fraying rope end before it is whipped (Wikipedia 'Seizing'; Animated Knots)
- **when_to_use**: Use a constrictor when you want a fast, very firm binding with nothing but string: closing a bag or sack, tying a bundle of sticks, canes or tubes, holding rope strands together while splicing or before whipping, holding parts together while glue sets, or as a temporary hose clamp or cable tie (Animated Knots; Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'). Use a double constrictor for slippery or waxed twine. Use a seizing to bind two ropes, or two parts of one rope, side by side, for example to hold an eye in a rope's end without a splice; it is less bulky than a knot and doesn't bend or damage the rope's fibres (ropeworks.biz; Wikipedia 'Seizing'). Use cable lacing or spot ties to bundle wires in robots, printers, cars and electronics where you want a slim, flexible loom with no sharp cut ends; it 'does not create obstructions along the length of the cable' the way cable ties do (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing'; AeroElectric Connection).
- **when_not_to_use**: Not for heavy or critical loads: seizings slip as a loaded rope stretches thinner (Wikipedia 'Seizing'); use a splice or rated hardware. Never for holding a person. Not on flat or square-edged surfaces (Animated Knots), and not where you'll need to undo it often: use a miller's knot, which 'is suitable for most applications, and is easier to tie and untie' (Wikipedia 'Miller's knot'), a slipped constrictor, a cord lock, or a reusable hook-and-loop cable tie. Not as a lasting hose clamp on anything under pressure: fit a worm-drive or spring hose clamp. Not to join two ropes end to end (use a bend), to make a loop (use a fixed loop knot), to finish a rope end for good (use whipping or heat-sealing), or to build frames from poles (use pole lashings; the transom knot is only a light-duty one, Wikipedia 'Transom knot'). On soft or delicate items it can crush or mark them (Wikipedia). For flight or mains equipment, follow the wiring standard that applies; this entry gives no mains wiring steps, and mains work must follow the law where you live.
- **environment_limits**: Slippery cord: synthetic and waxed twines slip more, so use the double constrictor (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'). Rope under strain gets thinner, so seizings can slip (Wikipedia 'Seizing'). Over time and heat, wire insulation can creep: AeroElectric has seen PTFE-insulated bundles 'tied so tightly that the insulation cold-flowed and allowed wires to short together'. Engine-bay conditions: nylon cable ties suffer from ozone, hydrocarbons and embrittlement as they lose plasticiser, while polyester flat lace lasts well (AeroElectric Connection). Heat: nylon braid ends can be sealed with a hot knife (NASA-STD-8739.4A §9.3.10), which also means synthetic cords melt near hot parts. Vibration: lockstitched lacing and constrictors don't shake loose the way half hitches can (Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962; Wikipedia).
- **safety_notes**: Seizings and binding knots are not for heavy or critical loads (Wikipedia 'Seizing'), and a home-made knot, seizing or lacing must never hold a person. Pulling a constrictor really tight with bare hands can cut them: use two rods as handles (Wikipedia 'Constrictor knot'). When cutting a jammed binding, cut the riding turn carefully and not too deep, with the blade moving away from your hands (general knife safety) ⚑ [uncertain]. Never tie a constrictor or any binding round a finger, wrist, limb, neck or any living thing: it jams and may be impossible to undo without cutting. Medical note: the constrictor has been studied as a surgical ligature (Wikipedia, citing Taylor and Grogono 2014); that use is out of scope here, and nothing in this entry is guidance for use on or in people. Electrical: lacing pulled too tight can damage insulation and short wires (AeroElectric Connection), and starting stitches 'shall not place stress on wire terminations' (NASA-STD-8739.4A §9.2.4). Trimmed cable-tie ends can be sharp, which string ties avoid (AeroElectric Connection). *(⚑ parts need confirming)*
- **common_failures**: Slipping off or loosening on a flat or tapered surface: tie it on a round part or over a corner (Animated Knots; Wikipedia). Jamming so it can't be untied: expected with a tight constrictor; cut the riding turn (Wikipedia; Animated Knots), or tie the slipped form or a miller's knot if it must come off. Capsizing: some bag knots, such as miller's knot (2) (ABOK #1242), which 'sometimes capsizes' (Animated Knots). Sliding off a short bundle cut close to the knot: use a boa knot (Wikipedia 'Boa knot'). Crushing or marking a soft bag or item: keep the wraps together and don't over-tighten (Wikipedia). A seizing slipping under heavy strain (Wikipedia 'Seizing'), or riding turns pulled so tight they separate the wrapping turns (ropeworks.biz). Lacing that loosens because half hitches were used instead of lockstitches (Wikipedia 'Cable lacing'). Lacing so tight it cuts or cold-flows the insulation (Bureau of Naval Weapons 1962; AeroElectric Connection). NASA's list of lacing defects: improperly laced ties, wrong material, bundle 'damaged or deformed by tie', 'Loose ties', 'Ends not trimmed' and 'Incorrect spacing of ties for bundle size' (NASA-STD-8739.4A).
- **related**: 
  - Hose clamp (worm-drive clamp, ear clamp, spring hose clamp): the hardware version, for any hose that must stay tight under pressure
  - Linear ratchet (cable-tie ratchet): cable ties do the bundling job in seconds but leave a hard lump and sharp ends
  - Hook-and-loop fastener: reusable cable wraps for bundles that change often
  - Basic knots (overhand knot, half hitch, reef knot): the reef knot is also a binding knot, but far less secure than the constrictor
  - Rope-end finishing (whipping, heat-sealing, back splice, aglet): whipping binds a rope end for good, where a constrictor is a quick temporary binding
  - Rope splices (eye splice, short splice, braided-rope splice): a splice keeps nearly all of a rope's strength where a seized eye is only for light loads
  - Pole lashings (square, diagonal, shear and tripod lashings): wrap and frap poles into frames; the transom knot is a light version
  - Hitches to a post, rail, ring or eye (clove hitch): the clove hitch is the base of the spot tie and close cousin of the constrictor
  - Lacing (cord through rows of holes or eyelets): a different lacing, which pulls two edges together through holes
  - Cable gland and strain relief: takes the pull off wires where they enter a box, which lacing must not do
  - Spring clip clamp (clothes-peg clip, spring clamp): a quicker way to hold small parts while glue sets
